mr president, standing here reminds me of the first time i entered this grand chamber. i remember the excitement i felt, knowing that in this room we can deliver the greatest amount of good for the largest number of people. that is why we are all here: to improve the lives of our citizens. yet, since i arrived, what have i seen? i have seen an old boys’ club in charge, a few men from a few countries seeking to dominate every major decision, and a way of working that leaves many meps and the people they represent out in the cold. that is not fair, not open and not representative of the diversity of europe. so i stand here with a clear purpose: to make sure that every voice matters in this parliament and that every mep – every one of you – has equal power. there is an old saying: ‘a strong woman stands up for herself, a stronger woman stands up for everyone else’. so my appeal to every single one of you in this. don’t vote for the candidate who wants to further their own agenda. vote for the candidate who wants to help you deliver your agenda and your citizens’ agenda and who wants to stand up for all 750 of you. vote for the candidate who embodies change and who shows the people of europe that we are here to represent every corner of europe, every viewpoint, every single citizen. vote for the candidate who will listen to you. now i might be deaf, but i am a very good listener. and let us today send out a signal that we stand by our own motto of ‘united in diversity’: that, wherever you come from, whatever background you have or obstacles you have faced, europe is a place where even the greatest of challenges can be overcome with hard work and optimism. since i was elected, i have been honoured to work with meps from across this chamber. any of you who have worked with me will know what i believe in. i believe in uniting people, in equality and fairness, and in respect. those are the values i would bring to this role if you vote for me today. so let us make a bold and decisive step towards change right now. let us restore people’s faith in the european parliament. let us end the old boys’ club and breathe some fresh air into this building. if you think it’s time for change, then i can deliver it: nobody left behind, no more backroom dealing, no more old boys’ club. it is time that every voice matters. (applause)
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, i would like to thank the prime minister for the inspiring speech. i thank you also for always defending the interests of the european parliament, and that will be necessary in the coming months, in particular because of the negotiations on brexit (although i will not talk about brexit as we have a debate this afternoon with president tusk). and i agree with my colleague, mr kamall – which is not always the case – when we say it could be a true turning point. let’s face it, the turning point is already here: a us president, trump, who is openly against the european union and saying that other countries will break away. what other turning point do we need? why are you still here, i ask myself? go to the united states, go to the inauguration of mr trump instead of being here, but a us president openly saying that other countries are going to break away from the european union? i have never seen it, never heard it before, and i think that it is a wake-up call for us to reform the european union as fast as possible. if we don’t understand now that we have to stick together, that we need unity, that we need reform, that we need a more integrated european union – also for our geopolitical interests – then when are we going to understand it? it is the moment, and that is your enormous historical responsibility. you have an important summit in valletta in february, where you are going to talk with the other leaders of the european union about the future. i think it is absolutely necessary, and we in the house – the coalition between the ppe and alde – have prepared five concrete points, which in our opinion are necessary for the reform of the european union. and we also offer a very good method for doing this. why not use the method we used with the monti report? the monti report was a report on the own resources of the european union where the three institutions work together – the council, commission and european parliament – that is the proposal we are making. start the process and let it be an open process. it’s not the process of two groups; it has to be a process, gianni, of all pro-europeans in this parliament. it’s not the time to start the elections in 2019 and to say, ‘i’m in opposition to the rest of the european parliament’; it’s time to work together, because it is now that we have to rescue europe – not in 2019.
for the greens/efa group mr president, mr prime minister, welcome to the european parliament and, from the outset, all our encouragement to the maltese government for its first presidency of the union. lithuania proved in 2013 that a first presidency from a small country can be a success. i'm sure you're capable of doing the same. but that's not to say it's a bad time. between russian, turkish, chinese and now american presidents determined to divide europe and an extreme right determined to dynamite it from within, the european union, the world's first experiment in transnational democracy, is threatened. i want to address two issues of your presidency. you have put migration at the top of your priorities, and that is natural. the year 2016 was the deadliest year in the mediterranean, the causes of migration are far from being eliminated and, at this very moment, tens of thousands of refugees who have arrived in the union are still stranded in completely inhuman conditions that threaten their lives. malta, together with italy and greece, is at the forefront of this challenge and it cannot be said that the solidarity of the other member states of the union has been particularly swift or particularly generous. i would therefore have expected malta to use all its energy to mobilize this solidarity. the july 2015 agreement for the relocation of 160,000 refugees remains poorly implemented: only 10,000 refugees have been relocated at this stage. therefore, the implementation of the agreement must be accelerated and the allocation made the norm. it is unacceptable that countries on the front line, such as malta, have to face this alone. but that's not what i read in your program. on the contrary, it is in the spirit of the eu-turkey 'contain and contain' agreement. worse still, you mention the possibility of a similar agreement with libya. so tell me, between two governments, two parliaments, countless warlords, with whom will you make a pact? to get what? the confinement, in the hands of mafia groups, of migrants sold into slavery and prostitution? that would be unworthy of the values of the european union. the second issue i want to raise is that of tax justice, which is not even on your list of priorities. unbridled tax competition between member states benefits only the large taxpayers. we must put an end to this and make tax justice a top priority. otherwise, the explosion of inequality, fuelled by fiscal injustice, will allow the ranks of national populist parties to grow further. after the many scandals and revelations, the european commission, supported by parliament, has multiplied legislative initiatives of which i find no trace in your programme. and we understand that. according to a study carried out by us greens, malta is a tax haven for foreign multinationals and wealthy individuals. between 2012 and 2015, your country withdrew €14 billion in tax revenues from other eu member states. i therefore ask you to reopen negotiations in the council as soon as possible on this subject, in particular on the anti-money laundering directive, on the creation of public registers of beneficial owners of trust and shell companies, on public tax transparency for multinationals and on the start of harmonisation of the corporate tax base. mr prime minister, we know that there are many, many electoral deadlines in europe and that they can be paralysing. i urge you not to resign and to rely on the growing indignation of our fellow citizens to remove the obstacles you will encounter on your way.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, president tusk said in his speech that the world has become a more dangerous place. that is the least we can say, because i remember that the last strategic paper of javier solana of 2003 spoke about the ‘ring of friends’ around us. i think it is more the ‘ring of fire’ around us. putin, the conflicts in the middle east, but merely also the new us president-elect, mr trump. i have to tell you, i am a little bit disappointed at the reaction of the european union against a us leader who is saying that it’s the end of the european union – that there will be other member states leaving the european union. it’s insane! i think that we have to react to this. what would happen in the us if european leaders were saying, ‘we think that california will break away’, or ‘florida will break away’? immediately, i think that the ambassador of the european union would be called to the state department in washington to explain what this means. we have to be very conscious of the fact that this will be a turning point on 20 january 2017. whether you like it or not, it will be a turning point – maybe not completely, but certainly in the way that we have to look at our relationship with the us, and more precisely inside the european union. it is now the time not only to fix the european union, but to do a number of things that were impossible in the past. five years ago, you were saying that the european defence union was not necessary. in reality, it is definitely necessary, and we have to speed it up. it is the same thing with the european border and coast guard. the migration crisis can only be tackled with the european border and coast guard. not with one with eur 250 million – that is what we spent today on the european border and coast guard – in the us, the amount spent on the border and coast guard is usd 32 billion. that’s the comparison we have to make. the same with the governance of the eurozone, where the five presidents’ report – when is it coming? how long do we have to wait for a legislative proposal from the commission to come to the council and to the european parliament? it is time to fix it, like it is time also to fix the cleaning up of our banks. it has taken eight years now, and we can tell that they have not cleaned up our banks in europe; we still face the problems. so, in the brexit negotiations, i think you are completely right, and mr weber is completely right when he said no cherry picking at all. we shall never accept it. you can say, ‘i want to leave the union, i want to leave the european judicial court, i want to leave the customs union’, but you cannot at the same time say ‘oh, but that little piece interests me, and that is something that i like ...’ no way! it is a fair agreement that we are looking for, not a situation where it is better to be outside the european union than inside, as a member state.
member of the commission. – mr president, thank you very much for this discussion. it was definitely a painful discussion because all of us feel very uncomfortable about the situation. nobody should feel proud of this situation, especially because of our moral framework, because all of us, we know well and we want to offer these people dignity in this very unprecedented situation. let me take this opportunity to repeat that the commission is committed to helping the authorities of the countries concerned in their response. this is why commissioner avramopoulos was in lesbos and in greece today to talk with the greek authorities. this is why i am going to serbia on saturday, in order to meet with the serbian authorities and to discuss all things, from unaccompanied minors to the conditions of the refugees, even in belgrade. through our various funding instruments we are ready to respond to additional needs. i can say now that in serbia on saturday i will announce additional funding, especially to support our humanitarian partners on the ground, with the collaboration, of course, of the serbian authorities, because we strongly believe that no one should remain in the cold in these conditions. i completely agree with you that in these very challenging days for the european project we can send our eu citizens the message that we are ready to make a difference in order to provide assistance to these vulnerable people. eu citizens want to see actions, not words, and our success in these very difficult circumstances will be measured by how effectively we can work together. here, i would like to say a big thank you to this house for its support, because today i saw that you are ready to give us support in order to continue on this difficult road. of course, regardless of the emergency situation – and we have to meet this emergency situation – in these challenging times we should not forget the need for longer-term solutions. i call on all other eu member states, through, of course, the maltese presidency, to continue stepping up the number of people they relocate from greece and italy. member states should also deliver on their commitments and deploy their asylum experts for a sufficiently long period of time. i completely agree with my dear friend mr kyrkos that the situation regarding experts on the ground is unacceptable. so far, on average only half of the experts requested are present on the ground. i admit that the eu-turkey agreement has some controversial parts but, at the same time, it has allowed us to put a stop to the countless deaths at sea. but here i would like to emphasise that this refugee crisis is not a national problem. it is not a greek, an italian, a serbian, or a spanish problem; it is a european problem and its solution remains a collective european responsibility. this is why we have to insist on having a common european position and we have to, of course, ask the international community to help us in this condition because it is also a global issue. it is also a global responsibility. i would like to focus on some issues. i totally agree with linda mcavan about the cash voucher scheme. from my experience on the ground, i saw that this is the only way to offer dignity to refugees. this is why it is one of my top four priorities in greece, along with shelter, education of course, and special care for unaccompanied minors. in this way we give, first and foremost, dignity to these people, as i already said. this is the foundation for everyone in order to deal with the situation when they come as refugees. from my experience also, and from my staff’s evaluation, this is much more cost-effective and efficient than any other scheme, and especially the catering services which are currently provided in greece. the cash voucher scheme also supports the local markets, the local economies, and this is why we established in turkey the essn project for one million syrian refugees inside turkey. the greek minister for migration, mr mouzalas, has publicly announced the transition to this scheme, from catering services to cash and voucher, by march. we should not delay this transition. i would like to thank mr moraes for his kind words. as i said, there are funds available. if the space is given, we will support new shelters. if another ship comes to host refugees we could support that too. this is why we are in daily contact with the greek authorities through commissioner avramopoulos and mr verwey from our services. we find daily that the solution is not easy but we give our soul to achieve the best and i think this is what we need. i would also like to focus the issue of the transfer of refugees and migrants from the aegean islands to the mainland, which many of you raised. after considerable talks with the greek authorities, we decided to transfer vulnerable people at least, in order to avoid a deterioration in the situation. i would like to say that on the mainland of greece we can manage to deal with the present situation and many of the refugees who were in inappropriate camps have already been transferred to very good conditions in many hotels around thessaloniki and around athens. if we can manage to establish the cash voucher scheme by march, and if we can manage to transfer more refugees from the aegean islands to the mainland, i think the situation will get better. thank you very much again for your support and for your help and assistance in these very difficult conditions.

in writing. merkel's approach, wir schaffen das, has always been the combination of solidarity with genuine war refugees, and at the same time working on effective control of our external borders. yet 40,000 people who arrived earlier in europe are still stuck this winter in temporary reception camps in greece and the balkans. that is unacceptable. today, solutions are needed, humanitarian responses, warmth and a roof over their heads. but that's temporary. a structural solution has also been on the table for some time. it must be carried out now. all member states must now commit themselves to this refugee crisis. this means, of course, that refugees and migrants themselves must also be prepared to accept the offer of shelter that is made to them and to apply for asylum wherever they are received. there are duties on both sides. only with strict border controls and a clear and easy registration of who is a refugee and who is a migrant can we sustain a social support for a humanitarian refugee policy.
member of the commission. – mr president, let me start by congratulating you on your reappointment as vice-president of this house. almost a year ago, in march 2016, i was announcing to you in this house the launch of a broad discussion on the european social values and rights for the world of work in the 21st century. this was the consultation on establishing the european pillar of social rights, an initiative already announced by president juncker in the state of the union address in september 2015. today, we are here to discuss your own—initiative report, which represents a valuable contribution to the consultation on the european pillar of social rights. i would like to start by congratulating ms rodrigues not only for a very ambitious report, but also for her ability to get the support of all political groups in this house for this initiative. during the past year, we have consulted widely at european and national level, engaging european institutions, social partners, civil society, national governments and parliaments and national—level stakeholders. i would like to thank all of you who organised debates here in the european parliament or in your constituencies on the pillar of social rights. they all contributed to the broader consultation. we have received about 16 500 online replies and nearly 200 position papers. we have been listening to stakeholders’ concerns about their rights, their careers, their life choices and opportunities in the 21st century. we heard a clear call for action. i am convinced more than ever that the debate on social issues should be at the heart of the discussions on what kind of union our citizens want to have. next monday, we will hold a conference at the highest political level, where your rapporteur, ms rodrigues, will be one of the main speakers. president juncker will be there, along with vice-president dombrovskis and many ministers and high—level representatives of european social partners and civil society. we also expect the new president of the european parliament to be there with us. this will be the occasion to reflect on the results of the consultation and to reflect, collectively, where we want to go from here. following that, the commission will adopt its final proposal on the pillar in the spring. i am very happy that your report not only wholeheartedly endorses the pillar, but that it also appeals for greater ambition. this house has repeatedly called for a better balance between our economic policies and social realities. this is not only an economic or social imperative, but is a political necessity. there is a need to regain the trust of citizens in the european project by reaffirming the commitment to european social values and delivering on this commitment. this is the approach the commission is following: strengthening the european social model and promoting upward social convergence. most importantly, your report is a resolute call for action which would make a difference to european citizens. i would like to highlight some of the most important suggestions in your report, which we are considering for the implementation of the pillar. your report rightly stresses the need to address new forms of employment and working conditions and calls for horizontal legislation on decent working conditions. we share your view that we have to consider the new forms of employment relationships. but we cannot turn back the clock. we also need to encourage new ways of working. i believe we should not discourage one form or the other, but should ensure that a core of rights is available to everybody. pivotal to this core of rights, in my view, is that everyone should have a written contract. in this respect, as mentioned in the 2017 commission work programme, we will consult the social partners on revising the written statement directive. further, i take note of your call to ensure adequate social protection for people in all forms of employment – standard and non—standard employment relationships, and also self-employment. in fact, this message also came out from the consultation on the pillar: how to ensure that a larger number of people benefit from the coverage of social protection. this is a key question for the future of work and for the sustainability and relevance of the european social model, and that is why the commission has included in its 2017 work programme an initiative on access to social protection. i welcome your call to address the issue of work-life balance. we have already concluded the consultation of social partners, and i am pleased to inform you that we will launch a package of legislative and non-legislative measures to support working families and businesses in spring this year. our aim is to present a more holistic approach to addressing the challenges of work-life balance – not only for parents, but for other carers as well, and we will propose up-to-date solutions to meet the real needs of today’s families. i do share your concerns about poverty and the fact that we need further efforts to achieve the poverty reduction and social inclusion target of the europe 2020 strategy. in this context, your report rightly points out the importance of an adequate income for maintaining human dignity. i agree that decent living wages are important as a means of avoiding in-work poverty. we take note of your recommendation to set up wage floors in the form of a national minimum wage, as that is an issue, but – as you rightly underline – with due respect for the competence and practices of each member state. as regards reinforcing convergence, the commission, through its coordinating and facilitating role, helps member states find ways of achieving upward convergence whilst maintaining competitiveness and a high level of employment and social protection. you also call upon us to put forward a clear roadmap of concrete measures for the full practical implementation of the european pillar of social rights. here you can count on me. the pillar must be more than words, so we will chart how we move from political commitment to delivery. this is an agenda which needs to fully engage all stakeholders, in particular member states, in line with the subsidiarity principle. we are ready to play our part, but the centre of gravity of social and employment policies is, and will remain, with national and local authorities. the primary responsibility for making social rights a reality lies with national governments and social partners. the european pillar of social rights is an opportunity – we could even say an imperative – to address challenges and make the most of opportunities in the changing world of work and society in the 21st century. it is a crucial step towards a shared vision of a more social europe and a union founded on fairness. we will need the commitment of all stakeholders – european institutions, national governments and parliaments, social partners and civil society – to achieve these objectives on the ground. we count on parliament’s support to make sure that the pillar is not only an aspiration, but will become a reality for all citizens.
the committee on women's rights and gender equality has adopted a report on the situation in the european union. mr president, commissioner, i simply wanted to thank maria joão rodrigues for the work she has done on the substance, of course, but also on all the political discussions she has had on the social rights pillar. commissioner, when we have voted on this report, you will have a valuable tool at your disposal, one that is fundamental to the objective that mr juncker has set for himself, namely the triple a social dimension of the european union. the pillar of social rights concerns all citizens: children with the child guarantee, young people with the youth guarantee and the strengthening of this guarantee, workers, workers and jobseekers, in the context of their workers' rights but also social security. and, most importantly, this pillar of social rights has placed equality between men and women at the centre of its priorities. equality can be achieved through work-life balance by increasing parental leave, maternity or paternity leave, because, as we know, it is because of these situations that women are discriminated against in the labour market today. therefore, as you said, these legislative and non-legislative tools must help to combat inequalities. and the first of these inequalities, which is blatant today in europe, is that between men and women. i therefore count on you, madam commissioner, and on the whole commission, but also on the political commitment of the council, so that this pillar of social rights does not remain a dream or a utopia, but becomes a reality.
for the ppe group few quotes from commission president juncker have been repeated as often as that one sentence from his speech in october 2014. in the midst of a fragile economic recovery, he then expressed the wish for a triple a social status for europe, and in doing so he immediately put social policy on an equal footing with economic policy and gave you, madam commissioner, a huge task. you'll be filling that assignment with glee today. although youth unemployment remains a problem, the figures are falling year by year. but we can't let the reins celebrate. every young person who is unemployed at home is one too many. the semester process will now also include social indicators. and that's a good thing. you made proposals on strengthening skills and you are working very hard on a solution to tackle social dumping, something that our construction and transport sectors in flanders really need. but perhaps even more than the files i just mentioned, it is the social pillar that will shape that famous social triple a status. so expectations are very high. but you also know that the debate on the role of europe in social policy is at the same level. it is by definition a policy in which the principle of subsidiarity must be fully applied. and let us not set up a european system of what can and should be done at member state level, but we must not shirk our responsibility either. if europe can add value, then we must act. i am thinking in particular of creating a level playing field for new forms of work as opposed to existing forms of work, a european initiative which can provide the member states with the necessary backing to create justice in this area. we look forward to your proposal on the social pillar, commissioner, and i hope that this owninitiative report from parliament will be a source of inspiration for the task ahead of you.
i am absolutely in favour of a social europe, but we must dare to ask ourselves how this social europe can best be achieved. but this report is going the wrong way. first of all, it repeatedly violates the principle of subsidiarity. if we are not to alienate citizens from the union any further, social policy must be implemented as closely as possible to the citizens. the eu's added value lies mainly in coordinating measures, benchmarking and the exchange of good practice. and that's how we can achieve upward convergence. secondly, i would like to stress that social prosperity cannot be built on an economic graveyard. a competitive europe is an essential precondition for a social europe. finally, we must not be afraid of flexibility. after all, flexible working is one of the ways of getting people into or back into work. after all, work is still the best form of social protection. in addition, and this is also very important to me, it is up to the member states to provide adequate social protection in addition.
in writing. when we talk about quality job creation, we automatically think of a green and social economy. if we want to strengthen the social pillar in europe, we need to protect social rights: the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's social policy is not distorted. we've known that for a long time, and we've been saying that for a long time. this report is a worthy contribution to going beyond fine words and principles. job creation and the green economy go hand in hand: the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos at work. they want social protection and flexibility. understandable, but not without the necessary social protection, job security and a good balance between work and leisure. the cooperative approach is also proving useful as a sustainable organisational model for smes. co-ownership, co-determination are ingredients for a social and solidarity economy that enables social protection of workers in smes. the report contains many of the above elements. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of workers against the risks of exposure to carcinogens and to the use of carcinogens in the manufacture of foodstuffs. i shall vote in favour with conviction.
in writing. i welcome the european pillar of social rights as an important initiative to achieve greater social unity and to bring our social model into the 21st century. it is an opportunity to set up concrete actions that citizens feel will benefit them. i am thinking of the right to a minimum income, a package of social rights for all workers (employed and self-employed), the right to education, a child guarantee to ensure that fewer children grow up in poverty or social exclusion. this requires not only legislative initiatives but also additional resources. in addition to the concrete initiatives, and at least as important, the social pillar must ensure that social policy is placed on an equal footing with economic and monetary policy. the social pact should be concluded in the same way as the fiscal compact, which obliges member states to balance their budgets. the social pact must not only enshrine the major principles of the social pillar in european legislation but also make it possible for member states to be obliged to take their social policies seriously. poor scores on (youth) unemployment, (child) poverty, etc. are at least as problematic as poor budget figures.
author. madam president, hosea yeimo and ismael alua, the two papuan peace activists who are the subject of our debate this morning, have indeed been released but their fate remains worrying. accused of rebellion, they now face life imprisonment. it is therefore essential that the european external action service maintains the pressure. that is precisely what we are asking for in our resolution. i would have liked our joint text to mention and demand in such a clear, precise and firm way the release of the governor of jakarta, whose fate has been mentioned. basuki tjahaja purnama, a chinese christian, is unjustly accused of blasphemy by radical islamists. madam president, the european union's legitimate good relations with indonesia - and i am not forgetting that this country was one of the first to be hit by islamist attacks - must not prevent us from continuing to be demanding on the issue of respect for human rights.
for the ecr group a look at the history of the central african republic reveals that the country and its people have suffered for decades from ethnic and religious violence, dictatorships and gross violations of human rights. the people deserve better. we therefore welcome the peaceful election of president touadera. but the situation in the car is not yet what it should be. too often we hear of rape, massacres and famine. we therefore fully support the resolution before us. we want, of course, first of all, that all those who have been guilty of violence, of violations of humanitarian law, be held to account. the government really needs to work on that. the european union is also committed to the reform of the judicial system, together with the international community. we also support the talks between the various armed groups under the auspices of the un. we also call on all international partners to honour their financial commitments so that the terrible famine can be tackled in a structural way. half the population of central africa is starving, and this is a disgrace. we can't just leave these people to their fate.

commissioner, let me begin by thanking you for all the proposals that have been put forward. i've done it before, but i'm doing it with a lot of conviction. you have worked hard on this together with your colleagues, but you are doing so in a transparent, open way, together with parliament and with the stakeholders, and that is very much appreciated by our group. we had a debate before on the ban on muslims entering the united states and i found the replies of the group chairmen very similar on one point. we are going to have a european union that is more than ever there, that offers a solution with confidence, said the other vice-president of the commission, mrs mogherini. and we must do the same in this dossier, because we must not forget that on the other side of the ocean there is also a negative wind when it comes to climate and energy, and so we need our own, self-assured policy. we must be resolute in this regard and this group will support and strengthen all the proposals you put forward. you know our ambitions to set higher targets for energy efficiency and renewable energy, targets which are also binding. that's worked in the past. why should that not be the case in the future? and finally, something we often forget, but the commissioner knows that: governance. you need very good and strong european-driven governance and we will also be putting forward strengthened proposals on that together with the other groups.
we had an interesting debate last week in the itre committee on how we should approach the energy union. i would like to emphasize three points here today. the first point is that we must take account of the affordability of the proposals put forward. for example, in my country alone, there is a 4.38 billion euro bill waiting to be paid to adapt our energy system to the new targets by 2030, and that is not even taking into account the cost of efficiency. secondly, it is also important to make it attractive to invest in energy efficiency. to this end, it should be possible to adapt eurostat's accounting rules to spread energy efficiency investments over different financial years, if necessary. the third element is what the commission calls smart financing, which means that financial institutions must also be involved in order to make investing more financially attractive.

on behalf of the alde group. madam president, in the latest eurobarometer survey on food, the presence of pesticide residues in fruit, vegetables and cereals appears as the main concern of european citizens; the figures speak for themselves, with, for example, 95% distrust in greece, 85% in italy and 72% in belgium, to name just a few countries. we must of course respond to this growing concern in europe about the use of chemical pesticides and the risks they pose to health and the environment. it is time, indeed urgent, to offer farmers, amateurs and sunday gardeners, a wider choice. this is, in essence, the message we are sending with our resolution on low-risk pesticides, whether they are of microbial, plant or animal origin. there is no reason, and this has been said by mr poc, in particular, that only six biologically active substances classified as low risk should be authorised in the european union. by comparison, there are currently over 300 in the world - mostly in the united states and japan. we stress in our text several points to unblock this situation, particularly with regard to the legal framework. our request is clear: first, that the commission submit a legislative proposal to us to set up an accelerated procedure for the approval of biopesticides before the end of 2018 and thus make a more varied offer available to farmers who choose sustainable; secondly, and for amateur gardeners this time, but it's also important, make biopesticides the preferred option alongside other non-chemical methods. this possibility - the right to choose which i have mentioned - does not, in our view, mean more pesticides, quite the contrary. i personally regret that to date only denmark and france - madam president, i conclude - have committed themselves to a genuine reduction plan with a clear timetable and targets.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the use of conventional pesticides is not restricted to the use of pesticides. therefore, there is a need for a transition towards greening of pesticide use; hence this debate today: promoting the use and development of low-risk biological pesticides. we have put forward six specific demands with our colleagues and i should like to hear the commissioner's reaction to them in a more specific way. firstly, there is a need to increase the use of pesticides of biological origin. they must be more widely available. secondly, there is a need for more research and development, because they can indeed be an alternative to conventional products. the standing committee on plants, animals, food and feed should actually adopt criteria for low-risk active substances as a matter of urgency. thirdly, the commission should actually speed up the authorization, assessment and registration of such low-risk plant protection products together with the member states. fourthly, member states should include the use of these low-risk biological pesticides in their action plans. that's not happening enough. fifthly, the refit programme which will assess the pesticide regulation must not affect food safety and public health. finally, commissioner, we ask you to come forward with a legislative proposal before 2018 to speed up the authorisation of these products. i hope you can agree with that.
on behalf of the ppe group. – mr president, i would like to wish the rapporteur, ian duncan, a happy birthday and to congratulate him on accomplishing an extremely complicated effort with a combination of intelligence, flair and contagious scottish humour. thank you very much for that, mr duncan. this reform is key in terms of our commitment to the implementation of the paris agreement. it gives incentives to stakeholders from industry and the power sector, and it supports the best performers – those performers that strive for innovative and sustainable production techniques. but, at the same time, the new emissions trading system (ets) and, in particular, the additions made during the vote by the committee on the environment, public health and food safety, give guarantees to those sectors that are confronted with fierce international competition. currently, a lot of attention is focused on the so-called carbon adjustment mechanism and, in particular, the fact that it will be targeted on particular sectors such as lime and cement. you will not be astonished if we say that we are not in favour of this particular proposal for several reasons. one reason is that it would be damaging for those cement and lime producers which export to countries outside the european union. what is worse is that it would be implemented by means of a delegated act. for every true member of this parliament, i think that should be a no-go. therefore we propose to support the proposal adopted by the committee on industry, research and energy, which is moderate and could be acceptable to all. let us not forget that the cement sector continues to fall under the general system, meaning that the allowances will be attributed only to the 10% of best performers. so, as the commissioner has said, the momentum is there to find a broad majority for a robust and balanced ets. let us choose the moderate option. let us not jeopardise this proposal by focusing on one particular sector. (the speaker agreed to take a blue-card question under rule 162(8))
blue-card answer. – as i made clear at the beginning of my speech, this is about implementing the paris agreement – and not just the building sector, the transportation sector and the agricultural sector but also, of course, industry and the power sector have to implement what we agreed in paris. therefore, i must mention again that there is a lot of ambition in this proposal: what the commission has proposed, the linear reduction factor of 2.2%, is much more ambitious than what we have now. and do not forget the market stability reserve (msr). i was a rapporteur on the msr a few months ago, and the level of ambition now contained in this proposal is much higher than what we proposed a few months or a year ago, even if the msr is not yet operational. supporting that ambition is a very important aspect of this.
mr president, to reduce co2 emissions substantially, we need an emissions trading system that is efficient, balanced and innovative. if we are to put the paris agreement into practice, the carbon market must work better and more efficiently. for example, we need to encourage a more robust price for co2 climate-friendly investments and create an important balance in this process. the reform must protect the climate and jobs, and that is essential. we can't let winners turn into losers. when companies leave europe for third countries where climate regulations are less strict, we lose twice: the jobs are gone and we're doing nothing for the climate. the strong focus on innovation is also crucial. a low-carbon europe is not only achieved through a workable system of emission allowances, but also requires innovative solutions. our policy choices must be sustainable, efficient and effective, so that they can last for decades. we want to be at the front of the pack with flanders and europe without having a flat tyre. this reform is a step in the right direction and i support it.
on behalf of the group of the european right mr president, i should like to speak specifically about banks tonight. in 2008-09, the european commission adopted special state aid rules to allow member states to bail out their banks. this european parliament has been asking for six years now for a complete end to this temporary state aid. that is also the logic of the new rules of the banking union, that in the future we will opt for bail-ins instead of bail-outs. because what do we see in italy today? that the rules were first circumvented and now they're even just ignored. and that creates a lot of anger in the population, a lot of anger about these bank bailouts. but what do we see? that in the past, huge profits were made and went to the rich bankers, while the losses had to be borne by the ordinary people, by the taxpayers, and that can no longer be. look, i have no problem with people working hard to earn a good sandwich, but then they have to take the risks and the losses. more responsibility, that's what we need. i would also ask the commissioner to continue to do a good job of this.

rapporteur. – mr president, in a few weeks’ time we will all be going to italy for the 60th anniversary of the treaty of rome, often described as a big success and a big leap forward. in fact it was the outcome, dear colleagues, of nearly 10 years of negotiations and debates between the leaders of most of the western european states, leaders who at that time, as we all know, wanted to build up a united europe after the atrocities of the second world war, and it started, as we know, with the historic speech by winston churchill at the university of zurich in 1946. a year later, in 1947, at the albert hall in london, churchill, the british bulldog, made it very clear what he wanted. he said, and i quote him: ‘i present the idea of a united europe in which our country, britain, will play a decisive part as a member of the european family’. moreover, churchill – and it’s good to remember this – unlike the prime minister at that time, clement attlee, even wanted britain to participate in the european steel and coal community, but he lost the vote in the british parliament by 309 votes to 296, a small difference, i would say. indeed, dear colleagues, it is good to remember that at that time the tories were openly pro-european. and now, more than 60 years later, we think that with these reports the european union needs profound reform again: let’s face it, our union is in crisis. our european union does not have a lot of friends at home, and certainly not abroad; the union does not deliver any more. it is always too little, too late, and that, in my opinion, is the reason why many people and many citizens are angry in europe. they are not against europe. they are against a union that does not deliver the right results, that is not capable, for example, of finding the right answers to the refugee crisis, responding to migration, or breaking down the economic stagnation after the financial crisis of 2008. people are not against europe. they want more europe to face these challenges, but they are critical, it is true, of the way that the european union works today. and that is the reason for these three reports. brexit, trump, putin – i think that there are reasons enough to reform our european union and to do it in a very profound way. that is the reason for these three reports, which you have to see together as one package. we want to do three things. first of all, we want to create a more efficient union, for example by slimming down the commission, ending europe à la carte, and creating a single seat for the european parliament. we also want to make the union more democratic by reforming the european elections, expanding the process centring on lead candidates, and reforming and turning the european council into what we call a council of states, alongside the european parliament, in which we serve as the representatives of the citizens. and furthermore we want a stronger union, better protection for civil liberties in the union, a union including a euro zone with a government, a fiscal capacity, a finance minister, own resources, a convergence court, and conditionality. i think that these reports give a good blueprint of what is necessary for the future and that is not a battle between the european union on the one hand and the nation-states on the other. no, europe needs both. in this report we are also proposing a green card, for example, for the national parliaments, along with a right of initiative for this european parliament and for the european council. once these reports are adopted, on thursday, i hope, what is the way forward? i think that there is one way forward and that is to propose to the commission, mr vice-president, and also to the european council, that we start an interinstitutional reflection exercise, which is something that we can do better together. we have good experience with the monti report on own resources. why not do the same again? and that is not a luxury, dear colleagues: this report is an absolute necessity.
the european union is an artificial construct, an artificial construct. it has not grown naturally from the population or at least very partially. it is a concept of an elite of what are called founding fathers here, who with a lot of propaganda and with some treaties try to make the thing alive. in such an unnatural construction there are of course unnatural effects, and one of the most unnatural effects is the fact that here in this parliament the main tune is the federal music, that one finds here eurofanatism, which is not really normal. normally, parliament should be the driving force behind the popular resistance to this european construction, the resistance which is becoming increasingly visible in the population through referendums, election results and so on. the eu's critical mass should be heard here, and it is heard here less than ever. this morning is a good example of that. here the opposite effect occurs. here is a large majority who follow the federal music, which always follows the same schedule. there are problems, so there must be more european union, and if there are good effects, there must also be more european union. that is the timetable, and it is in full swing here today, including this morning, again. which brings us to those two records. the first report could still be called somewhat logical. i am not saying that the commission should not be involved in this. it is logical, because the treaties are what they are, but it is at least for me, and as an eu critic, a reminder of the reasons why we fought the lisbon treaty so fiercely at the time. if you see what you can do with it and how far you can go with it, then we know now and today and thanks to this report why. and then there is verhofstadt, i have known him for 30 years, he wants to expand further, he wants to further suffocate us socially, economically and militarily in that european union. back when he was young, his arrogance was called a form of enthusiasm and voluntarism. we can only conclude that in the present context what he brings here is a form of eurofanatism which is harmful to european cooperation. we, the deplorables, will reject both reports.
"blue card" question. what is it? mr president, you say that these reports do not take account of the citizen. i would like to remind you that the eurobarometer report makes it very clear that europeans want to invest more in european defence for our security, to make more use of a european approach to asylum and migration. do you agree with me that the things that are in the reports do take account of what the european citizens want? a genuine european defence, a genuine european approach to asylum and migration. so if we are now saying that these reports are not in line with the wishes of the european people, i think we should go through them together and i should point out to you the need for these reports to set out the vision of where europe should go.
what should be the priority of the european union, mr president? that seems obvious to me. we need to make sure that there's more security. we need to control migration and we need to provide jobs, jobs, jobs. we should be spending all our time on it. all our time on security, on migration, on jobs. but what do we see today? that in addition to these points there are also a number of other topics being debated that people are not waiting for. do you really think people are waiting for european taxes? do you really think that people want to give more power to this european commission and weaken the member states? do you really think that the people want to use flemish tax money to pay back debts in southern europe? i want to make a call today, a sincere call. let us focus solely on that essence. let us go for a european union that provides more security, that controls migration and that is about jobs, jobs and jobs, and all the rest should be frozen.
rapporteur. – mr president, i will be very brief. first of all, i think that the commission could react. fiscal capacity for the euro area has been on the table for two years now and then the commission comes and says that they have to examine it. but that is another point. i have heard three fundamental criticisms. the first is that this is too institutional, then that you are creating more – and a bigger – europe and people do not want that, and that it is too visionary. those are the three criticisms that i have heard this morning. firstly, on institutions, it is not the case that we produce a report on institutions because we like institutions and want to create them. no, we do this because they are the only way to tackle the problems we are facing today. we have been talking about, for example, cleaning up the banks. you need funds to clean up these banks at the european level. on refugee flows, you need a border and coast guard to tackle that problem. in the future, on terrorist threats, you will need an investigation and intelligence capacity at european level. so we are pleading for a number of institutional reforms, not because we like institutions, but because this is the only way to tackle at a european level the challenges we are facing today. the second thing is that this is only a bigger europe. no, in the report it is about a smaller commission, and less regulation but more policies – that is what we need – and also social policies and tax policies. and then, finally, it is too visionary. well i think that in europe we need a vision and a project again. it is only when we show the way forward and a vision for the future, rather than just concentrating on day-to-day politics, that people and citizens can again be converted to the new european ideals. for the rest, i am very encouraged that a big majority of the house has supported the report instead of the extremes on the left and the right.
i would like to address mrs mogherini, who unfortunately cannot be here today. i would like to congratulate her on the statement she made last week. her message was strong and clear. the regularization legislation adopted by the israeli parliament on 6 february must be condemned. we're the ones who did this. it is a dangerous step in the wrong direction and it goes beyond the rules of international law. but more is needed. the latest developments show very clearly that words are not enough. in april 2003, the quartet presented its roadmap for peace. today, almost 14 years later, the two-state solution is further away than ever. in recent months, israeli settlement expansion, the demolition of palestinian homes and european-funded projects have taken on alarming proportions. human rights organisations are systematically silenced. only by taking concrete measures can we change this. we must make it clear that international law must be respected by both sides. if this does not happen, peace and a two-state solution will become a complete utopia. i would like to put a few very specific questions to you. in view of the situation, how do you intend to further deepen bilateral relations with israel, and in particular to shape the partnership agreements? secondly, what consequences will you attach to the one-state reality? thirdly, what will be the concrete steps to further refine the policy of differentiation between israel and the occupied palestinian territory? finally, a question which i have asked here on several occasions: how far have we come in terms of repayment of the european projects which have been destroyed?
mr president, the settlement law and the decision to expand it - more than 6,000 new homes in january 2017 alone - are simply another israeli annexation of palestinian territory. the statements and condemnations have shown their limits towards the israeli government. the union must now act. are we going to wait for the total annexation of the west bank before we can act? in the context of the association agreement, which many of my colleagues have mentioned, it is quite possible to take concrete measures. article 2 of the eu-israel agreement says, and i quote: 'relations between the european union and israel shall be based on respect for human rights and democratic principles'. it can therefore be said that article 2 is simply not being respected, which should make it possible to question this association agreement or even to make it conditional on further discussions.
i would like to thank the rapporteur and my colleagues for the good cooperation. we have a good balance and this is supported by the european commission's report. some points that are important to us: firstly, we must be clear about economic and budgetary policy. we need stability, especially in the current economic and political climate. it is therefore important that the rules are not only on paper but also respected and enforced. this is essential for the confidence of businesses and investors and for the confidence of citizens in europe. secondly, we reiterate the importance of reforms and the modernisation of economies. we ask member states to follow up the country-specific recommendations more closely. the potential for economic growth, innovation and investment is still too limited. the single market is not yet complete and the rules are not applied in the same way everywhere. our companies are suffering. they must have access to risk capital. we need to continue to invest in research and development, education and health care to be more competitive in the world. my last point concerns compromise x, which just failed at econ yesterday. this compromise adds a new element to the report. it calls for greater attention to the sustainability of budgets. we often see that there is a conflict between short-term and long-term political objectives. i think that the passing of debts on to the next generation for short-term political reasons is not only economically irresponsible but also ethically irresponsible. to counteract this, we ask the commission to develop an indicator to measure the impact of the budget on future generations. we hope that this transparency will help us to make better budgetary decisions, including for our children and grandchildren. the commission has already proposed that the compromise be put to the vote in plenary by a majority vote.
mr president, the european semester is a mechanism for coordinating economic policies. it would be a grave mistake to forget the social aspect. hence the importance of having introduced a more qualitative approach to employment into the semester. i am particularly grateful to the rapporteur, mrs toom, for including social concerns in her report, such as the strengthening of the youth guarantee, the importance of education policies, support for micro-enterprises, ensuring the sustainability of social security systems and the participation of the social partners in the semester. but it seems to us, at s&d level, that there are essential points missing from this report. that is why we are proposing amendments for tomorrow's plenary session, for example on the question of trade union law and collective bargaining, which guarantee better social cohesion in the field of work, a decent minimum wage, the fight against the wage gap, the quality of employment and access to a public pension system that is dignified and sustainable, but not only sustainable in terms of longevity, but also in other areas of the working world. we also want to insist on reform plans based on solidarity, integration and social justice, which protect the most vulnerable and raise the standard of living for all. i am convinced that these reasonable proposals for improvements will be heard by a large number of people in the plenary tomorrow, so that we can take a step forward for the well-being of all european citizens.
we note that a sustained three-pronged policy of investment, structural reform and sound public finances is beginning to bear fruit. despite all the critics, everyone has to admit that the efsi fund works. the objective was to inject an additional 315 billion in investment into our economy by mid-2018, and according to recent evaluations, the commission is well on track to achieve that result. the belgian economy, with an output of 2.9 billion, is ranked seventh in the european ranking. secondly, however, structural reforms are also being implemented. the member states themselves do learn from each other's good examples, so that labour market reforms ensure quality job creation. the figures are right there, too. in the third quarter of 2016, as many as 2,8 million additional fixed contracts were added. that's six times the number of temporary jobs. quality jobs provide protection, not just because they earn money, but because they also provide social protection. an adequate social system empowers countries and their citizens in times of economic downturn and that is why i look forward to the proposal for a european social pillar. it's an on-again, off-again story, though. the strict path of the stability and growth pact must not be abandoned. let's look at the national debt, for example. the european union as a whole is fortunately in decline, but belgium is lagging behind with a public debt of 107 per cent of our gdp. a sword of damocles hangs over the future generations. it is therefore high time that my country too took up its responsibilities and put a debt brake in place.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the european union's financial system is not undermined by the failure of the european union to meet its obligations under the treaty. how we can put an end to this is a question we have to deal with here in the european union. how can we break this bond? we have put in place a whole series of regulations to make our banks safer, but our work is far from over. the european central bank is now pursuing a monetary policy which is making our financial system once again more insecure. free money makes people look for higher returns and therefore also look for more risks. on the other hand, we see that the italian banks have been in trouble for years, that they are almost drowning and that we have to keep them afloat day after day. all of which points to that old disease of the european union: if we have rules, they are circumvented too much, they are not respected enough, they are not applied well enough. and today we're seeing that again. we must break this spiral. we must break these bad habits. we must also put an end to state aid to the banks. we are taking another big step forward here.
the committee on the environment, public health and food safety has adopted a report on the proposal for a council directive on the approximation of the laws of the member states relating to the protection of the health of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos at work. the committee on the environment, public health and food safety recommends, by a large majority, that ceta be adopted. as rapporteur, i tried to evaluate ceta in a fair but critical way. i took as a starting point our resolution of april 2015 on ttip. and in my view, ceta fails to meet four conditions. one, there is no explicit reference to the precautionary principle. secondly, regulatory cooperation, while voluntary, is not limited to specific sectors where the eu and canada have an equal level of protection. thirdly, ceta contains provisions on public and social services which affect the discretion of member states. fourth, ceta contains provisions which undermine our long-term gmo legislation. in addition, there is the ics arbitration mechanism, which provides a parallel jurisdiction for foreign investors, undermining the sovereignty of member states. i also expressed my concern as rapporteur about the provisions on chemicals legislation, the negative list on public and social services, the undermining of standards on pesticide residues, the regulations on endocrine disruptors, the threat of the rich-to-poor approach to food safety and the harmful provisions affecting our climate and energy policies. the vast majority of colleagues in the envi committee were not listening to this, despite the objections of millions of citizens. i therefore recommend that you, as rapporteur, approve ceta, although i may be voting against it myself.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i should like to begin by congratulating commissioner malmström on her extraordinary patience, remarkable competence and, above all, her willingness to deal with the often unfair and untrue statements she has made. because, after all, will ceta force us to eat chlorinated chicken, hormone-treated beef, gmo's or dismantle our public services? of course not! what is not possible today will not be possible tomorrow. we will remain free to further strengthen our quality standards and develop our public services. are we giving multinationals an undue weight that will harm our legal system? of course not! the desire to establish a new international court only strengthens our capacity to enforce the rules globally and to inspire stricter ones if we wish. ladies and gentlemen, if we cannot sign an agreement with canada, with whom can we sign one? who can honestly believe that a country of 35 million people, whose democratic performance and social and environmental protection often exceeds the standards of many european states, could deviate from the economic, social and political model of the world's leading economic power? those who oppose this agreement and abuse the good faith of citizens deny the social, human and economic progress of free trade, that is the truth. in fact, it is an ideological block, nothing more!
madam president, madam commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the caricature that there are only two schools of thought in international trade - the protectionist school, advocated by the populists, and the ultra-liberal school, shared by a large majority of this parliament - is intolerable. i would even say that this bipolar conception is suicidal for europe today. europe can do better, europe deserves better. at a time when everyone is wondering about their future and when the breakdown of confidence in the political and economic system is at its peak, we in this house are going to sign an agreement. we're going to vote a "business as usual" deal; an agreement that gives more rights to multinationals; an agreement that weakens public services, farmers, smes and the least exporting countries; an agreement that puts investor rights above social and environmental rights; a covenant that is not a new generation covenant, but a covenant of the past. that is why i shall vote against it.
in writing. ceta as it stands is not acceptable, as it is a new generation agreement aimed at harmonising standards. but it does not guarantee that these will be raised. these standards are essential and deeply cultural. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. imagine what the impact could be on food or social requirements, if the safeguards were not clearly established. today, the european union is negotiating major trade agreements that consider trade to be the alpha and omega of development, without first defining its positioning from an economic, social and environmental point of view. the objective behind these types of agreements is cooperation and regulatory convergence to remove non-tariff barriers to trade, but without first addressing the issue of differences in regulations and public aid, the european union runs the risk of unfair competition between canadians and europeans. these are among the reasons why i am voting against ceta today.
"blue card" question. what is it? i would like to point out something to the honourable member. you say here: we cannot accept guy verhofstadt's crazy idea of a european army. i would like to say that the eurobarometer is a survey of the members of the european parliament, of all the citizens of europe, and that it shows very clearly that 80 per cent of our citizens want europe to be a european defence. so it's not that crazy. i would also point out that if you look at the defence expenditure of the 28 member states, you will find that we spend half of america at a return of only 15 per cent. may i ask you a question ? i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is not in favour of saving so that we can pool all these resources and produce better output, thus better protecting our european citizens?
tomorrow the european parliament will normally adopt a new anti-terrorism directive. the aim is to arrive at common definitions of terrorist offences, to which minimum penalties will be linked. the terrorist motive is now considered an aggravating circumstance. for example, online hate speech by is sympathizers will now have to be prosecuted across the eu and be taken offline. also prohibited are hacking of critical infrastructure, travel and recruitment for terrorist motives. at the same time, there is attention for the victims. the directive requires member states to take concrete measures to provide better medical, psychological and legal assistance to victims of terrorism and/or their families. efficient assistance, swift legal settlement of claims for damages and the elimination of the administrative agony of being recognised as a victim of terrorism, that is the aim. after the attacks in brussels on 22 march last year, it became clear that there was still a lot of room for improvement in this area in my country too. that is why the n-va is satisfied with the proposal.
in writing. strengthening european cooperation in the fight against terrorism is a top priority. the new eu directive will strengthen the common approach to terrorism and syrian fighters in the member states. with these measures we are taking a crucial step towards much closer and more efficient cooperation between national intelligence services. they will automatically exchange information with each other when detecting terrorist activities and share information on suspected and convicted terrorists with europol. that is the only way to prevent terrorist acts and to deal with the terrorist threat. the commission is also proposing to introduce a system of checks at the external borders of the union. that is of course in the interest of all europeans. it is a crucial intervention to keep our internal borders open, to protect schengen. it is a key task for the eu, which we must and will now put into practice.
on behalf of the group of the european right with this agreement we have reached with the council, we will now be able to carry out extensive screening of all eu citizens, including returning syrian fighters with belgian passports. this is not a day too soon. today, there is only a minimal legal check on travellers with an eu passport who land at a european airport from outside schengen. this will soon be a thing of the past, because thanks to the n-va, the rules are now being tightened. soon all travelers will be subjected to systematic scrutiny. an excellent case for the safety of our citizens. in concrete terms, the schengen border control system, which defines the practical implementation of controls at the european external borders, is being adapted. this will help to determine more quickly whether travellers seeking to enter europe are being searched by police or intelligence services and whether their passports are forged or stolen. instead of travelling further, criminals are arrested immediately thanks to the new rules. we are therefore committed to further strengthening our external borders.
madam president, commissioner, as a democrat from the alde group, i am in full support of the adoption of this regulation, which will oblige the member states to carry out systematic checks on all persons, citizens or nationals of third countries, who cross the external border. why would you do that? because we know that many of the perpetrators of the attacks which have bloodied our countries were trained abroad and returned across the external border. because we know that there are still some 2 500 european fighters in syria, iraq and libya and that as daesh retreats, many of them will return, necessarily across the external border. in these circumstances, it is imperative to carry out systematic checks to reduce the risk of attacks. that said, i would like to stress that the obligatory corollary of these systematic checks is that member states must communicate the information they hold on terrorism suspects so that this information is available to border guards. if not, the increased controls will be a needless and costly scarecrow and a media myth.
at present, information should only be shared in exceptional cases. but given the changed security situation, the question is whether the exchange of information between member states on terrorism and serious crime should remain free of obligations. my party is in favour of a thorough exchange of information because it promotes security. we were just talking about the border control regulation, which introduces systematic checks on everyone entering or leaving the european union, including through international airports. the aim is to screen both eu and non-eu citizens by consulting eu databases such as the sis and international databases such as the sltd system. it goes without saying that these databases should contain relevant data. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the environment from harmful substances. the uniform definitions of terrorist offences should also facilitate this. at the same time, we understand that intelligence services may exchange just less information if they are forced to share everything with countries they do not fully trust. the fact that the council is now considering a directive for a stricter legal framework for the exchange of information and intelligence between law enforcement agencies at union level is already an interesting option. it is really important to find a balanced and widely accepted solution.
on behalf of the alde group. mr president, minister, commissioner, we all agree that the exchange of information is essential. commissioner, i am not going to talk about technical matters today. you've taught us a lesson, so i won't venture that far. i do not dispute the progress made in recent years in the exchange of information, but, mr president-in-office, this is the least we can do after the events we have seen. the increase in the number of jobs is a clear indication of how insufficient this was in the past. the need therefore remains the same and the limited progress made is not sufficient. i also take note of what the commissioner said and welcome it with regard to the creation of the high level group on interoperability. it is nevertheless extraordinary, commissioner, that it took ten or twelve information collection systems at european level, covering very different subjects, to be set up before there was any real concern, in a systematic way, about their interoperability. it's not a criticism, you're not responsible. i would also like to warmly welcome the commission's proposal to make it compulsory to report persons suspected of terrorism in the schengen information system. but that is not enough, everyone agrees. this obligation concerns only one database, the sis, and one category of suspected persons, terrorists. am i wrong, commissioner? on the other hand, the basis on which law enforcement agencies still operate in the field of information is pre-lisbon. we are still in the pre-lisbon. however, the transitional period for actions in the field of information exchange, as set out in protocol 36 of the treaties of the european union, expired on 1 december 2014, commissioner, and it is provided in the declaration 50 attached thereto that the european parliament, the council and the commission will seek to adopt legal acts amending or replacing union acts which were adopted before the entry into force of the treaty of lisbon. so, commissioner, when is this proposal?
in writing. driverless cars, industrial automation, robots that assist people, sex robots, etc.: robotics has become an important part of our daily lives. today we are voting for a european framework on robotics rules. this is the first european and probably world-wide attempt to officially move robotics from science fiction to reality. the theme is exciting, but it should not make us forget the issues of employment and the fight against inequality. anticipating change is the best way to manage it. the employment issue must be placed at the centre of priorities to defend workers' rights and accompany them in these changes as well as the ways of financing social impacts. we also ask that the issue of inequality be dealt with and highlighted in this text. indeed, without guidance, the development of robotics could result in a significant concentration of wealth and power in the hands of a minority. europe has a role to play in this respect too.
a writer. madam president, i would like to congratulate mrs evelyn and myself on your election as vice-president, ladies and gentlemen. i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply. i'm going to ask you two simple questions tonight, and i think you'd actually like to answer those simple questions with no. but i would like to ask the first question: is this german toll for cars in line with eu law? i would also like to quote a well-known expert on these matters who said that a road fee is only in line with eu law if it does not discriminate on grounds of nationality. that is not what i am saying, that is what you said, commissioner bulc, at the beginning of the infringement procedure in june 2015. let us therefore proceed from this principle. has this discrimination been eliminated by the so-called agreement? not in my opinion. as a result, eu foreigners are still more affected than german nationals. in other words, a belgian driver with a euro 6 car pays the toll, but a german driver does not. the answer to the first question, 'is the new german toll model in line with eu law?' is therefore 'no'. i come now to the second question: is this toll a symbol of good european cooperation? again, the answer is no, it is not. there is currently no european toll system. this toll is contrary to the fundamental principles of our union, which you and the commission must protect. we must not have the impression that our european partners are financing german roads at the expense of their neighbours. because what would be the consequence?
