mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter. what does the european union need more than ever? a simple question, a simple answer: yes the eu needs success stories! but we don't have to invent these success stories or artificially market them. no, these success stories are right in front of us, right under our noses. i am thinking of the new cycle path, the village cultural centre, cross-border hospital structures or the neighbourhood house where the integration of the unemployed takes place. the europe that is supposedly so far from the people is in every market place, on every corner, but is constantly overlooked by most passersby. thanks to eu regional policy, european money is flowing into thousands of good and concrete ideas right at the doorstep of every european. all this and much more would not exist without europe, but unfortunately citizens often do not know this. europe is tirelessly at work everywhere, but remains largely invisible. we have to do something! the european idea must and can be better told. the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council for adoption. the delay in adopting and implementing aid programmes is a major obstacle in this respect. this is where eu regional policy needs to change in its implementation: faster, better, less bureaucracy and better told. we will then be able to inspire many more people with good ideas for good projects.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on the internal market and consumer protection. in the committee on the internal market and consumer protection we have been resolute in our efforts to improve passenger protection. today, too often, passengers are left in the dark when they encounter problems on their air travel. where can they file a complaint? who can give them the most up-to-date information, and so on? we also believe that those who book air travel offline should have the same rights and obligations as those who do so online. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the price of the product is not too high. the safety aspect must also be taken into account. we must focus on new control technologies and also on proportionate penalties for those who do not comply with the standards, which in turn contribute to a safer travel environment and shorter travel times. finally, as rapporteur for imco, i am disappointed that more elements of our texts were not included. we believe that they are essential to the development of a strong and comprehensive aviation strategy.

on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, i find it a crazy idea that it is now mr juncker’s fault that there is a brexit. i thought it was more or less an internal catfight within the conservative party that was the reason for brexit. it is crazy how some people then blame this house here and say that it is the cause. but that said, i welcome the contribution from the commission and i certainly welcome your introduction, mr juncker, because you made mention of the founding fathers: altiero spinelli, and i could add paul-henri spaak, i could add heinrich von brentano, founding father of the german grundgesetz, and i could add jean monnet and robert schuman. i could even add winston churchill, because from 1946 to 1951 – a part that was forgotten by mr boris johnson in this biography of winston churchill – he was the leader of european integration and of the future european union. that said, it is important to recognise one thing here, which is that, before we go to rome for the festivities, what we want today is something different from the treaty of rome, because the treaty of rome was a customs union. it was maybe the right answer in 1957 but, let us be honest, it is not the answer to the challenges we are facing today. i think it is very important to talk about the founding fathers because they had more ambition than just the treaty of rome. the treaty of rome was a fall-back option, a fall-back solution, because they were not able – because of the ‘no’ vote in the french national assembly – to make a defence union, to make a political union, and to make an economic union. it is wise to remember what they wanted after the second world war, which was to create a union that was capable and decisive. but what is our problem today? you will find it in your five scenarios. you have given us five scenarios but what i remember from your introduction is that we can forget the first three. let us talk about scenarios four and five, and i think that is the right decision to make, but, let us be honest: what is lacking in the european union of today? why are we not capable of stabilising our neighbourhood? it is because we do not have the capabilities to do so. why are we not capable of finding a solution to the migration crisis and the refugee crisis? because we have no capabilities to do so at european level. why are we not capable of cleaning up our banks, eight years after lehman brothers? eight years after, we are still facing problems with an unrealised banking union, with no recovery fund and so on. why? because we have no european capacities and capabilities. and finally, why do we not have big european internet companies? if we talk about apple, microsoft, amazon, samsung, they are all american or asian. and why? because we do not have a completed digital market in the european union: no european capacities, for example for a european telecom authority or a digital authority, as you propose in your fourth scenario. that is the lesson. we are still a loose confederation of nation states based on the unanimity rule, and if there is one country blocking – and you know this better than i do, mr juncker, having been a member for a long time of the european council – if only one member says ‘no’, then we apply the unanimity rule and we do not go forward. too little, too late – that is the consequence of the absence of the european capacities and european capabilities that we desperately need. so we do not need to start asking whether it is four, five, or three, because we are already familiar with the different circles in the european union. we have 50 exceptions in the european budget. there are 50 different circles in the european union. the question now is how can we overcome that? how, for one, do we convince european leaders at national level to make the jump forward that we desperately need? that is the question, and therefore our proposal from parliament, in our papers and in the reports that have been discussed and approved two weeks ago, is to start an interinstitutional reflection. we alone cannot decide this. you alone, mr juncker, cannot decide from your side in the commission. even the council cannot do that – and maybe they do not have the inspiration to do it. so our plea, mr president of the commission, is that you back our proposal for an interinstitutional reflection from day one, from now on, so that we are not only talking about brexit here in this house, but we also open an offensive – a positive, optimistic reform of the union in parallel with brexit. and it is not only a negative story of one country leaving us, but also a story of 27 who know that their destiny in the future will be in unity, together, in the european union.
for the greens/efa group mr president, ladies and gentlemen, it is not enough to say that the european union is not at its best, and i thank you, mr president juncker, for coming forward with proposals. in your paper you attempt to answer the question of 'how'. we believe that before answering this question, we should ask ourselves why, and in particular why an increasing number of our citizens have become defiant, or even openly hostile, to the very idea of european integration. it is clear that in their eyes the european union has lost its legitimacy. there is, of course, the question of institutional legitimacy: the union would be too distant, too opaque; decisions would be taken in too hidden a way by politicians who would not sufficiently assume their responsibilities. if that were the diagnosis, we would expect ambitious proposals on transparency and democratic control, and all of this is necessary. but i think that the mistrust has deeper roots, because if it is about legitimacy, it is legitimacy by the result. what can be achieved by a single market and a single currency, combined with tax and social competition? what can be the result of free trade agreements and regulatory standards which make the right to profit the absolute and supreme norm, if not the enrichment of the holders of capital at the expense of all others? this, i think, is the source of the large battalions of our fellow citizens who are joining the eurosceptics, or even the europhobes. if the european union were a vehicle, it would be less appropriate today to discuss its shape, the number of seats or even the speed of this vehicle than its engine and, above all, its direction. mr juncker, if i may, you propose that we should go on the road, backwards, forwards together or in differentiated groups, but always in one direction, as if the idea of a change of course were simply unthinkable. you had the courage, mr juncker, to put the right diagnosis to us when we faced the challenge of asylum and immigration. you have told us - and you were right - that if we want to keep this area of free movement which we so much want and want, we cannot do without a common asylum and immigration policy and a common management of our borders. you had that courage. strangely, in the face of the explosion of inequality and a single currency that is tangible and that is to say little, you do not have the courage to tell the historical truth. there is no - i mean no - monetary union in the history of mankind that has lasted without fiscal union, without social union and, therefore, without political and democratic union. this is the contradiction we must resolve today if we want to preserve the single market and the single currency, and we want to do so. if we want to win back the hearts and minds of our fellow citizens, mr president, we believe it is urgent to reconnect the construction of europe with its original ambition, namely to secure peace on this continent - and this remains topical - by the extension of democracy and by shared prosperity. if we want to reconnect europe to this objective, we must abandon this idea of fiscal and social competition and move towards convergence, that is, putting the dignity of all, not the prosperity of a few, at the heart of the european project.
mr president, president juncker, today in belgium it is complimentendag. do you know what that means? it means giving compliments to each other, it is a very special day, so let me start with a compliment. you have written a very comprehensible white paper, something one can easily understand: i can give it to my mother who is 68 years old, or to my daughter who is nine years of age, and they will understand it. it is a good start to a discussion. but having said that, our group is also disappointed because, here in parliament, all of us are elected to prepare the future of europe, and that means getting into the concerns of europe, and you know very well what they are. if you look at the great challenges of europe, they are migration and security, solidarity – or the lack of solidarity – and growing inequalities. you mentioned the social pillar, and, of course, climate change. if you want to address those things – and we need to do that, it is our mutual responsibility – then you need a union in which we work much more closely together. so you do not need all these scenarios. we know which scenario we need to prepare the future of europe.
i am glad that you have spoken so much. but i'd like to see action. a year ago europol reported that there were 10 000 missing persons in europe. today child focus belgium announces that 116 new cases have been opened in belgium. last week i was visiting dunkirk, a two-hour drive from brussels. i'd like to invite you to come and see the french refugee camp there. there are 100 unaccompanied minors seeking asylum in wooden barracks, without care, without education and without seeing anyone to submit an asylum application. this is the europe of 2017! we cannot tolerate that. i'm officially asking you for a real action plan with proper reception and short procedures. the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat trafficking in human beings. where children are sitting, authorities should take pictures so that no one reports a missing child by e-mail after 8 or 9 days. you know, commissioner, if my dog does not come home tonight, i will go after my dog harder than europe searches for the 10 000 missing children. come up with that plan of action. i am happy to report to you on the recommendations of the malta conference. i was there myself and i want to hand them over to you personally. it's got some real stuff in it!
in writing. as we enter 2017 and all the efforts made in many areas are constantly being affirmed, it is still too easy to give examples of women still not being placed on an equal footing with men. the wage gap is one such example and remains too large, with an average of 16.7% in 2014. in fact, for equal work, a woman is paid 83.6 cents for every euro paid to a man. at this rate, equal pay won't be a reality until 2084. it is clear that much remains to be done in this area. the pay gap is still a reality, and change is too slow. our aim should be, more than ever, to address the causes and consequences of these gaps in order to succeed in reducing these persistent differences between men and women. another objective is to change attitudes, especially at the level of some men, who are not ready enough to work on this problem. as i said before, men and women are not and will never be the same, but they should at least have the same rights.
in writing. ladies and gentlemen, i would ask you to vote in favour of this report. in recent years, drug companies have charged exorbitant prices for their new products. in my country, belgium, we are talking about more than 43 000 euros for the treatment of hepatitis c, but also more than 100 000 euros for a year of breast cancer treatment. in the end, these prices put the social security budget at risk. in addition, the reputation of the most expensive drugs as innovative is often exaggerated. are these really innovations? to justify high prices, pharmaceutical companies point to the high costs of drug research and development. but how much does it really cost to develop a new drug? the information is not public. in addition, a significant proportion of research is carried out in universities financed by public funds. so he is pleased that we are now addressing the matter. thank you for your attention.
i would like to speak a little about turkey, about the statements made by mr erdogan, who is strongly opposed to the european union. we have seen the riots in the netherlands, we have heard mr erdogan's statements calling the dutch fascists, calling them nazis. this is not possible. we cannot tolerate this as a european parliament. i sincerely believe that we should add this to this week's agenda. i know that this is scheduled for the april part-session, but we have the political news. you can rightly hide behind the procedures, behind the rules and say that this was not done in time. that's right, this wasn't put in at the right time: i'm saying this now. but there's something else we can do. we cannot hide behind the rules and debate the political issues of the day - as real politicians, as a real parliament - and therefore also debate turkey this week.
mr president, madam commissioner, access to goods and services and the provision of goods and services are two areas where gender equality has never really been taken into account. these are sectors which are increasingly dominated by men. this is why strong positions are needed in this respect. while the proposed directive offers strong tools to ensure the principle of equality between men and women, the full potential of the directive depends on raising awareness among service providers and users, and on the coherent integration of the gender dimension in the sectors covered by this provision. on the issue of eu gender equality funds, i would like to stress that although there are several economic support programmes relating to gender equality, very few of these programmes have clear objectives, specific means, effective implementation or systematic monitoring. as regards the annual report on gender equality, i would like to recall that, in general, all of us european human rights defenders must mobilise to defend equal rights between women and men. in the professional sector, i see that there are still many differences. i would remind the council that it is thanks to him or because of him that the european maternity leave is in the dustbin. what about the 28 member states which have again decided to postpone the discussion on the issue of women's representation on company boards? i will ask the commission for clear measures to combat sexual harassment in the workplace more effectively. women are still discriminated against in the world of work, especially when they are lesbian, bisexual or transgender. for the latter, did you know that, for example, more than 30% of transgender jobseekers have experienced severe discrimination in their job search? behind these statistics are people who suffer daily from the violation of their rights. (the rapporteur agrees to answer a question by mr glinne) (rule 162 (8) of the rules of procedure)
answer is "blue cardboard". there is a lot of discrimination, but it is clear that we must focus on access to professions, access to training, access to education. we have a lot of respect for women who choose to stay at home, have children, raise them, it's their choice, there's no worries. but what's abhorrent about our society is that 60 percent of people who graduate from college are women, and the higher you go up in the hierarchy of societies -- whether in the public sector or the private sector -- the more they disappear according to the glass ceiling. discrimination in relation to the pay gap is also shocking and it is therefore quite normal that we try to redress these injustices, which have too often been admitted. today we have to fight and make progress - because there is progress - go much faster.
i'm advocating an inclusive society with equal rights and duties for all. this applies not only to all genders, particularly women and girls, but also to people with disabilities and other minority groups. a structural approach to gender inequality is needed. compulsory quotas or increased funding without concrete measures do not guarantee greater equality in the labour market or in the public sphere. people should be paid equally for equal work. we can only close the gender pay gap with more transparency and a thorough evaluation of the gender pay gap. it is unacceptable that we see little progress in the fight against violence against women and gender-based violence. violence against women, but also against men and children, is a very serious violation of fundamental rights. i therefore call for support for the istanbul convention to eradicate this extreme form of discrimination.
the eu is a world leader in women's rights. still, we must not think that the battle is over. research shows that we are only halfway there in terms of gender equality and that progress is slow. on 14 march, the day for equal pay was celebrated. women in belgium still earn on average 20% less than men. so they must start working ten years earlier to receive the same pay. but the pension gap also remains unacceptably wide. many women work part-time involuntarily because of gender stereotyped expectations or because they work in precarious statuses. this approach is essential to achieving gender equality. the extension of paternity and parental leave and the encouragement of fathers to take more of them is necessary to achieve a more equal distribution of care responsibilities in the family. the role of employers is also important. they must create a company culture that does not prevent fathers from taking their parental leave. finally, promoting gender equality also means combating gender stereotypes throughout society, starting at an early age. for example, many gender stereotypes can still be found in schoolbooks or textbooks. we have to put an end to this.
mr president, commissioner, you are the commissioner for security, so it is quite logical that you have placed your intervention in the context of the fight against terrorism. i'd like to believe you, but i'm having second thoughts. terrorists do not go to the gun shops to buy their weapons, presenting their id cards and a certificate of good conduct. i have here the europol report from november 2016, which indicates that terrorists procure weapons of war on the illegal market, then on the internet, and that the deactivation of weapons and the activation of deactivated weapons were used only in one case, that of the paris attacks. we are wrong, commissioner: the priority is to combat the illicit trade in arms and not to hinder those who legally practice sport or collectors. of course, there are many positive elements in the proposal and i would like to follow them, but i am faced with a fundamental problem which i personally cannot ignore: the sale of arms to private individuals on the internet. i don't understand why certain medicines are forbidden to be sold on the internet without a doctor's prescription. we'd be more strict on medication than we are on guns. i do not accept this situation and i shall vote against this motion reluctantly.
in writing. strengthening controls on firearms licences, but also on checks on blank or poorly neutralised weapons such as those used in the paris terrorist attack, improving traceability, increasing and regulating the mutual exchange of information on weapons: these are the objectives of our revised legislative text, the result of almost a year's negotiations and which will be voted on this afternoon in plenary. a text for a safer europe, combining greater security for citizens and the interests of legitimate gun owners. our text establishes a common legislative base for all the member states and guarantees the necessary consistency that is much awaited by the most rigorous european countries in this field, such as belgium, germany and france. the member states remain free to be even stricter on their own territory than the european legislation suggests. the new rules reconcile the need for greater security to which citizens are entitled with the interests of legitimate gun owners such as hunters, sportsmen, collectors, reservists and extras.
the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos at work is a very good one. the eu is heavily dependent on third countries for imports of raw materials. many of these resources are in danger of being exhausted in the relatively short term. yet we are extremely sloppy with it. each european citizen produces about five tonnes of waste a year: the weight of an adult african elephant. and we only recycle a third of that. our waste mountain is growing, while precious resources are still too often lost underground. we need to break that dynamic and make a strong commitment to a circular economy, where we recover valuable resources. this is not only good for the environment, it is also necessary to keep our economy competitive. more recycling and making waste disposal a winding-up story is essential, but better recycling alone is not enough. if we really want to shift to a circular economy, we also need to make effective use of less waste, more reuse and closing cycles through innovative business models. everyone's got to take a bath, including the producers. in addition, a single method of measuring recycling rates should ensure that we no longer compare apples to pears and that everyone takes responsibility. flanders, the nation i represent, is a pioneer in the field of materials management and the circular economy. the path we have travelled over the last 20 years has strengthened my conviction that much is possible if the political will is there. we can learn a lot from each other. i am thinking, for example, of our best practices, such as the concept of 'circular shops' to encourage reuse and innovative ways of recovering organic waste. trash is cash: this pun came from you, mr timmermans. a lot of stakeholders and companies have already understood that. the ball is now in our court to design a good legislative framework that provides the right incentives and sets the bar high enough.
mr president, each european country has its own talents. germany has the happiest workers, estonia produces the least waste and my country, belgium, is the european champion in recycling its packaging - more than 80%, 64% of it, close to the european average. this means that we are interested in this circular economy package and that the targets set by the european parliament for 2030 in the packaging and packaging waste directive, namely 80% for wood and 90% for aluminium and glass, are clearly achievable. now, as our rapporteur has said, this legislative package must be thought of as a starting point. so be careful not to make a false start. i come now to what i wanted to talk about this morning, the issue of food waste: 80 million tonnes of food wasted every year, 400 euros of food thrown away per household. beyond the ethical problem which is unbearable, there is economic loss, ecological loss. in conclusion, it is therefore essential to vote for amendments nos 145 and 236 keep these two numbers to halve this waste by 2030.
let me begin, as many have done before me, by thanking our rapporteur, mrs bonafè, for her incredibly hard work. she has not only brought the whole dossier to a fantastic conclusion with the shadow rapporteurs, [...] we shall see in the vote, but she has also taken the lead on behalf of our group and with the other groups in putting this high on the political agenda. you referred to it yourself, commissioner, in your introduction. at first it was a little difficult for the commission and parliament to find common ground in this story. we did that. i would like to say that the commission is very much aware of the problems which have arisen in the past, and that it is not the commission's intention to make a distinction between the two. this is essentially about our economy: how to change our economy, how to make it truly sustainable and, as the commission's assessment also indicates, how to create jobs, often local jobs. this is actually the summary of what is important in this dossier, and this means that this is the beginning of a number of steps that have to come. because it can't just be about waste, it has to be about the design of our products and how our economy turns this into a truly circular experience. it also includes, for example, a sharing economy: how can we go from owning products to sharing our products or renting our products? there's a much more sustainable way to look at it. in any case, i wish us a fantastically good vote, within the general objectives of the european union.
in writing. we want member states to take preventive measures and reduce food waste by 30% by 2025 and by 50% by 2030. the total amount of food loss and by-products in flanders is about 2 million tonnes per year. we need to be more careful with our natural resources, the soil, the water use and stop carrying food for miles. i am very pleased that we are making these objectives binding. food waste encompasses the entire production process. this includes food waste. most importantly, this definition enables policy makers to identify clear causes, measure quantities, compare results and define a common strategy, across the whole chain. the circular economy legislative package integrates four waste laws. it is good that the call for a virtually complete phase-out of waste disposal is being made. by 2030, only 5% of our household waste is allowed to go to landfill. this is ambitious, also because it is combined with a higher target for recycling waste (70%). this is how you prevent member states from burning all the waste. parliament now has the mandate to negotiate the final legislative texts with the council (the member states) and the commission.
"blue card" question. what is it? madam president, i think you have confused two things very badly in this debate by comparing trump with putin. let us stay here in the hemisphere with the debate. the point today is that we as europe should let the president of the united states know that women's rights apply to all of us and that we must respect women's rights throughout the world. so if you are now taking this position against abortion - although we have all known your position for a long time - it means that you are actually against the right of self-determination of all women. do you know, ma'am, that every minute somewhere in the world, a woman dies from an unsafe abortion? europe must put a stop to this.
madam president, on january 23, the trump administration made the decision to stop funding ngos, solely because these ngos fund services that serve women's rights and the right to sexual and reproductive education. this decision will deprive millions of women worldwide of access to contraceptive methods. it will increase the use of clandestine abortion, which is risky - we know - for women. it will increase the number of deaths, which are maternal deaths, but it will also increase the number of gynaecological diseases, such as fistulas, or raise the question of prevention as regards aids. we can only take a position at european level, that is to say, no, we are not criticizing the american question, we are saying: what role do we have to play on the international stage in terms of law and women's rights? i think it's our duty to be able to compensate for what americans don't do anymore. today, she decides has raised €180 million. i think we need to complement the means that have been obtained by she decides.
president trump had barely been in office a week when he introduced the global gag rule extensively. surrounded by, shall i say, fellow old grey men. now that's not so unique: his republican predecessors did that to him. but with this, combined with his sexist remarks, i think he's hitting all of us, all of the women here in the hemisphere, very, very hard. we all know america is a global player. that is why we as europe must act more forcefully. we must say as europe: we can't be touching on women's rights. because that 's what this is about: he's cutting funding, but he's also making sexist remarks. so i support the colleagues who have called for support for she decides, i support the belgian minister alexander de croo. we were there, i was at that symposium. what you felt there was phenomenal. i would like to feel that here in the european parliament. that is why, commissioner, my questions are: what are we going to do to counteract the effects? are we going to supplement she decides? what are we going to do to take action against sexism?
i would like to make two further points in this interesting debate. the first point is the situation in the eastern congo. since 2014, more than a thousand people have been brutally slaughtered, women brutally raped, children used as child soldiers. and we actually find that no one is held accountable. we're not going to find out who did this. we're not going to punish the perpetrators. that is why i would urge that an independent inquiry be launched. we must punish those responsible, ensure that the violence in eastern congo is brought to an end and give the victims and their families a face and a name. we owe it to them. the second point i would like to raise is the impending destruction and displacement of the palestinian bedouin community in khan al-ahmar. there is a school there with eu funds which is now in danger of being destroyed. we must also tackle this.
the aim of this legislation is to ensure uniform control of the food chain throughout the european union. i think we should be proud of the work we've done. we have therefore spent hours negotiating, mainly with the council and even with three presidencies. we are harmonizing the work of the various food safety inspection services. we are extending the controls from the controls on food, animals, animal feed, animal welfare and animal health to the controls on plant health, residues and pesticides. we're also focusing on fraud. we've learned from the horse meat scandal that it's necessary. i personally think it is a good thing that the specific controls on organic farming have been taken out of this regulation and will be covered by the specific regulation on organic farming. for organic farming, there are separate certification bodies which monitor not only the quality of the final product but also the quality of the product and the whole production process. the independence of the inspection services is better regulated. unannounced inspections are essential. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the draft budget. there will be more severe fines. the specific clause on whistle-blowers is of course also of great importance. i would have preferred to see the responsibility of veterinarians in slaughterhouses more clearly defined, but that was not feasible. i would ask my group to approve this report.
we're now more than two years after luxleaks. meanwhile, there's been the swiss leaks, the panama papers, the bahamas leaks and who knows what else is coming. the outrage after each new revelation gave us as politicians a clear mandate, indeed a duty, to address these abuses. in the european parliament we have entered into a glove with the two special committees on tax rulings and other measures of a similar nature or effect and the committee of inquiry into money laundering, tax avoidance and tax evasion. we have always come up with concrete proposals for solutions, but unfortunately our power is limited to holding the member states and the commission to account. however, important steps have also been taken in the legislative field, such as the automatic exchange of tax rulings and the anti-beps directive. but is this enough? can we now look our citizens in the eye and promise that luxleaks will never happen again? i'm sure you didn't. i would like to mention three things. first, the list of tax havens is a missing link. i would like to ask the member states to throw aside taboos and come to a single, definitive european list. because as long as there are backdoors, they'll be found. i'm sure of it now. the sanctions attached to this will also be crucial. but secondly, in addition to formal sanctions, we must also address the reputational effects. that is why i am calling on the banks here and now to sign a european charter committing them not to deal with tax havens. thirdly, the back doors within europe must be closed. and let 's be honest here . this can only be done through the proposed ccctb. unfortunately, the council's signals on this are far from encouraging, but it is now or never. in the meantime, several countries have also announced a reduction in their corporate tax rate, making a debate on a possible european floor rate inevitable in the long term. in conclusion: the catch 22 in which the member states are currently holding each other captive in the field of taxation is a sign of short-term thinking and, i regret to say, not of the political courage we need to look our citizens in the eye again and work towards a fair europe.
in writing. in a fair tax system, multinational companies also pay their share and must do so where they add value and make their profits. in july 2016, we voted on the recommendations of the luxleaks special commission report on tax evasion and tax avoidance by multinational companies. eight months later, where are we? the european parliament has just adopted the establishment of a public central register of beneficial owners of offshore companies under the anti-money laundering directive and has reopened negotiations for a common consolidated corporate tax base. i would like to conclude by stressing that we are on the right track. indeed, in less than three years, we have made a huge step forward in the fight against tax evasion. however, we must not stop on this good road and it is essential that we continue our efforts in the months ahead.
mr president, commissioner, first of all i would like to support the excellent report by our colleague, mr siegfried mureşan. my colleague jäätteenmäki has said this - and i will not repeat it - but in fact we find in his proposals almost all our concerns and priorities. i want to focus on a problem which i consider to be essential in the present state of the european project: the level of youth unemployment is too high in the union and is even dramatically unbearable in some countries. at a time when so many populists - and there are some in this room - want to sabotage the european project, we have an obligation to show that the union is a real added value for the younger generations. the youth employment initiative deserves strong support and strengthening. the council's laziness in this matter is a disgrace. the erasmus + programme, which is undeniably successful, must be extended to young apprentices and make room for young workers in the future. our rapporteur also takes up in his report the proposal for a european interrail pass, and the commissioner seemed to find this idea sympathetic. i also find it sympathetic, but it must be both well calibrated and financially manageable so that it does not become an electoral gadget serving the interests of a single political party. in our view, to be worthy of support, this proposal must meet four criteria. firstly, the pass must be available to young people from all backgrounds and not just the wealthy. secondly, the benefit of the pass must be linked to a trip to another country as part of a training or discovery process. thirdly, the full gratuity is not a stake: an adequate personal contribution must be the pledge of the beneficiary's commitment to the travel project. fourthly, funding should not necessarily be based solely on the european budget but can be extended to other sources of funding. finally, the best investment we can make is in our young people. please, let's not miss the future of europe.
everybody in this room has said it before: 20 million people are starving in south sudan, yemen, nigeria and somalia. every day 21,000 people die of hunger around the world. that's one every four seconds. that means, if you can do the math quickly, a huge number of deaths in the time that we're having this debate. mr commissioner, you said: we have a very large relief programme planned. we've been putting money out and arranging for emergency aid. i understand from your words that you see the need and the urgency. all the aid agencies are asking us to do something very urgently. in yemen alone, half a million children are facing starvation. i'm afraid the emergency aid we're providing is not sufficient. i would like to pledge our support from alde to do more: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i'm really afraid that if we don't do more very soon, my children will be asking a committee of inquiry later to say, europe, why didn't you do more?

was the birthplace, with florence, of the renaissance, and we know it was the start of the european union. well, i hope that on the 25th it can also be city of the rebirth of the european union. for the greens/efa group mr president, mr tusk, you are a lucky man, because jarosław kaczyński your sworn enemy wanted your head so badly that he managed to unite the 27 other heads of state and government, none of whom i say no wanted to give him this gift. so you're reinstated. however, i must say that your first term of office did not impress us, because we are wondering, in the face of the unprecedented crisis facing the european union, whether you are the man for the job, whether you have the leadership and the vision to bring europe out of the doldrums. i refer to the letter you wrote to the heads of state and government before the bratislava summit on 13 september. you seem to be taking stock of the disillusionment of many europeans with the construction of europe, citizens who - to quote you - want to know whether the political elites are able to regain control of events and processes that are beyond them, confuse them and sometimes terrify them. as the first challenge, you mention the challenge of migration, and you are right. there is cause for concern when we see that the causes of migration - be it tyrannies, wars, resource depletion or climate change - are nowhere near disappearing. and your answer is fortress europe, or how to keep at a distance and repel those who flee from hell. in addition, in your letter you describe those who oppose this concept of 'fortress europe' as 'politically correct'. is it politically correct, mr tusk, to consider that it is not normal that turkey, which is six times less populated than the european union, twenty times less rich than the european union, is hosting twice as many refugees as the european union? is it politically correct to denounce the imprisonment of asylum seekers by hungary of your friend orbán, without europe saying anything, in violation of international law? is it politically correct to deny that 2017 sees even more people die in the mediterranean after the record of 2016? where are you, mr tusk, when it comes to reminding member states of their humanitarian responsibility and respect for the law? the second challenge you face is terrorism, and again, you are right. i think what you say is very right. when you say that the member states need to cooperate much more, particularly in the collection and use of intelligence, you are right. but you cannot ignore the fact that a number of countries see the widespread surveillance of our citizens as the one and only answer to terrorism. you know that in a democracy, there are at least two basic principles. the first is the separation of powers. this is particularly the case where the judiciary must be able to control the executive. the second basic principle is proportionality between the restrictions applied to people's freedom and the objective pursued. but these two principles are increasingly being violated in a number of our member states, and i shall mention france and belgium this time, not to mention the same countries. where are you, mr tusk, to denounce these deviations in the member states? the third challenge you face is that of globalisation, and again you are right, but i do not understand you anymore. your speech is incomprehensible, because once again - and i quote you - you reiterate that free trade and competition are in the interests of europeans. when this free trade and competition weakens public services and our social security systems, when it levels wages and working conditions down, when it destroys fiscal justice, tell me in the interests of which europeans this neoliberal globalisation is working. i tell you, the only europeans to benefit from this are the holders of capital. the others pay the bill. where are you, mr tusk, when, instead of encouraging this senseless logic, you should be reminding the heads of state and government that the ambition of the european union is shared prosperity? next week, you'll be in rome to celebrate the 60th anniversary of the treaty. this meeting could legitimately be the celebration of 70 years of peace on this continent, with the exception of the former yugoslavia. i think it's something to remember and it's a success. my grandparents lived through two world wars, and i'm lucky i didn't live through those ravages. but if you want this meeting to be the new starting point for european enthusiasm, the rome meeting must be the one where a change of course is being set in motion. we need a fairer europe, one which sets minimum social standards in all the member states and which organises, not fiscal and social competition, but fiscal and social convergence upwards. we need a europe that puts the energy transition at the heart of its investment. why would you do that? of course, it's a climate imperative, everyone knows that, but we also know that it addresses social needs, energy poverty, health needs. more and more people are getting sick on our continent because of fossil fuels. it meets the economic imperative, because every year we pay hundreds of billions of euros outside europe to buy the things we burn, fossil fuels. this is a strategic imperative, because to whom are we paying for these fossil fuels? first and foremost, vladimir putin, who is our supplier for about one third. finally, we need a europe that can steer globalisation towards social, fiscal, environmental and democratic convergence from the top. mr tusk, i have only one question for you: will you be the man for this situation?
the treaty of rome was an acceptable form of european cooperation. it was not institutionalised and the participants remained free member states, which, on a voluntary basis and without hindrance to the optimisation of their own and national interests, formed a union on very clearly defined bases and areas of cooperation: an added value. since 1957, however, european cooperation has been systematically extended and finally, from about the time of the maastricht treaty in 1992, it has completely lost control of its content and its institutions. the people of europe are under guardianship, especially after the introduction of open borders for migration, the schengen treaty and the monetary union. the federal 'always-more-eu' logic must be broken. if the whole of the european union is to be saved from a collective exit scenario, new and wise founding fathers will have to return to an intergovernmental system.
the conclusions of the european council are clear: the european union must be able to take action to combat poverty and social exclusion. but the citizen is tired of hearing words and not seeing action. if that's the focus, why are we spending less than 10 percent of our 2017 budget on security and migration? this is also what we see in the debate on the future of europe. the plans are focused on 2025, but we can't wait that long. the citizens are now expecting action there too, and we can take action: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. we are facing great challenges and they call for action now.
president-in-office of the council. – mr president, in a week’s time we will be commemorating the first anniversary of the brussels attacks – on 22 march – which are still so vivid in our memories. let me start by saying that i agree with you that it is time to take stock of where we are one year after these attacks in terms of security, and i welcome this debate. we have made major progress in the last two years, but we continue to face unprecedented challenges. the european day of remembrance of victims of terrorism on 11 march – last saturday – was an important reminder of why it matters so much to continue our efforts. all citizens have the right to live free from fear, whatever their opinions or beliefs. putting in place measures to prevent and fight terrorism whilst maintaining an open and free society is a delicate balance. while stepping up our counter—terrorism tools, we should preserve our values and fundamental rights. looking back at where we were before 2015, i think we can safely say that we have come a long way. together we have adopted a number of legislative instruments and other measures to prevent and fight terrorism. in 2016 together we successfully concluded the negotiations on the directive on combating terrorism within a very tight schedule so that it could be signed today. one of the keys to ensuring the security of our union is better information exchange and better information management. we are therefore working hard to implement the 2016 roadmap on information exchange and information management containing 50 actions for improving information exchange. the high—level expert group on information systems and interoperability set up by the commission last year is equally important. we are confident that these efforts contribute to enhancing trust amongst member states and europol and to increasing information exchange. the figures are quite promising, for example regarding europol’s databases. the content of the europol information system increased by 34% between 2016 and 2017 and the number of data on foreign fighters increased by 11%. a record number of 1.4 million searches were performed in the europol information system in 2016, an increase of 127%. the use of secure information exchange network application (siena) messaging, which was upgraded to the level ‘eu confidential’ in 2016 and now better suits the needs of intelligence services, has also increased by 19%. as regards information-sharing by security services, europol has created the possibility for counter—terrorism units to communicate bilaterally using the secure information exchange network. the european counter—terrorism centre, operational since january last year, is more and more fulfilling its role as an information and cooperation hub in support of member states. the firearms directive is another important achievement in the fight against terror. yesterday you approved the compromise text, and this will allow the council to vote and successfully close the legislative procedure at first reading. the new directive will enhance the traceability of firearms. it will provide more stringent rules in terms of deactivation of firearms. this is particularly important since it was precisely poorly-deactivated and reactivated firearms that were used in some of the recent terrorist attacks. finally, the new directive also prohibits some dangerous semi-automatic firearms. radicalisation is something that obviously must be addressed, primarily by member states but also by a coordinated and broad approach at eu level. we have started updating the set of guidelines agreed in december 2014 to complement the revised eu strategy for combating radicalisation and recruitment to terrorism to bring it into line with the current developments. this complements the efforts undertaken by the commission in relation to the recently-launched network of national prevention coordinators. focusing on online initiatives, the work of the internet referral unit at europol is crucial. cooperation with service providers has proved successful, and approximately 90% of the content considered illegal is taken down by service providers. at the eu internet forum ministerial meeting in december last year, the internet companies involved announced the creation of a new shared database to facilitate identification of potential terrorist content on their platforms. another important part of our joint endeavours concerns the rights of victims of terrorism, which have been significantly strengthened by last week’s adoption of the directive on combating terrorism. this directive includes a catalogue of specific measures and services for the victims of terrorism. it also introduces a coordination mechanism in case of a terrorist attack. in relation to external borders, an additional safeguard will be provided as of next month, when the amendment to the schengen borders code to reinforce checks against relevant databases at external borders in light of the increased terrorist threats will enter into force. member states will have to carry out systematic checks on all persons – also those enjoying the right of free movement under eu law – when they cross the external border, both at entry and exit, against databases and in order to verify that those persons do not represent a threat to public order and internal security. allow me to say a few words on the external security and defence aspects. in the present international environment, europe must do more to protect its citizens and contribute to peace and stability in its neighbourhood and beyond, including by committing sufficient additional resources. after the endorsement of the eu global strategy by the council in december 2016, the european council addressed this topic at its meeting last week. it welcomed the real progress made in a number of key areas. building on that momentum, work will be taken further and the european council will revert to this issue in june to provide further strategic guidance. i use this opportunity to highlight the fact that last week the european council reiterated the call it made last december for us, as co-legislators, to agree on the proposal for an entry and exit system by june of this year. we have also been called upon to speed up work on the proposal for the european travel information and authorisation system. i hope that together we will be able to meet this call. finally, i can assure you that we are continuously striving to improve, to close gaps and to enhance our security, including by assessing our structures and policy, which can contribute to ensuring the eu policies are fit for purpose. i thank you for this opportunity and look forward to hearing both the commissioners and honourable members in this debate.
member of the commission. – mr president, honourable members, minister borg, today we are commemorating a tragic anniversary. almost one year to the day, terror struck brussels. we are here, united, to pay our respects to the memory of the victims of terrorist attacks. we remember and honour those who died unnecessarily – innocent citizens. we remember and recognise the suffering of their families, and the survivors of the attacks. they have to live forever with the memory of the atrocities. we all know that terrorism is brutality, an act of inhumanity and of callousness. that is why today is also a day of resolve, of resilience, of determination, to do everything in our power to prevent such terrorist attacks from happening again. this is a duty we all share here in this room, and it is ultimately our duty towards our citizens, who increasingly look towards europe to feel safe against terrorism. almost one year ago, we said that the only way to fight terrorism decisively is together, through a genuine and effective security union in europe, where the security of one member state is the security of all. starting from our external borders, where we put in place a european border and coast guard, proposed an eu entry-exit system, and etias, our travel information and authorisation system for visa-free travellers. ensuring systematic checks on everybody crossing our borders; working with our strategic partners like the united states of america, but also our neighbours – the western balkan countries, lebanon, jordan, tunisia and turkey; improving the way we exchange information: interconnecting our information systems to make sure we connect all the dots; establishing our european pnr system to track the movements of dangerous persons; criminalising all aspects of terrorism with our new counter-terrorism directive; strengthening our agencies, starting from europol, where we set up our counter-terrorism centre to help member states in their operations; cutting the access of terrorists to explosives and firearms (you actually voted on our new firearms directive this morning); going after the money of terrorists, with a series of proposals targeting money laundering, asset-freezing, cash movements and suspicious financial flows from third countries; strengthening the security of all modes of transport, which are often the main targets of terrorists. we do this through stepped-up intelligence-sharing, local risk assessments, innovative methods and technologies, and the key role of operators for a successful implementation of measures. and finally, working to counter radicalisation in our communities, schools, prisons, and especially on the internet. now that daesh is losing all its territory on the ground, the internet is where they will focus. i have just returned from silicon valley, where i went to meet the major internet companies working with us in the eu internet forum, an initiative we took two years ago, and it has proved itself very successful. what i confirmed in this visit is that we managed to build a solid relationship of trust and partnership with these companies. our goals are shared. they want to clean up their platforms from all terrorist content. we want to keep our citizens – their users – safe. concrete results from this partnership are already visible. the referral mechanism we set up with europol last year resulted in almost 90% removal rate of the content we refer to the companies. i have seen in action their new project to eliminate terrorist propaganda – a shared database between them to ensure removed terrorist content stays offline. i heard how they want to work with us to bring the smaller, more vulnerable platforms into our partnership. and it is not just about removing terrorist content; it is also about creating content with positive narratives. today, as i said, we launched the civil society empowerment programme, with a eur 10-million financial envelope attached, to help ngos pass positive counter-messages online. things are moving forward, but we still have a lot of work ahead of us. we have put important proposals on the table that you need to adopt swiftly to make a difference for the security of our citizens: entry-exit; etias; the schengen information system, on which work should be completed this year. what we agree should be fully and correctly implemented: pnr; the counter-terrorism directive; our new rules on firearms have entered the crucial stage of implementation, and as you understand, we cannot afford to fail. tragic anniversaries such as today’s underscore our responsibility to deliver without delay for the memory of the innocent victims, for the safety of our citizens, for our collective security.
on behalf of the ppe group. mr president, in my country, 22 march has become a symbol of mourning. a year ago, like other countries before and unfortunately after, brussels was struck by terrorist violence. our thoughts are with the deceased and their families, with all those who are still suffering in their flesh and in their lives, with the victims of 22 march and with all the others in europe and elsewhere. aside from the safety measures, life has now resumed. brussels will always be brussels. 'brussels, my beautiful' as dick annegarn sang it, the european brussels, brussels with its joyful melting pot, a symbol of openness and diversity. the terrorists are demanding that we design our defenses better, but they have failed and will always fail in their attempt to impose their totalitarian vision. identities cannot exclude each other, they must add up: this is the condition for living together. radicalisation leading to terrorism is a complex phenomenon which requires us to act on all fronts to guarantee security while preserving our humanity. lauriane shared the values of democracy, justice and tolerance with her father, a journalist at the public radio station, and she was one of the victims of this heinous act. after this tragedy, on the air, his father michel visart, courageously gave us a message of hope. i echo his words: 'building walls of exclusion or cultivating hatred will not help. if we want a different world, we need this respect, this tolerance and this love. may these words help us to reject the retreat of identity, to strengthen our security, while preserving our freedoms and our living together.
next wednesday will be exactly one year since isil carried out horrific attacks on belgium's zaventem national airport and the maalbeek metro station in the european district. dozens of people were killed and hundreds of people were injured. i would like to express once again my sympathy to the families and friends of the deceased and the injured, whose pain and sorrow i can hardly fathom. in the light of this horror, we have voted for several very concrete european laws to prevent such attacks in the future. i am thinking of the pnr directive, which allows intelligence services to carry out a thorough analysis of passenger data provided when booking an airline ticket. suspected travelers are now much more readily targeted. i am also thinking of the recent amendments to the schengen borders code, which will allow us to check all eu citizens at our external borders, including at our international airports. any returning european jihadist who fought in syria or iraq will now be caught. but our work is far from finished. we must now start implementing the etias system. this means that we can subject all foreigners who wish to visit the eu and do not need an eu visa to a prior check, as the americans already do. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. i hope very much that this matter can be discussed in all serenity in the interests of our citizens and their safety.
mr president, one year ago, almost exactly the same day, a monstrous attack struck brussels: 32 dead, 324 wounded, 224 people in hospital. this terrible record is added to other attacks: madrid, london, paris, nice, berlin, and i forget. in all, several hundred people were killed and nearly ten thousand were injured. the whole of europe was shocked and stunned, wounded, but it quickly rose to the challenge. in response, the european union has taken a series of measures to prevent and combat the terrorist threat. the minister and the commissioner have made an exhaustive list of them, and i shall not go into it. i would like to welcome these initiatives, which we have supported, and those which still need to be taken and which we will support. but that's not the main point of my talk today. i want to spend my time with the victims. i have learned from the press that the last victim hospitalized following the attacks in brussels was able to return home on 15 february 2017. the press isn't saying what condition she's in. this news leads me to ask whether we have properly assessed the suffering and needs of the victims, and whether our compassion goes beyond mere words. i know that the member states concerned - including my own - have taken more or less rapid and comprehensive measures in favour of the victims. i also know, as the minister has pointed out, that the european union, in the new directive on combating terrorist attacks, expressly provides in article 24 that member states must provide support and assistance to victims. but what about european solidarity? we all proclaim that terrorist attacks are a criminal attack on the values we all share, that every attack in one country is an attack on all the others and on all europeans, but my question remains the same. we have created a mechanism, the solidarity fund, to show our solidarity when one of our countries is the victim of a natural disaster. we are in solidarity with the failures of nature, but we are not, in the present state of affairs, in solidarity with the aggressions that threaten us all. we're intervening for damaged property, but we're not doing it for the families that were killed or the people that were hurt. that is a great ... mr president, i would like to express our immediate solidarity with all the victims of the attacks in brussels on 22 march 2016 and their relatives. i believe that nothing can ever erase their suffering caused by acts that should never have taken place. but since these attacks, what do we do? i see that the priority, in almost all parts of europe, is the general surveillance, by technological means, of the whole of society. to date, however, no one has been able to establish whether and how this widespread surveillance, which has been in place for a long time, particularly in the anglo-saxon countries, has been able to prevent a single attack. at the same time, in belgium and france, the criminalisation of intentions rather than actions is being introduced. all this seems to me to pose a double democratic problem. first, in terms of the separation of powers, laws in many european countries give police powers without any control by the judiciary. in a democracy, however, the control of the executive by the judiciary is important. secondly, there is always the principle of proportionality: any restriction of freedom must be proportionate to the objective to be achieved: who can consider it reasonable to put society under general surveillance and to consider every citizen as a potential suspect? so what do you do? i think the priority for europeans is simple. most of the perpetrators of the attacks were known, so we have to collect information in a targeted way, share this information between services - we know that sharing information remains the weak link in the chain - and finally exploit the information, and for this we need human resources. i note that in most member states the police services are being weakened and are now exhausted.
one year after the two attacks in and around my flemish capital brussels, i regret to say that it has not stopped since. it won't stop on its own. oh, yes, we try to understand, we try to process, we're all just people. oh, yes, and we must all continue to stand together in solidarity. oh yes, "tous ensemble" is painted on the walls of the maalbeek metro station, just 200 to 300 metres from the european parliament in brussels. but this wasn't a natural disaster, this didn't happen to us. this was done to us by fellow humans. this was not inevitable. this should have been avoided and could have been avoided. all those many attacks in europe are almost exclusively committed in those countries which have in the past absorbed large muslim communities into their societies. there is a fatality in the case of terrorism which raises the question of whether there is a link between immigration and terrorism and whether that link is inevitable. we can only hope not. the perpetrators of brussels were convicted criminals who had been released. they took advantage of europe's open borders, of our freedom to travel and to transport goods. all of these are things that we're so proud of, but they're being used by terrorists against us. our way of life, which we are so proud of, but which is seen by a growing muslim community as unacceptable. what we have called homegrown terrorism since the london attacks is growing into a serious incompatibility with our culture on our soil. we have to act. we must stop this naive mass immigration, this stupid migration, and we must manage our society in a way that makes it clear to everyone, everywhere and at all times, that we are not going to adapt to this way of life. we must make it clear: adapt to our way of life or move. it's urgent, because the lesson of march 22nd last year is and will be: terrorism does not stop, not by itself.
almost a year ago, brussels was struck in the heart, the capital of europe: 35 dead, 324 wounded. let us first pay tribute to those victims today. it's exactly one month ago today that the last victim left the hospital. none of them have been able to claim compensation. that is why i strongly support the proposal by mr deprez, who is actually calling for the creation of a genuine solidarity fund for the victims of terrorism in europe. secondly, europe must be strong together. populists say: fold back into yourself, set boundaries. no, the war on terror is going to be won if we do it together. a genuine european intelligence service, better protection of the external borders, tackling the financing of terrorism, combating radicalisation. together we are strong. what are we waiting for? let's do it together.
member of the commission. – madam president, honourable members, i thank you for this very rich debate. the last two years in particular have been marked by too many attacks and by too many victims. our thoughts go out to them and to their loved ones. as minister borg said, last weekend we were with some of those victims, including some of the victims of the brussels attacks, i was struck again – as i am each time i have the privilege of meeting victims – by their bravery and by the power of their testimony. however, on this occasion i was also struck by the welcome that they gave to the just-adopted counter-terrorism directive, and, in particular, to the elements – not least those introduced by this house and reinforced by this house – on the rights of victims. they welcomed the fact that we were requiring member states to make sure that victims have access to the support they need not only immediately after an attack, but also for as long as necessary thereafter. that was an important piece of work that we did together. these attacks across europe display a common factor: many aspects of their preparation and execution are transnational. as a number of you have said, that is why it is so important that we work together to ensure the most appropriate response and increase our collective resilience. i would like to salute the commitment that this house has shown on security dossiers over the recent discussions. it is crucial that we maintain this momentum and continue to work for their rapid and effective implementation. as many of you have said, we have done a lot, but there is still a lot to do. many of you have mentioned the importance of information-sharing. as we discussed in plenary last month, i believe we are moving in the right direction; there is a clear increase in the use by member states of the schengen information system and the europol information system, but there is still a long way to go. i hope that this house will move quickly now to support the proposals that we have brought forward for the reform of the schengen information system, including, for the first time, a provision to oblige member states to share information on terrorism. step by step, we need to continue our work to build trust. improving information exchange is also at the heart of our work on interoperability that a number of you have mentioned. overcoming the current shortcomings in information management is a key priority for the commission. the process we initiated last year, with the strategy presented in april and the high level working group, will allow us to address these shortcomings in an inclusive and transparent way with you here and with the council. the group will present a final report in early may, and that will be a basis for further discussions with parliament and the council to look at how best to take this work forward. a number of you have also underlined the importance of operational police cooperation. the commission has encouraged member states to make use of the possibility of carrying out police checks in internal border areas. at the same time, and in answer to one of your colleagues, we are considering practical steps to strengthen police cooperation across internal borders. our objective is to enhance security within the schengen area, including internal border areas, as part of the work that we are doing to use schengen to get back to the normal functioning of schengen. but we will not defeat terrorism with databases and legal frameworks alone. we also need to continue our work, as many of you underlined, on implementation. we need to tackle radicalisation online and in our communities and prevent the hatemongers from inciting people, often young people, to go down the route of violence. we also need to continue to build our collective resilience and starve the terrorists, and others who seek to do us harm, of the means they use, whether that is arms, explosives or funding. as has been mentioned, while we are focusing on the threats related to terrorism today, quite properly we should not lose sight of the continuing work that we are doing to reinforce our security against serious and organised crime and the threats that come from cyber criminals and others who operate in cyberspace. in all of these challenges, there are no quick fixes. we need to continue to work to ensure the security of european citizens, recognising that this is a long-term challenge requiring the engagement of all of us. for our part, the commission will continue our efforts to build an effective and sustainable security union, and i sincerely hope that we can continue to count on your support in this work.
president-in-office of the council. madam president, i have to say that this is the third time i have been here in plenary but i think this was the most profound debate i have attended and it reminds us of the tragedy that took place a year ago, a tragedy that as many of the honourable members have referred to has literally hit the heart of the european union. i remember it was early in the us, not only remember them, but as we did today, constructive dialogue, continue to work to improve the security of our citizens and to live up to the expectations they have, that is, to offer that continent again a safe, peaceful atmosphere, although the challenge is great and some of the members, indeed the absolute majority of the members also contributed to the discussion and reacted to states, we will be able to achieve the delicate balance we need to strike between, of course, offering security to our citizens, and at the same time, preserving the freedoms offered by the treaties, interesting debates and there was a feeling of optimism and, at the same time, the will to continue working forward in this respect.
in writing. since 22 march 2016, the black day when thirty-two people were cowardly murdered in zaventem and maalbeek, crucial steps have been taken in tackling and preventing terrorism. that's the best way we can show our respect for the victims and their families. the counter-terrorism directive, adopted last month, is a crucial step in stepping up the joint approach to syrian fighters, terrorist networks and the drying up of their financial flows. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to cover the protection of personal data and the protection of privacy. closing external borders is also essential to enhance internal security. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of personal data. it is clear that the most effective approach is one of increased european cooperation in tackling and, in particular, preventing terrorism. that is a major task for europe.
on behalf of the s&d group. madam president, commissioner, tomorrow we will finally vote on a regulation which will make it possible to exclude from the european union market the so-called 'blood minerals'. it is true that we tend to talk about these 3 tgs in our jargon. what is it? tungsten, tin, tantalum and gold. all these minerals are used in our daily lives: in our mobile phones, in our tablets, in our refrigerators, in our cars, etc. the vast majority of them come from regions which are in conflict, but also from regions which do not respect human rights in the exploitation of these minerals. we have talked a lot about the great lakes region, but i think that this regulation also has the objective - and this is what is interesting - not to target specific regions, but to target all the regions where these human rights are not respected today. women and children exploited, raped, murdered on the altar of the mere profit of certain companies. the commission's initial proposal, you will agree, was based on a voluntary approach, which we in the european parliament found to be unambitious. thanks to the vote in this house, we were able to move these lines, the lines for a regulation that was going to be made compulsory, and i must also admit that even here in this parliament not everyone shared the idea of making this regulation compulsory. unfortunately this mandatory approach was not fully shared by the council either and during the trilogue, most of the council's demands were to actually reduce this mandatory approach and so we are now left with, it is true, a regulation that allows for a mandatory approach, but only on part of the supply chain. we have accepted this compromise because we think we have to move forward, but i think we have to be careful about the application of this rule. we certainly don't want it to allow companies to continue doing what they are doing today, which is killing people in conflict zones. you can count on us to be vigilant in the application of this regulation.
madam president, madam commissioner, i would first of all like to congratulate the commissioner and mrs arena on the result we have achieved today, even though i may be a little more nuanced in my judgment. in may 2015, our parliament passed an ambitious bill that required all companies using conflict minerals to ensure traceability throughout the supply chain. this represents - i remind you - 880,000 companies. the trilogue agreement is obviously an important step forward, but it will only be binding on a minority of companies - smelters, refiners and importers of crude ores and metals beyond a certain quantity. for others, it will be self-regulation, that is, their greater or lesser goodwill. i think the council has missed a remarkable opportunity to moralise behaviour which is too often scandalous, which fuels murderous conflicts and turns a blind eye to the disastrous human situation of child miners, victims of intolerant economic cynicism.

in writing. a new food scandal is plaguing the eu: the importation of rotten and unhealthy meat from brazil. twenty-one brazilian companies are under suspicion. four of them exported meat to the eu. this scandal is the tip of a huge iceberg. between 2000 and now, the rapid alert system for food and feed lists 258 notifications concerning brazilian meat products and 553 notifications concerning poultry. between 17 november 2016 and today, there are 24 serious reports of salmonella and 3 of e. coli in frozen meat and poultry. this scandal is linked to intensive farming techniques that cause massive deforestation in the cerrado, a savanna-like area where deforestation is 2 to 3 times faster than in the amazon. this also has a direct impact on human rights: 207 environmental activists were killed between 2010 and 2015. the former fvo carried out 3 audits in 2016 and found that meat exports did not meet european export standards. however, this did not affect trade in any way. the fvo is doing an incredible job, but there is insufficient political control over the follow-up of their conclusions and recommendations. that is why i am calling for an annual political debate on the recommendations of the fvo.
