a very important thing - start measuring the nox, also in car inspections. we are also strengthening - and we are doing so in the dalton report, which has also been well supported - type approval and market surveillance, and they cannot make an agency into a fetish. it is important, however, and i appeal to my colleagues, particularly those of the liberals and the epp, to think it over carefully. it's not about extra. it is about ensuring that we have an independent body that can carry out proper controls. and finally , never wasted a good crisis. we want to make sure that the car of the future that is being built in europe is a car that is completely clean, and we want to invest in that too. i want to thank all of you who have worked so hard on this committee.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the commission's proposals are implemented. the large differences between car emissions in the lab and those on the road were known and there were indications of possible fraud. yet no action was taken to tackle the structural problem forcefully. on the contrary, the introduction of effective field tests was significantly delayed. the conclusion is therefore not to be misunderstood: the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat the spread of the disease. this is particularly the case for vulnerable road users and children. poor air quality is choking citizens to death and making them sick. in the eu, 75 000 people die prematurely each year from nitrogen dioxide alone, mainly from polluting diesel cars. however, the technology is already available to achieve the proposed standards today, also in real driving conditions and without negative impact on co2 emissions. it is incomprehensible, it is unacceptable that some member states and the car industry are still on the brakes. their delaying maneuvers must stop. the protection of human health and the environment is a shared social responsibility. today we have a unique opportunity to show our citizens that we will not let them down and that we will put an end to the structural failure that 'dieselgate' has exposed. in this specific context, strong market surveillance offers the best guarantees to exclude political influence. i shall vote in favour of the agency in the future, both in the report by mr gieseke and mr gerbrandy and in the report on type-approval. i hope that parliament will not let this opportunity pass. failure to learn from 'dieselgate' would simply be unacceptable and would further undermine the credibility of the european union.

vice-president of the commission. – mr president, your endorsement of the political agreement will be an important step in enabling cross-border investments, in providing companies with easier access to capital markets and in delivering better and more concise information to investors. the reform of the prospectus rules delivers on a key element of the capital markets union action plan. after 12 years of a harmonised set of prospectus rules, it was time to make the eu prospectus fit for the capital markets union. in preparing the 2016 prospectus reform we have listened to issuers, investors and market operators and decided to go much further than the last reform that took place in 2010. the new rules that you will hopefully agree to will introduce an entirely new eu growth prospectus, a higher threshold while member states can rely on local listing rules only, a new registration document for frequent issuers and enormous simplification for secondary issuances. let me briefly present the key achievements introduced by the new prospectus regulation. the eu growth prospectus is designed as a key ingredient for developing the sme growth markets introduced by mifid ii. both smes and mid-caps can use this simple prospectus to raise capital on sme growth markets. many well-functioning ‘junior’ markets of today can become the sme growth markets of the future. the new prospectus threshold will be almost doubled to eur 8 million, compared to the previous eur 5 million. for offerings below that threshold, member states can rely on local listing rules issued by the operators of junior growth markets, which can support the development of local or regional stock exchanges for small companies. issuers already admitted to the regulated markets and sme growth markets will benefit from a lighter prospectus for their ‘follow-up’ – or secondary – issuances. the alleviations for follow-up issuances reflect the fact that these companies are well-known in the market. about 70% of all equity prospectuses approved annually concern follow-up, so-called ‘secondary issuances’. our impact assessment estimates potential savings for well—known issuers to amount to no less than eur 130 million annually. finally, the introduction of a universal registration document (urd) for frequent issuers will trigger a ‘fast track’ approval of five days, instead of the statutory limit of 10 days. the urd, therefore, has the potential to decrease approval times considerably and allow issuers to seize market windows at short notice. mr president, honourable members, i would like to thank you once again for the excellent work done on this file.
the european parliament has called on the commission to take the necessary steps to ensure that the eu's financial system is in a position to meet the challenges of the future. we can either react with fear or we can take this wake-up call and shift two gears in the building of a european capital markets union. europe needs investment. but first, the investment climate must be radically improved. the new prospectus legislation is an important piece of this puzzle. european companies are still too dependent on bank financing today. only 20% of them are taking money from investors. this is a serious under-utilisation of the growth potential that additional resources provide through the capital markets. the capital market is often too big a step for smes in particular. a major threshold is the high cost of a prospectus. under the current rules, prospectuses have become telephone directories in which even potential investors cannot find their way. the new legislation will make the drawing up of a prospectus much easier and cheaper. in fact, expenditure below 1 million will be exempt altogether. for smes, mid-caps in sme growth markets and certain smaller spenders up to 20 million, as already mentioned, there will be a lighter eu growth prospectus. we help promising companies climb the financial ladder and raise the capital they need to grow. the prospectus regulation offers a unique opportunity to make it much easier for smes and growing companies to raise funds. but that is far from our work on a european capital markets union. the proposal for more efficient insolvency and restructuring procedures, for which i am responsible in the econ committee, must also be tackled with the same unanimity and speed. moreover, in the quest for a deeper, genuine european capital market, the discussion of a pan-european regulator will sooner or later be inevitable. but let's take a first horde tomorrow and vote on more efficient prospectus rules.

statements by several european leaders who show that there are alternatives to ever more europe. we need to follow the path to a decentralised confederal europe. there simply is no support for more on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, i have the feeling that it was a very sad moment on wednesday of last week when the british ambassador gave his letter to president tusk. that was my feeling anyway: a very sad moment. it is true, naturally, that the relationship between britain and europe was never an easy relationship, let us recognise that. it was never a love affair and certainly not a question of wild passion. i think it was a little bit like a marriage of convenience, if i can use that term. it was already clear, dear colleagues, from the beginning. in the 1950s britain decided against membership of the european coal and steel community. attlee and labour did not want it, and it was churchill and the tories who were in favour, it is good to recall this. and in 1955 at the start of the common market, britain walked away from the negotiating table. in the early years of the union it was the british prime minister macmillan who looked at the continent with nothing less than suspicion. what were they cooking up there in brussels, were they really discussing coal and steel and customs union, or were they also talking politics in brussels, plotting on foreign policy? oh, god forbid, defence matters even! so british prime minister macmillan wrote to his foreign minister, and, i quote, i have the quote here: ‘for the first time since napoleon the major continental powers are united in a positive economic grouping, but considerable political aspects’, and to his own surprise, macmillan had to admit this new experiment, and i quote further, ‘was not directed against britain.’ so when britain finally joined the european union in 1973 after, as we all know, several blockades by general de gaulle, the headlines were festive. you have to read all the british press in 1973, it was a great day for britain to join the european union. let us be honest about this, it was only a short honeymoon, as we know, because margaret thatcher asked for her money back and her successor john major called the euro, and i quote again, ‘a currency as strange as a rain dance, with the same impotence’. well, i have to tell you that the pound slipping against the euro, as we see today was not exactly what major expected at that moment. but all the rest, let us be honest with each other, is history. perhaps let us recognise that it was maybe impossible to unite great britain with the continent, and naive maybe to reconcile the legal system of napoleon with the common law of the british empire, and perhaps it was never meant to be. (applause)but, and this is important – and i hope you are applauding this also – our predecessors should never be blamed for having tried, because it is important in politics, as it is in life, to try new partnerships, new horizons, to reach out to the other, to the other side of the channel. i am also convinced and 100% sure about one thing: that one day or another, dear colleagues, there will be a young man or a young woman who will try again, who will lead britain into the european family once again. (applause)and a young generation that will see brexit for what it really is: a catfight in the conservative party that got out of hand. a loss of time, a waste of energy, and i think, a stupidity. although i continue to think that brexit is a sad and regrettable event, i also believe it is important to remember something. remember what britain and europe in these more than 40 years have achieved together. it is true, we may not have had the most passionate relationship, but it was not a failure either, not for europe, and certainly not for britain and the british. let us not forget, britain entered the union as the sick man of europe, and thanks to the single market, came out the other side. europe also made britain punch above its weight in terms of geopolitics, as in the heyday of the british empire. and we, from all sides, must pay tribute to britain, to britain’s immense contributions as a staunch and unmatched defender of free markets and civil liberties. and thank you for that because as a liberal, i will miss that in the future. colleagues, within a few weeks we will start the process of separation. and i think, mr juncker and mr barnier, the goal must be to have a new and stable relationship and a deep and comprehensive partnership and association between the uk and the eu that certainly will be very different, as we all know, from membership. in this new venture let us always remember one thing. our common bonds, our common culture, our common and shared values, our joint heritage, our history. and let us never forget that together we in fact belong to the same great european civilisation, from the atlantic port of bristol, i go as far as to the banks of the mighty river volga; but maybe that is a little too far for the moment. but let us be honest, and this will be my final point. brexit is not only about brexit. brexit has to be also about our capacity for a rebirth of our european project, because let us recognise that brexit did not happen by accident. even though since brexit i see what i call a change for the good in the mood of the public, let us not fool ourselves: europe is not yet rescued and europe has not yet recovered from the crisis. europe is still in need of change, i think in need of radical change: change towards a real union, an effective union based on values and based on the real interests of our citizens. and a union also – and i want to conclude with this – that stands up against autocrats. autocrats will close down their universities, to give one example. (applause) autocrats will throw journalists into jail, as is happening today. autocrats will make corruption their trademark. and yesterday, as we all have seen, beyond any humanity, autocrats again bombed innocent women and children with chemical weapons in syria, to give the nastiest example. so in these negotiations which will have to start in the coming weeks, let us never forget why our founding fathers – british and other europeans alike – launched this european project. there are three words: freedom, justice and peace – these are three great things that are worth fighting for. (applause)
on behalf of the verts/ale group. – mr president, prime minister may, you inherited a situation you did not create, yet you had a choice. and by choosing the hardest form of brexit you chose the most extreme interpretation of the referendum. in doing so, you encouraged all those on the continent as well as in the uk from the fringes of the political landscape and the benches of your own governments who have made grandstanding and threatening their brand of politics. on the one hand, we have to all those who call for making britain the world champion of social and tax dumping, or even for starting a war with spain. on the other hand, we have those who say we should punish the united kingdom. have the last 70 years not told them anything? mrs may, by your own choice you dug yourself a hole of contradictions. how can you have a hard brexit without having a hard border in ireland? how can you – to use your own words – have the freest possible trade in goods and services between britain and the eu while you take the uk out of the single market, which allows precisely that? but above all, how can you reconcile a hard brexit with your own stated desire of a more united uk and our claim to represent every person in the united kingdom, including the large number of people, especially the young generation who voted in favour of remaining in the european union? resolving those contradictions, so as to minimise damage to our citizens must be the objective of the negotiations to come. if we want common sense and the general interest to prevail we must ignore those who shout and posture. i agree with you, mrs may, when you say you want to build a stronger, fairer, better britain. achieving stronger, fairer and better societies is a goal that many share in this chamber. delivering this requires us to face the trumps and the putins of this world, to tackle climate change, to fight terrorism and organised crime, to find common responses to the global migration challenge, to curb corporate power. and who can believe, who seriously can believe when eu citizens altogether represent 7% of the global population on 2% of the land, that any – i say any – of our member states, including the largest is better equipped to faces challenges on its own? mrs may, like it or not, we are in this together. taking back control, being recognised as global players, requires all europeans, including britons, to act together. there is no such thing as absolute sovereignty. in the 21st century we can only reconquer sovereignty for democracies by sharing it. mrs may, you want to build a britain your children and grandchildren are proud to call home. let me remind you that, as we speak, many of your own citizens are proud to call not just britain but europe home. let us not let them down.
ladies and gentlemen of the eu majority in this parliament: please stop the emotional, half-hysterical act with an air of moral superiority. we as a parliament should not pretend that we can veto this. the only people who had to make a political decision in this matter were the people of the united kingdom, and there are many sympathizers within the european union for what britain is doing now. everything else is a technical matter. if we could do something useful here, instead of all sorts of suggestions of revenge or all sorts of proposals for the permanent linking of free trade to open immigration borders, it is to express our wish, our political wish, to treat the united kingdom without rancor as a privileged partner and an exceptionally good friend.
on behalf of the verts/ale group. – mr president, if we want to get to a sensible accommodation, mr farage’s speech is precisely the kind of nonsense that we should just ignore. i just want to say that brexit is a lose-lose game. i would have much preferred for us to stay together. l’union fait la force, we say in belgium, and that remains the case today. but there is one thing where i believe no compromise is possible, and that is the only thing that really annoyed me in mrs may’s letter. it concerns security cooperation. security cooperation is an area where we cannot let lose-lose logic prevail. it is in the interest of the safety and security of all our citizens, british or otherwise, to cooperate deeply in matters of security, and what we cannot accept is that any other kind of consideration – trade, commercial, whatever – stands in the way. there is no trade-off here. it concerns the lives of men and women.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, i find it fantastic that mr farage is using article 217 of the treaty on the association agreement. now that he is leaving, he starts to read the treaty and to find the treaty so good. it is fantastic to see this enormous positive development, if i may say so. that said, what is clear in this debate is ‘let us put the citizens first’, and it does not make any difference whether they are eu citizens or uk citizens. we will be firm, we need to be firm to defend the unity and the interest of the european union towards the uk authorities, but my appeal here in the house today is to be open, positive and generous towards uk citizens, because many of them, millions of them, want to keep their relationship, their link, their identity on the european level. and last but not least, my appeal also to the house is that, when we vote within a few minutes, we have a huge majority and almost unity in this house. it is key to do that, to have a united european parliament, together with the eu negotiator and with the european council, and i hope for a strong vote within a few moments.
in writing. after nine months of gestation, the uk government finally decides to trigger article 50, officially opening negotiations with the european union. in the committee on employment and social affairs we have particularly studied the question of brexit from the point of view of the free movement of persons. no fewer than 700 acts of eu law concern employment policy, social policy or the free movement of workers and all of them, without exception, will be affected by brexit. in a number of cases, transitional measures will be necessary to protect citizens. this request for divorce should also encourage us to revise the directive on the posting of workers as soon as possible, in order to find an appropriate solution for the union of 27. finally, the european social fund, as well as the european fund for the most deprived and the employment and social innovation programme, are likely to be affected by brexit. it is essential that we ensure that the budget allocated to this is sufficient, and that is despite the fact that the united kingdom is leaving. brexit must be transformed into an opportunity to revive and improve the european project.
in writing. i do not like the uk leaving the eu, but now that the process has started, we must move forward in a constructive way. today the european parliament is setting its negotiating position on brexit. i believe that the interests of the citizens must be at the centre of this. no one benefits from a hard brexit: the uk is not, but neither is the eu. at the same time, for the eu-27, brexit must be a shared choice for a social, sustainable and fair european union. the eu-27 must unite around a sustainable and social future. the brexit can thus lead to greater consensus on our common values. of course, we cannot be naive. the uk will try to pull the best deal out of the fire. however, it should be clear from the outset that there can be no cherry-picking: you cannot want to keep all the benefits of membership and yet leave the union. we must be very clear that the union will not accept the creation of a fiscal paradise with ecodumping and social dumping on our borders. the eu's role in the negotiations on brexit must therefore be to ensure that the climate agreement is respected and that social and fiscal standards are met.
in writing. from 15 june 2017, eu citizens will no longer pay extra charges when using their mobile phone outside their country. this victory, which we have won after a long battle with the operators, will bring down the telephone bills of millions of cross-border workers and tens of millions of european holidaymakers. however, to ensure that these costs for the individual are eliminated and that operators do not try to catch up elsewhere, it was also necessary to reduce wholesale roaming prices, i.e. the cost that telecom operators charge each other when the customer uses the internet abroad. the decision voted on today in the european parliament goes in that direction. the agreement gives the citizen a double benefit. on the one hand, he is assured that he will not pay any extra charges for using his mobile phone outside his country (provided that he does not use the service excessively, a threshold yet to be set). but that's not all. these lower data caps will allow european consumers to access more audiovisual content when travelling outside their borders.
those who know me know that i have always been a supporter of a short-stay visa exemption for citizens of ukraine. the visa liberalisation action plan has been the engine of far-reaching reforms. not least in the fight against corruption. i myself stood on maidan square in kiev several times in late 2013, early 2014, alongside a crowd of people who yearned for a society without corruption, for respect for the rule of law. we must recognize that a great deal of effort has been made, but that there is still a long way to go. and, unfortunately, visas are still being cheated. in kiev, shadow companies continue to operate selling visas, advertising openly on the internet and circumventing the schengen rules. they are shifting their scope to long-term visas for the purpose of visa exemption. i call on our ukrainian friends to make a real effort to combat such evil practices. the european union must also cooperate with all our member states and the european commission to ensure that the rules of the game are respected for all and to put an end to visa fraud.
i am sorry that certain political groups did not want to wait for the first evaluation by the commission. that said, i am not at all happy with the content of the resolution by rapporteur moraes. i have therefore worked with the epp group to propose a more positive alternative. let me emphasize a few points. firstly, transatlantic data transmission is essential to our economy, trade and security. no one can deny this, and so please let us not put it aside in this debate. the commission has also been asked to provide information on the implementation of the privacy shield in the united states. second, with the privacy shield, we have achieved a level of data protection that has never been achieved before. a giant step in the right direction was taken. the rights and legal protection of eu citizens in the transfer of data to the us are improving dramatically. i also believe that the commission should always strive for the highest possible standard of data protection, but some political groups and members of parliament still feel that a copy of the european rules is sufficient. the united states, however, is an independent nation. although it is an important strategic ally of the eu, we must not forget that it is not a member of the union. i and my delegation strongly believe that having an operational privacy shield is in the best interests of our businesses, the european economy and the privacy of eu citizens.
the previous resolution on human rights violations in russia was adopted just four months ago. then and now the n-va delegation voted with conviction 'for'. we support the political and prisoners of conscience, persecuted journalists and human rights defenders in russia and the occupied territories such as crimea. officially, free speech is a constitutional right in russia. however, once you gain followers and thus become dangerous in the eyes of the regime, the law abruptly stops. you become a target of intimidation and persecution. alexei navalny, boris nemtsov, denis voronenkov, nikolai gorokhov, vladimir kara-murza, aleksandr litvinenko... they are the known victims of a regime that is increasingly tolerating less and less free speech, press freedom or opposition. the mass arrests during the 26 march demonstrations are disturbing. the eu must continue to send the message to the kremlin with one voice that respect for human rights and international agreements is a prerequisite for becoming a reliable partner again.
if i'm not mistaken, calling president lukashenko a dictator is actually a compliment: he is a despot, he is a tyrant. listen to what this stepfather of the belarusian nation said about the work of some journalists. i quote: if any of those journalists or politicians in my country were to speak like that, i would personally tear out their tongues. this is the tyranny of this tyranny. for years, he's been preoccupied with his cult of personality and his own power. he has narrowed the political playing field, he has narrowed the middle ground, and he has pushed aside anyone who could offer a political alternative. one political scientist summed it up this way: in belarus, you don't have to understand who is guilty or innocent. there are no laws, no courts. lukashenko himself is the law and the court. he judges, but his decisions are proven wrong. in the end, there is no fear and no conscience. lukashenko himself is the embodiment of fear and lack of conscience. we must not be indifferent. the eu must continue to give its full support to the democratic forces, defenders of human rights and fundamental freedoms in belarus europe's last dictatorship .
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, i would like to ask prime minister orban whether he remembers the first time that we met. mr orban, it was in a hotel in budapest in december 1989. i do not remember its name, but i remember our meeting because before it i had a meeting with sdsz, the old liberal party, and then i asked you, as the leader of fidesz, why you were not with your mother party in the meeting room and you explained why this was. i remember that it was just a few months before the successful participation of fidesz in the first free elections in hungary. by the way, at that time you were helped by george soros – i remember that – and had a programme at that time that was more or less a little bit more progressive, i should say, than the programme of the sdsz at that time. i would say that it was more social liberal and a little bit like emmanuel macron at that time. i would say that you were the emmanuel macron of hungary in 1989. i do not think that emmanuel macron will be pleased with what i have said today. i did not say it in french so i hope that not much will be made of it. but let us be honest: a lot has changed since 1990 and 1989 when we saw each other. you changed. you dumped your democratic principles and, in a certain way, you say openly that you want to create, not a liberal democracy, but that you want to have an illiberal state. at the moment the list of what you have done is a long one: harassing ngos, chasing critical media away, building walls, your attempt to reinstate the death penalty in your country, even when this is not possible based on our treaties, and now you have decided to close down a university. i would ask you how far will you go? not very courageous, i should say, and certainly not in line with a politician based on principles. so my question to you is to ask whether you think that it is time to make a choice again, as you made that choice when you became, after being a liberal democrat, a little bit nationalist and a conservative? is it not the time to ask yourself in your soul how you wish to be remembered in the future? do you want to be remembered as somebody who liberated your country, hungary, from communism – and you did that – or do you want to be commemorated as an eternal enemy of our open european democratic society? that is the choice that you have to make now. prime minister of hungary. thank you, mr president. i think it's only fitting that i answer some important questions. first of all, i want to thank you all for this debate. thank you for your attention to hungary, and thank you for your opinions. those who have publicly attacked hungary, the hungarian people, and the decisions they have made here, well, i would like to thank those representatives in particular. thank you for your solidarity. there were hungarian commentators. i don't want to qualify the attitude of hungarians here in brussels attacking their country, i believe that these debates should be held at home. i would like to say to mr verhofstadt that i have the impression that you are trying to tell the hungarians what to do. please leave this to the hungarian electorate, they can decide this every four years. i would like to thank vice-president timmermans for the speech he gave. thank you very much for offering an open and substantive dialogue, we are ready to discuss all the questions you have raised and i see that many of them will be easily resolved. i am unfortunately obliged to respond to my colleague mr pittella, because i feel that he has treated me unfairly in taking his remarks very harshly. mr. deputy, allow me to correct one of your thoughts: migrants do not want to go to hungary. the migrants want to go through hungary to austria, germany and sweden. we're actually protecting austria, sweden, and germany. we are actually observing the schengen agreement. and i find it strange that you, as a member of the european parliament, attack hungary because hungary is complying with the schengen treaty, not primarily for its own sake, but for the sake of the germans, the swedes and the austrians. we hungarians think that we deserve recognition rather than attack. the comments that are being made in the european people's party to try to create resistance to my party, i have to tell you that this is a if i hurt someone, i apologize, we know this well , this is an old communist tactic. you want to salem the european people's party. they're probably trying to sabotage it so they can be stronger here. we know this, this is how the communists destroyed democracy in hungary. i would recommend to the european people's party not to accept this solution. ladies and gentlemen, our position on immigration is clear. illegal immigration must be stopped, the screening of refugees and migrants must be done outside the european union, and people in trouble must be brought to help where there is trouble, not brought into the european union. who doesn't make the transfers? my dear fellow pittella, less than 10% of the population has been relocated. it wasn't hungary that didn't participate, it was only a thousand people. 10% of the frame size was moved. it is clear that the policy of relocation, of mandatory quotas, has failed. it is unnecessary to force it, because those states which do not openly oppose us do not enforce it either. this was a bad idea. if a bad policy idea turns out to be a failure, then it's worth stopping and looking for another solution. i have to say a word about the money. look, the money that hungary gets from the european union is not charity. cohesion policy is good for everyone. every single member state of the european union benefits. you make money on it, the people who give you money make money on it. not a few. let's talk about this openly. net contributors are the beneficiaries of cohesion policy. your officers talk about it openly. they also give numbers, i won't quote them, of what percentage of the money you give us goes back to your country. i am convinced that this cohesion policy is the right thing to do. we have abolished tariffs, opened our markets and allowed the free flow of capital, when we were so poor after communism, so short of capital as church mice. i am convinced that without cohesion policy there can be no fair competition. you cannot ask us, honourable members, to keep our mouths shut, and you cannot talk to us as if you were giving us gifts and we were your bought and paid for. we cannot accept this position, hungary must reject it. as for hungary being some kind of authoritarian political system, and thanks to me personally, although obviously it's not about me, but about ten million hungarians, i would like to inform you that i spent 16 years in opposition in parliament and i'm just starting my 12th year in government. and i'm one of the few european executives who, when he lost, didn't go into business, didn't sit on high-paying boards, didn't give guest lectures for huge sums of money. when we lost, my party and i personally, we stayed in politics and took on the opposition debates and fought and won back the people's trust. to accuse such a country and such a ruling party of authoritarian regimes and to mention authoritarian countries in relation to us, i think it's simply not fair and it's not fair. ladies and gentlemen, i think many things, i think many things about george soros, but i don't think that he would ever in his life give a scholarship to anyone because he thought that he would buy their opinion for life. so i have to reject the assumptions that are made. and finally, i have to say a word about illiberal democracy, because you mentioned it. the thing is, in central europe, they think that if the liberals don't win or they're not part of the government, then there's no democracy. we've had enough of this. the truth is, we think that if the liberals don't win, we can still have democracy. illiberal democracy is when liberals don't win. thank you for your kind attention.

only thing the central european university is asking is to be left alone to do what it does well. i come from a country where, for centuries, hungarian scholars came to study and also to enrich my country. we live in a european union where, through the erasmus programme, the cross-fertilisation of young minds across the european union is one of the biggest assets of this european union and one of the biggest success stories. we should cherish this academic freedom, this exchange of ideas, and this way of promoting a vision of the world that is open to other views. with regard to other views, i fully respect different visions and opinions about the future of the european union. everybody is entitled to his own opinion, but not to his own facts. i know we live in a time when very on behalf of the alde group. – madam president, i am not going to repeat what has been said on the situation in turkey; i think that we have a common assessment, commissioner. what is more important is what you have asked us: what our view is in the light of the gymnich meeting. together with mr weber, i have published a common op-ed article, you could say, on that in a number of newspapers, because i think it is critical now to go into a new cooperation and to make a new proposal to turkey. i think that we have to stop accession negotiations. in fact, they are no longer accession negotiations; they are in fact suspended – not formally suspended, but, in fact, suspended. the only thing we are doing is still paying eur 650 million every year for the pre—accession process, and it does not help these people in turkey, journalists, or civil society in general. on the contrary, i think that continuing this so-called not formally suspended ‘accession’ is a cover-up that even makes it possible for some people in the turkish authorities to further clamp down on civil society, on journalists, on the opposition, and so on. our proposal is to reconsider that and ask: why not do what is in the treaty? the treaty says that we can make association agreements with neighbourhood countries. why not make a new association agreement and propose to turkey such a new association agreement where you have two chapters: one chapter on economics, trade, customs union (we could propose an offer of an upgraded customs union, even further than what is on the table now); and at the same time, the second track, if on the turkish side they open civil society, they agree to restore democracy in a certain sense. i do not want to call it a ‘helsinki process’ and to make a comparison to the strategy we followed with the soviet union (your remember that), but, in a certain way, that is what it is: a two—track approach. we should then use the eur 640 or 650 million to help civil society there as a part of such a new strategy. that seems to me the new – even broadly supported – way forward that you could give as a message to the gymnich meeting on saturday.

in writing. this afternoon, the european parliament will vote in favour of binding rules for textile suppliers and the clothing sector. the respect of workers' rights by manufacturers must become an obligation if products are to be sold in our territory. textile workers, often young women or children, work at hellish speeds. in bangladesh, 47.5% of workers work between 60 and 98 hours per week, 75% more than 48 hours per week. discrimination based on sex or age is widespread. more than a quarter of workers are paid less than €50 a month, below the national minimum wage. europe cannot remain blind and deaf to these human rights violations. in concluding its trade agreements, it must make any trade agreement conditional on respect for the rights of workers. we can weld clothes; you can't buy the workers' rights or the human rights of those who make them!
in writing. i invite you to support this report. the text put to the vote encourages the eib to continue its efforts to overcome the investment deficit, market failures and sectoral shortcomings and to invest in projects and operations with real added value in order to achieve a stronger european cohesion in economic, social and territorial terms, a more favourable investment climate, a higher employment rate and the restoration of sustainable growth throughout the union. it also recalls that supporting economic recovery, sustainable growth and strengthening cohesion is a key objective and that the eib needs to better anticipate structural challenges, in particular those related to the reindustrialisation of europe and the digital knowledge-based economy, in order to generate new economic opportunities and innovation and to encourage the development of a circular economy and better use of renewable energy, in line with environmental, climate and energy objectives.
madam president, madam commissioner, i thank you for this important tool, which says something about the europe we want. in education, the role of the european union is to build bridges between the different actors and policies, whether social, economic, youth or education, to show that paths are possible. member states have agreed to establish certifications which refer to the european framework. we need to organise the portability of qualifications in europe so that they respond to the realities and developments in society. the european certification framework is a crucial instrument for improving the employability and mobility of learners. there are as many relevant pathways as there are individuals. it is essential that the courses offered are flexible and that training is accessible in order to improve skills and progress towards a qualification, towards certification, i.e. recognition of acquired skills. the issue of mobility is important, especially for young people and crossborder regions, who are familiar with the issue of recognition of skills and qualifications. the revision of the common eu-wide certification framework is intended to link the certification systems of the various member states. this work must be able to draw on the reinvigorated social dialogue in order to establish a better match between qualifications and the labour market and to integrate the issues of mobility and lifelong learning.
i am not going to go into the details of the commission's proposals. every day, large amounts of food are lost in the various links of the food chain. the numbers are indeed mind-boggling: globally, 1.3 billion tons of food is lost every year, about a third of all food production. in the eu, an estimated 88 million tonnes of food is lost every year, equivalent to 173 kg per person per year. it is obvious that we must put a stop to this. food waste is bad for people, the environment and the economy. moreover, food waste is at odds with our ambition to move to a circular economy, where we are just fully committed to waste prevention and we try to reuse or recycle as much of our precious raw materials as possible. i therefore support the priorities set out in this resolution: halve food loss by 2030, develop a concrete action plan to achieve this goal, and make a strong commitment to awareness and prevention. the key to success is undoubtedly the involvement of all players in the food chain and a key role for regional and local authorities to ensure that tailor-made products are delivered. flanders, the nation i represent here, is taking the lead and has already developed its own action plan to reduce food loss by 15% by 2020. all actors, from farmer to board, are represented in a chain of consultation. many interesting initiatives are emerging, such as the chefs charter of horeca flanders or the green vent scan. the donation of food is being facilitated digitally through online platforms and apps. the extension of the 0% vat rate for the distribution of unsold foodstuffs also has a positive effect. consumers are also given tips, information is given on the expiry dates and 'similar' fruit and vegetables are back on the shelves. the compulsory collection and recycling of bio-waste and the encouragement of home composting are also important in the fight against food waste. a recent visit with some colleagues to the ecowerf composting plant in leuven shows the enormous potential in this respect. in short: the fight against food waste is a matter for everyone and governments should set a good example. let's work together on that.
for the ecr group the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the situation in the former yugoslavia is not further worsened. it is blamed on the difficulty of filling those places because of insufficient cooperation from the member states, but reports from the commission show that greece and italy are failing to organise the flow. for example, there is a huge backlog of registrations in italy. so it is unfair to point the finger only at the north. furthermore, it is not true that hardly any asylum seekers were relocated. most asylum seekers who entered europe continued their journey to northern and western europe. the dublin rules on reception and registration in the first member state of arrival, from which one can be subsequently relocated, are hardly respected in practice. the n-va insists that the external borders must be closed first, otherwise the vacuum cleaner will be opened. in concrete terms, we must return every asylum seeker who illegally enters the eu with the help of human smugglers to a safe place in the region of origin. as long as the eu continues to reward illegal arrivals with papers, families will continue to risk their lives on the road. we must therefore move to a system of legal entry, coupled with a strict maximum limit.

the european commission's reflection note contains some interesting starting points. the commission is often wrongly, but often rightly, accused of being concerned only with liberal ideas. it is very important that limits be set now. it is interesting that social and environmental standards should be placed on an equal footing with economic principles. it is also interesting that we need to invest much more in the european union to make our people resilient and to enable a fair distribution of wealth. but, commissioner, we must be careful not to write only interesting notes. recent surveys show that the population remains pro-european but raises serious questions about the solutions offered by europe. you gotta butter the fish. in other words: we must re-examine our trade agreements, our investment policy and our fiscal policy within the european union.
for years, economists, multinationals, bankers, politicians and the media have overstated and exaggerated the benefits of globalization while downplaying its disadvantages. the commission now acknowledges on half a page that globalisation also has negative effects, but then concludes that there is no turning back. what is going wrong in european industry is being covered up by another globalisation fund, which is supposed to mask the pain of the most alarming factory closures. in this global world there is always someone who wants or has to sell his labour somewhere for less money and less social protection. hyper-globalization is leading to an economy where products are made by children's hands in the east and consumed by the beneficiaries in the west. as a result: the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat the problem of unemployment. we need a new economy that does not isolate itself but also does not naively exploit the opportunities of the free market. in short, an economy for our own european people.
mr president, in 2011, an oecd study indicated that globalisation was creating inequalities. today, in 2017, i thank the commission for having effectively recognised these inequalities. however, the commission's concrete response does not go in the direction of better managing globalisation. on the contrary, it is a boost to a dozen or so free trade agreements built on the global model of the years before the 2008 crisis. there is a certain kind of inconsistency between your thoughts and your actions. the commission, it seems to me, is a prisoner of the ideology of maximum openness, while others here in this house are locked into total closure. in my view, both of these paths are dangerous because they destroy well-being. yet there are ways to control this globalization. if you are short of ideas, i will give you some: for example, tax conditionality, the fight against dumping, with the mandatory sustainable development chapter with sanctions, or the protection of investors, but not against the public forces. today, all these ideas are not being put into practice by the commission and we are therefore disappointed.
vice-president of the commission. – mr president, i would like to thank members very much for a very wide—ranging discussion. it was good that members brought many different dimensions to the discussion. first, let me say a couple of words about the nature of the paper. this is not an action plan. this is a reflection paper, one of five reflection papers intended to promote wide discussion, not only here but also in member states, amongst the stakeholders – national parliaments, governments and also ngos and various organisations. we need to give direction to our policies on how to shape globalisation. my second point is that the eu itself is an example of rules—based globalisation. we are a product of globalisation. we deliberately decided to create a borderless europe where people – students, the labour force, whoever – can travel across borders without any difficulty. we have created erasmus, the single market – which is, by the way, a free-trade agreement – and common action towards climate policy, and we are about to create the circular economy regulatory environment. so this is what globalisation is in europe, and we should take this elsewhere as well, shaping globalisation so that it is rules-based and based on values we believe in. many members rightly stated that we should not send out the message that the eu, parliament or the commission can address all the negative effects. in fact, many of the tools which need to be used are in the hands of the member states. education and social policies, for instance, are in the hands of member states. i will give you a couple of concrete examples of what member states can do. we have to strengthen the resilience of individuals and societies. there, for instance, the quality of education and the quality of teacher training is of the utmost importance. the better quality education we have, the better prepared our citizens are for changes. i like the word ‘resilience’ because it means that, whatever happens, we are resilient in facing challenges and we are strong enough and innovative enough to harness the negative effects of globalisation. many members, quite rightly, raised the issue of trade policy. this is part of globalisation. globalisation is not only about trade, but trade is part of it. for instance, the eu—korea trade agreement was agreed some five or six years ago, and since then our trade has increased by eur 15 billion euros. as we know that eur 1 billion of additional trade or exports support roughly 14 000 new jobs, increasing trade to korea has supported some 200 000 jobs in europe. i will put this figure in a local context. the country i know best is typically considered to have a nordic high—cost welfare society. regardless of high costs and a welfare-society structure, a well—known european car manufacturer established a factory in finland. this was possible because of robotisation. i do not know which part of the world the robots’ components came from, but nevertheless robots made it possible to produce cars in a high—cost welfare society. the town in which the factory is situated is the town with the highest number of robots per capita, and it is the very same town that has the highest number of vacancies per capita. so this is another example of globalisation. also, thanks to our trade agreement between the eu and korea, those cars manufactured in a high—cost welfare society can be sold to korea. that is just one example. another example come from sweden. you may remember that when the car manufacturer saab went bankrupt, some five years ago, it was a big shock to the town of trollhättan, where roughly 3 000 people lost their jobs. since then, labour unions, together with employers’ organisations, public authorities and the private sector have worked together, and today three out of four people who lost their jobs have found a new job and the unemployment rate is lower than it was before the bankruptcy. thanks to active policies and action at local level, they have managed to create new opportunities for citizens. my final point is that we must be a shaper of globalisation. i very much liked it when some colleagues said that we should be not a rule-taker but a rule-maker. that is why the commission has proposed an external investment plan, because we want to influence sustainable development in our developing partner countries, for instance in african countries. the better we manage to support inclusive economic growth in africa, the less reason there is for irregular migration and, of course, quality of life and fairness will increase in those countries. i hope that you will bring this discussion to your constituencies, promote a forward—looking discussion on globalisation and try to identify which policies are in the hands of the member states or local authorities and which policies are in our hands here, as european legislators and decision makers, which should be taken forward in order to shape globalisation in a positive manner.
europe has one of the most open economies in the world, and trade with our partners has created a lot of prosperity and jobs. but elsewhere in the world, our companies have been pushed to the limit too many times. we can't afford to be naive. the time has come to put an end to the excesses of wild free trade and one-way traffic: not with closed borders, but mainly through a level playing field and protecting our crown jewels. we cannot just let our power stations, airports and telephone networks fall into the hands of aggressive state-owned companies from outside the eu. the eu must act on plans supported by germany, france and italy to monitor any possible outside investment in european strategic sectors and to halt it if necessary. globalisation also means shipping goods from one continent to another (and sometimes back). it may be cheaper now, but will it be cheaper if we take into account the full environmental cost? unlike in europe (through ets and non-ets), this impact is much less or not accounted for in other continents. this must be urgently brought to the table, even if others in the world trade organization do not want to join.
in writing. it is positive that the european commission is now openly admitting in the discussion note on 'managing globalisation' what many have been saying for a long time: globalisation has not only advantages, but also many disadvantages. there's a difference between words and deeds. this is sometimes subtly reflected in the commission's choice of words: many europeans are also concerned. they associate globalisation with job losses, social injustice or low standards in the areas of environment, health and privacy and fear that globalisation will erode their traditions and identity. this is not a matter of mere perception but of bitter socioeconomic and ecological reality. the commission says that after this debate we will be better able to 'offer sustainable and fair solutions that meet the expectations of the european citizen'. i can see that. the desire to conclude bilateral free trade agreements such as ceta and ttip is still strong. these conventions, as has been amply demonstrated, will not contribute to more sustainable and social standards. this kind of free trade policy does not lead to a race to the top, but to a race to the bottom. it would be a credit to the eu to recognise this incoherent attitude.
mr president, the fight against food waste is probably one of the most blatant failures of our european policies. fifty european texts deal with this, more than that: directives, regulations, resolutions and despite this, each european household throws away the equivalent of 400 euros of food every year. it is an unacceptable waste, even immoral, when we know - and they have been quoted - the poverty figures in europe. the commission has no choice but to be ambitious, to put the gun to the fire and to stand with the european parliament in achieving our food waste reduction targets of 30% by 2025 and 50% by 2030. two points are essential: clarifying labelling, on the one hand, as has already been said, because 'preferably before' and 'up to' are real highways for waste, and thinking globally, on the other hand, if we want to reduce it from producer to consumer. agriculture, fisheries, the environment, health, vat - all these sectors are involved, but we need a driving force, a real will, a real strategy from the european union.
mrs mogherini, we are talking here today about the greatest humanitarian crisis facing the world, a crisis that has a major impact on the region. six million children are dependent on humanitarian aid. we are talking about europe's strategy here today, but frankly it is not very clear. too little, too late if you ask me. you are advocating political dialogue. we are a major humanitarian donor. all of that is true. but we're counting on the united nations very, very heavily. how credible is that if the united nations fails to condemn the latest poison gas attacks? we need to do more to bring the perpetrators to justice. i didn't hear you about the united states. i didn't hear you about russia. if we are talking about a real strategy for syria, i ask you: can it be a little more? can we go a little faster? so that we can save those children.
on behalf of the alde group. madam mogherini, i would like to thank you for the message of hope you have brought to the particularly difficult debate we have to have today. you are giving a message of hope, not only for christians, but for all religious groups that today are victims of persecution, discrimination, violence and murder. as regards the points you have raised, we must continue to press for religious freedom and, as europe, for respect for human rights worldwide. these are the things we must continue to do as europe, as the european union. we have to bet on that. that is why i am very pleased with the points you have listed. i would also like to stress that we must focus on punishing the perpetrators. as long as we allow impunity to exist, discrimination and violence will continue. we must also make a commitment to this as europe. it is a sign of genuine respect for human rights that you call to account those who perpetrate discrimination and murder. finally, mrs mogherini, you know that we as liberals attach great importance to peace and stability in the region. i do not know how many times i have discussed the middle east, israel and palestine with you here. well, we shall be putting an important resolution to the vote on thursday. the negotiations on this will take place tomorrow morning and we will work very hard with all parties to make a further appeal to israel and palestine for a two-state solution. if we take that step, i think the violence will decrease and perhaps there will be peace, which will also allow religious minorities to live together in peace. you have our support to go on and continue to advocate for human rights in the rest of the world.
mr president, two months ago, with the support of the united nations high commissioner for refugees, i had the opportunity to visit the dadaab refugee camp, which was set up in the 1990s and at that time had 90,000 places. today, it hosts more than 260,000 refugees. east africa is currently experiencing the most alarming humanitarian crisis due to drought, with somalia once again at the epicentre. the somali population is the largest in the camp, accounting for over 90% of the population. in 2011, drought had already killed 260,000 people. we must act with urgency. the igad summit recognised the role and generosity of neighbouring countries in taking on the international protection of refugees, despite their own socio-economic, environmental and national security challenges. however, i now call for a global sharing of the responsibility for this region. today's resolution is in that direction. it calls on europe to accept its share of relocations, as do canada and australia. it supports voluntary returns but acknowledges that such returns are difficult in the current context of drought. it knows that dadaab will not be able to close its doors and therefore asks for an increase in aid. it also calls for more development aid for the region and local communities, who are now bearing the brunt of this migration, but also for the eu's trust fund for africa to be properly funded and effective. the resolution calls for greater burden-sharing and sustainable solutions for social, economic and environmental development.

on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, i think on brexit everything has been set: we have a mandate and the european union, the 27 other member states and the three institutions have a unified position. everything is also going well with the negotiation directives that will be approved on monday. so i think there is only one thing to do now: to start the negotiations, and to start them as fast as possible. yes, we have to wait for the outcome of the british election, but after 8 june let’s start. i want to talk about something that is related to brexit, and that is the future of the european union and the reform of the european union, because it would be a mistake to put all our energy only into brexit in the coming years. at least the same energy, if not more energy, has to be put into the reform and the future of the european union. three times in a row now, the nationalists and the populists have been beaten – in austria, in the netherlands and in france – and i don’t think that happened by accident. i think that brexit played an important role in this. people all over europe have been shocked by the decision of great britain to leave the european union. don’t misunderstand me; people are today very critical towards the european union – and for good reason – but what they don’t want to do is what britain did: to leave the european union. it’s very clear that the austrians didn’t want an auxit, the dutch didn’t want a nexit and the french didn’t want a frexit. they say: reform the european union, yes; leave the european union, no bloody way. that is the message of the people of the european union today. but there is one mistake we cannot make, and that is to think ‘oh, it’s over; populism and nationalism have been defeated’ – and i have heard this reaction all too often in european circles – ‘macron won, le pen lost, relief, let’s go to the normal agenda and let’s do business as usual’. well, i think that is the one mistake that we cannot make. people voted for macron for change and not for the status quo; not for a standstill. they voted for a radical change in france, but also in europe, and so the best thing to do is in fact to back his reformist agenda – a reformist agenda that we all know now. a reformist agenda with the new european budget, minister of finance for europe, european treasury, european investment bonds and the transnational lists that he is proposing. i have to tell you, manfred, that i was relieved to hear on monday the positive reaction in berlin: a reaction a little bit different from the ‘nein, nein, nein’ of mr schäuble and of a lot of german press since the arrival of mr macron. i like more merkel’s ‘wir schaffen das’, as she repeated at the press conference with macron. so i think it is really time now to use this brexit negotiation for a new vision, mr tusk, and for a new future for europe. it is, in fact, a future that our young people want: look at what is happening – pulse of europe in more than 100 cities all over europe, in the squares, young people are coming together and asking for more, and a democratic, european union. what do you call this? i call it audacity. to paraphrase danton: ‘de l’audace, encore de l’audace, toujours de l’audace et l’europe sera sauvée.’
for the greens/efa group mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i am pleased that the council and parliament have been able to reach a broad agreement on a negotiating mandate with the united kingdom, a mandate without any revanchism or naivety. we are particularly pleased that the safeguarding of citizens' rights, which are at risk as a result of brexit, has been placed at the top of the list of priorities. but what does brexit tell us? have we really learned anything from this? i hear people saying that we have avoided the worst in austria, the netherlands and france, but i would like to warn us against any triumphalism. national populist forces are now reaching considerable electoral levels. when you add that to the abstentions and the votes cast, you can see how much the confidence of our citizens in their institutions and their political representatives is being shaken. in addition to a sense of loss of identity, this may be caused by a feeling of being downgraded or fear of being downgraded. the international monetary fund has just confirmed that globalisation and, above all, technological change are the main causes of the loss of the share of labour income in wealth, a wealth that is increasingly unequally shared. in the face of this, a timid awareness seems to be emerging, even in davos, even in the wto and even in the commission. we're starting to realize that our economic system leaves many of our fellow citizens stranded. but do you seriously think that for technological change and globalisation to work for everyone, it is enough to give everyone training that makes them employable and then it is enough to activate the unemployed workers, that is to put them under pressure, the same pressure that pushes them more and more to vote for the parties that surf on anger and stoke fears? rather, we think that in a world where increasing inequality seems inevitable, where machine work is increasingly replacing human work, where an mit study shows us that every robot installed takes out seven human jobs, and where technology platforms like uber are increasingly making work more precarious, we need to rethink both the organization of work and our taxation and provide robust social protection for all, and not just, if you will allow me, for shareholders. similarly, rather than a bottom-up approach under pressure from business, globalisation must aim to raise, level up social, environmental and democratic standards. this is how we will show that we have learned the lessons of brexit. it is only then that we will not only contain, but also reverse the dangerous wave of withdrawal into ourselves, because then our citizens will regain confidence, certain that their interests are protected. only then, with this newfound legitimacy, will we be able to proceed with further integration of the european union, which is the only way for us - i say the only way - to take back control of our destiny.
i am thinking here in the european parliament, in my seat, of the following: "if this were all a movie, what soundtrack would we hear, what music would be stuck to the footage?" i think that would be abba: "money, money, money. i'm going to the bank. it's so funny [sings] there are wonderful things happening here in the european union, wonderful things, but there are also many things that are going wrong, that are really going wrong. and the question for all of us is: "how do we meet these challenges?" unfortunately, i find that the union's answer is all too often: "money, money, money". that's what i said. we just throw a big bag of money at it and our problems will automatically be solved. mario draghi seems to be a fan of abba too. the president of the european central bank creates some money and he pumps it into the economy. is this good for growth? yes, in the short term. we'll buy something today that we would have bought tomorrow. since saving doesn't pay off, we just spend our money. but everyone feels that this growth is a sham, that it is not sustainable, that this monetary hocus pocus will have to stop at some point. and that's not the only side effect of this policy. there's more. people's purchasing power is declining. saving money is no longer profitable. pensions are in jeopardy. the inequality is growing, between the middle class and the people who are really rich, and certainly between people who are poor and the rich. we create bubbles, in the real estate market, in the stock market. you can see that's not a sustainable policy. and that is what surprises me most, how all the traditional political parties in this parliament are defending this policy. we talk a lot about tax fairness, about fairness. how in the world can you defend that and at the same time defend the policies of the european central bank that are increasing inequality? i can't get to that. i think that we, as parliamentarians, as politicians, would be better off choosing a different soundtrack, a different song, that we would be better off not listening to abba, but listening to the beatles, that we would be better off not listening to "money, money, money" but listening to "money can't buy me love". i won't sing that, that's for next time, but i really think that's the answer. we will not buy love for the european union with a bag of money. that's not how we're gonna do it. what are we supposed to do? reforming the system. the hard way. save your money. don't take shortcuts, but do what needs to be done. and there, too, the policy of the european central bank is bad, because it undermines that very need for reform. what bothers me most of all, however, is the fact that the european central bank is not sufficiently transparent about this - it has already been mentioned by the honourable member. there's a lot of work to be done. that is why we have set up our own internet platform, "leefgeld.eu". we have our next event on 26 june, where we will be debating precisely the mandate of the european central bank, in brussels. you are cordially invited to attend.

in writing. we will vote tomorrow on legislation that will allow eu citizens who subscribe to online films, music and tv series to access this content even when they are abroad. almost one in two europeans is affected. in particular, 49% of internet users in europe now listen to music, watch videos and play games online. but when you leave your national borders, you can't, or hardly, access the online content services you subscribe to and therefore can't get the movies, tv shows, music, games, or even sporting events. we have got these restrictions removed and the citizen can access all this online content wherever he is in europe, when travelling or when he is on international business trips. good news for the consumer.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, of course there have been some difficulties in the implementation of systematic checks at the external borders. this is all the more regrettable since this is a false external border, which is destined to disappear in the near future. but these few difficulties must not obscure the essential point: systematic controls at external borders are necessary and justified. in belgium, on the first day of the implementation of these checks, 15 people listed in the police databases were arrested. i'm aware, standing in line is always awkward. however, it is not reasonable to feel sorry for ourselves on the grounds that a wait of more than 15 minutes is unbearable on the road when we know that people who take the plane sometimes have to wait for more than two hours and that all their luggage is checked. however, the current system provides that the member states concerned may use targeted checks use them! , that the member states use european funds to equip themselves with efficient and necessary equipment and, finally, that the commission indicates what fluidity really is to help the states to make use of the possibility of targeted checks.
the european transport sector is becoming more and more self-sufficient. deadly emissions, countless road deaths and increasing congestion are bringing the industry a bad name and serious headaches. the unfair competition caused by the abuse of european and social regulations also makes life very difficult for many transport operators. the setting up of shady mail-bus companies and the hiring of underpaid and untrained drivers is a widespread practice. it's nothing short of modern day slavery! this is a matter of great concern to me and to many flemings with me. these abuses have brought unfair competition, social exploitation and road safety to the fore. that is a pity, especially when you consider the important role the sector plays in our european economy. it is therefore urgently time to upgrade the european transport sector. this can only be achieved by more effective european cooperation and better implementation and enforcement of existing european rules. i look forward to the european commission's proposals with great anticipation. the eu is there to create prosperity, not to facilitate a downward spiral.
mr president, commissioner, as you know, belgium is being shaken by a legal case against a major road haulage company. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. if the facts are true, we are talking about extremely serious things: criminal organizations, human trafficking, social fraud and money laundering. i don't want this europe anymore. i believe in europe and i really want a europe that puts social and economic issues on the same footing. i want a europe where the principle of equal work, equal pay for equal work is a fundamental right that is applied and monitored. your responsibility here is immense, because the belgian case demonstrates the inefficiency of the system. you must put an end to the social carnage which has enriched some companies by sacrificing jobs on the one hand and the fundamental rights of tens of thousands of european workers on the other. we therefore expect a lot from your proposals and i hope that the first negative reactions we hear are only fiction in view of the immense challenges in the road sector. we look forward to your proposals.
mr president, criminal organisation, trafficking in human beings, money laundering, counterfeiting and the use of counterfeits: this is what the belgian road transport company jost is suspected of. it is now suspected of major social fraud, a charge which reminds us that social dumping undermines our society, with workers who carry out their jobs in sometimes inhuman conditions as the main victims. social dumping must be fought this is what the president of the commission often says to us with conviction and reason. this action is essential if the european project is to have a future. we cannot be satisfied with vague statements. we must act at all levels, especially at european level. the commission will present its road transport package. without anticipating the content, i would like to stress a few priorities. the rules on cabotage and posting of workers must be strictly applied. there is an urgent need to regulate the commercial transport of small trucks. it is a question of working conditions, unfair competition and road safety. finally, it is essential to guarantee drivers' rest time. the transport package must meet these challenges. social dumping must be stopped and decent working conditions for workers must be ensured. i am convinced that you can count on the social partners and the work of this parliament to achieve this.
the mobility package launched by the commission on 31 may is the perfect opportunity to ensure decent working conditions for drivers and to tackle social dumping once and for all. that's exactly what i'm going to evaluate the next package on: does it tackle social dumping and improve the working conditions of drivers? does it end exploitation and the sometimes terrible conditions in which drivers have to do their jobs? does it ensure that we have well-equipped drivers on our roads who are paid a decent wage? is the law being followed? these are the crucial questions we must ask ourselves and on which the package must be judged. the fight against social dumping is not a struggle between east and west. let's not fall into that trap. it is a fight for better working conditions for drivers, for greater safety on our roads, against fraud (often perpetrated by companies from western european member states who, through mailbox companies, ignore the existing rules) and against exploitation of drivers. i am counting on the commissioner to come out with a package that tackles social dumping and fraud unequivocally and completely ignores the improvement of the working conditions of drivers.
mr president, korea is often cited as an example in free trade agreements. when we look at the trade balance today in favour of europe, but also at the economic growth in korea, we might say that, in the end, things are going very well. it's part of the deal. today, there are a number of criticisms about the issue of workers' rights in korea. this is the main task we have to tackle today. if this chapter on social and sustainable development were indeed binding, and if it also provided for sanctions, we could force korea to take a step towards respecting these workers' rights. therefore, i think it is important that these checks be put in place fairly quickly, that there be this formal consultation procedure with korea and, above all, before any further work, i think it is necessary to require that country to return to this logic of sustainable development and respect for workers' rights.
