member of the commission. – madam president, i would like to thank members for their very open comments and questions, and also to for all the support that they have expressed by picking the right topics that need to be discussed in order to move forward on issues related to road transport in the european union. addressing eu-wide problems with eu solutions is the right way to go, and the best way to avoid unilateral, uncoordinated national measures which fragment our internal market. with the proposal before you, we are trying to avoid that and to ensure adequate working conditions for drivers in a more enforceable way, while at the same time reducing administrative costs and burdens for operators. let me now directly address a few of your concerns and questions and hopefully bring more clarity to the proposal and our further discussions. letterbox companies are illegal and are used to obtain cost advantage by some hauliers. that is a fact. we define here clear criteria to avoid letterbox companies and to better enforce this, in particular through enhanced cooperation between national enforcement authorities. i have been asked on numerous occasions to do something about vehicles below 3.5 tons, especially the so-called white vans that are very often used to bypass european rules. my message here is very clear: yes vehicles and hauliers under 3.5 tonnes are included in this proposal, and the commission is making sure that they are subject to certain european rules, as described in the package. there were a couple of comments on a european road transport agency. at this point we are focusing on improving the existing enforcement tools and systems and on further enhancing cooperation between national enforcement bodies. i hope it is going to work. experience to date with the implementation of social legislation does not show that cross-border enforcement is much more cost effective and more consistent when carried out by the member state authorities within established framework structures. a road agency could indeed be helpful but for the member states and the commission, in assuring consistent and efficient cross-border enforcement, we would first like to focus on the solutions proposed. but i remain open to developing this subject further, in cooperation with you. tachographs were mentioned several times. we propose to accelerate the deployment of smart technologies and smart tachographs, which will further facilitate the enforcement of social rules and avoid fraud. for example, operators equipping their vehicles with these modern digital tachographs before the deadline should be rewarded with fewer controls on european roads. on driving and rest time, i would also like to repeat that the limits will remain the same. the flexibility to organise operations can in no way go against road safety and social protection. on the contrary, they will contribute to facilitating returns home. this is the approach proposed by the commission. these rules could be complemented by additional social measures relating to working time but, for those, from today onwards the commission is opening a social dialogue and will propose legislative proposals in the autumn in the second phase of the mobility package. on charging, member states are still free to decide whether they want to charge or not. they are free to decide what they want to charge, which roads they want to charge for and which not. but, if they decide to use charging systems, we have given them enough time to readjust their systems to a compatible, interoperable eu-based system. on cabotage, let me underline once again that our new cabotage rules do not imply further market opening or further market closure. market access and business opportunities will remain as they are today, but more flexibility will allow operators to optimise their operations, and simpler rules will certainly be of great help to controllers or enforcement officers in controlling illegal practices much better. on proposal and posting, first for cabotage operations, these are operations whereby goods are loaded and unloaded within the same country. there is a clear and strong link with that country and, therefore, the drivers should be paid according to the rules of the country from day one onwards. let us say that a slovenian driver drops a load off in milan and reloads in milan to unload in rome. for the time spent on the cabotage operation between milan and rome, italian rules will apply. secondly, for international operations – these are operations where the loading and unloading take place in different countries – we clarify that there is a sufficient link with the country concerned only to the extent that the driver spends at least three days a month in that country. to put it simply, international journeys lasting less than three days are excluded from the posting of workers directive. lastly, i would like to urge you all to focus not only on these particular issues. the road initiatives contain a very comprehensive list of measures that are well balanced and represent a substantial step forward in the management of road traffic in europe, making it much more modern, fair, sustainable and future-oriented. we really need to improve the road sector in order to stay competitive and to make sure that europe stays a social place.
vice-president of the commission. – madam president, once again i appreciate the quality of the debate and i think it has been very clearly indicated what the points will be that we need to take care of, and on which we need to start daily contacts so we can work with you on speedy delivery. first and foremost, however, i really appreciate, and would like to thank the house for, the positive spirit and the welcome for the package, and also for underlining the importance of attention to the bigger picture, and how much we are trying to transform this very important sector. we have 50 million people working in transport-related industries, and we need to bear in mind how important it is for the quality of life in our cities and our rural areas, and how big a contribution transport can make to modernising and energising europe, and to a cleaner environment. i would agree with all the honourable members who have been calling for consistency and for speedy progress in working on this package in parliament and in the council, and i would like to reassure you that we are absolutely ready to respond to those calls. we had good experience, on the clean energy for all package, in working regularly with all the rapporteurs responsible for various parts of this package. i was invited to the conference of committee chairs where we had a very good cross-sectoral discussion. i know how important it is to cooperate well with all the committees because what we are doing here does not belong to one committee only: it has real cross-sectoral impact. to conclude, i would like to thank you for your strong encouragement and for the clear call to work together on providing leadership on climate change, because here i am absolutely convinced that we europeans are on the right side of history. the calls for a clean environment, for energy efficiency, for renewables, for modernisation and technological advancement of the economy, and for a high level of public awareness and citizens’ support, are very strong in europe. now we need to use this ‘first mover’ advantage to demonstrate our global leadership. i know that the world very much expects this from us, and i am absolutely sure that europe will deliver.
in writing. it is our duty to put an end to the social ills in the transport sector. the recent judicial raid on the belgian-luxembourg transport company jost shows that the fight against social dumping in the transport sector is finally getting off the ground. the measures proposed by the commission are a step in the right direction, but they are not without effective control measures. all trucks on international roads urgently need to be equipped with a smart digital tachograph. this will enable the inspectorate to quickly check whether drivers are complying with the new working rules and driving and rest times, the distances they are travelling and the speed at which they are travelling. we do not want to see the rules on driving and rest periods and the chaos in the sector eroded. road safety is our first priority.

on behalf of the alde group. madam president, madam commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, my group welcomes the creation of this new common visa model for third country nationals and congratulates our rapporteur on the quality of her work. following high-profile cases of visa sticker forgery in several member states, the experts considered that the security of the model in its current form was not sufficient and therefore, after analysing the most common types of fraud, they proposed several improvements. we support them wholeheartedly along with you, madam rapporteur. i have two regrets, however. firstly, we believe, and continue to believe, that a transitional period of 15 months is long enough, if not too long. secondly, we regret that a legal dispute has dragged the case for months. we are therefore pleased that a solution has finally been found through joint efforts, in particular to keep ireland in the scheme. i would like to take this opportunity to address the commission on two issues. commissioner, and i am speaking to the whole commission through you, first of all i think that the inclusion of national visas in the visa information system would also be a good thing to combat counterfeiting in this area and to strengthen border crossing security. secondly, do you not think it is high time that we also introduced a uniform identity card model? it is nevertheless surprising that third-country nationals staying or travelling in the union have a uniform document while union citizens still do not. at present, the security of identity cards is left to the discretion of the member states. that's 27 forms of identification. and we ask our administrations, our police, our border guards to know 27 different ways to prove identity. this is a source of confusion for the benefit of the mafia and anyone who wants to use a fake id. when will the commission take a serious initiative in this area, madam commissioner?

i am glad that we are voting on this important report tomorrow. research and innovation are essential to the prosperity of the european union. it is therefore not surprising that horizon 2020 is such a great success. the european union is demonstrating that action in the field of research and innovation has a clear added value. unfortunately, the program is also a victim of its own success. we see that the pass rate has been greatly reduced by over-enrolment. right now, we're at about 10 percent, which means some very brilliant researchers are cutting out and not taking their chances. this in turn threatens to throw away crucial opportunities. i therefore call for more budget for the next framework programme and for a stricter selection procedure in the two-stage procedure. we have had a long debate on the role of business in the framework programme. these companies are essential to the success of the programme: the commission has already taken a number of measures to improve the quality of the information it receives. i therefore think that we should consider the rules for participation in the next framework programme with a view to involving even more companies. finally, i would like to say that i think the report puts the right emphasis on what is going well and what is going badly in the programme and has a right vision for the next framework programme. we must continue to focus on the problems that are the citizens' concern: good jobs, health and safety. i am therefore pleased to see that the minds are ripe to invest in defence research.
continuing to invest in research and development is the only way to remain relevant as a society. it is therefore very important that we clearly indicate that horizon 2020 is a huge added value for the european union and the member states. not only by freeing up additional resources, but above all by focusing on cross-border cooperation. these cross-pollination activities are considered very important by all participants and together with the criterion of excellence form the core of this programme. if we look at framework programme 9, these are the elements which must be preserved. i have always been opposed to this research programme being treated as a kind of cohesion fund. we must recognize that there is a problem with the absorption of funds by the eu-13, but those member states must invest in good training and infrastructure. this is of course a prerequisite for excellent research and for obtaining european funds. that is why i also think it is important that we look at further deepening synergies with the structural funds. this already includes an r & d aspect, but it can be further developed to close the gap between the eu-13 and the eu-15. the participation of industry is also very important. the fund should not be used exclusively to finance basic research or to solve scientific problems. we also need to give it practical applications and bring it to the market. this balance is very important. we need to balance this and divide the focus appropriately between the high and low trl scales. we also need to recognise that industry plays a crucial role in realising and implementing innovation. it is often also beneficial for smes, which thrive under the wings of these large companies. we are sending a clear and positive signal to the commission and the member states with our report on how we see framework programme 9 and where our research policy should go. i look forward to further interesting discussions on this subject.

energy efficiency is the best and cheapest way to achieve our climate objectives. with these revisions, we are removing the advantages of labels, so that consumers can be clear about the performance of their device and are encouraged to choose the most economical product. at the same time, it will encourage manufacturers to develop and produce even more energy-efficient appliances. we are taking an approach that saves companies, from large producers to small traders, unnecessary burdens. we're dividing up the responsibility for distributing and putting the label on so that everyone knows what their job is. but my focus is on the consumer. better information leads to better choices. so we're creating a database where app developers and comparison websites can find all the data to give consumers a clear view. at the same time, it is important that we adequately protect these consumers. we have seen that this is absolutely necessary in the volkswagen software fraud scandal. for me, that means not only that you give a guarantee of at least two years, but also that you can guarantee that vehicles or appliances do not consume or pollute more than stated. if the labelling is incorrect, the consumer should be able to claim the correct replacements or refunds. i am glad that the commission is looking at how best to deal with this in our legislation. ladies and gentlemen, this dossier shows that europe is working for the people. it is a well-designed and targeted measure that makes each consumer think about his consumption and helps him make the right choices. the consumer will save energy, which is good for our environment, and therefore spend less money, which is good for his wallet.
the legislation presented here today is an important part of the energy efficiency discussions we have. the new energy labelling measures will enable us to achieve energy savings at european level equivalent to the energy consumption of the baltic states and italy combined, which is not to be underestimated. it is a very important consideration. this is one of the many measures we are taking which could help us achieve the european energy efficiency targets. but what do we see? the proposals for the winter package on energy efficiency make it very clear that the european measures taken by the member states in the field of energy efficiency cannot be counted towards achieving their objectives. i have some serious questions about that. because then what are we doing here? why are we making the european rules if they can't count?
in writing. there was no harmonisation of energy efficiency labelling for european household appliances. this dusty find will soon be filed away in the broom closet. we therefore propose a single labelling standardised from a to g to provide clearer information to consumers and to reduce their energy consumption. the aim of this measure is also to reduce the consumption of european citizens by 200 billion kw/h, which is a substantial saving for their portfolios. in addition, all new products placed on the union market will have to be registered in an online database, which will provide greater transparency and facilitate market surveillance by national authorities. 85% of european consumers take the energy index into account when making purchases. however, up to 25% of products placed on the market do not comply with energy labelling requirements. it was therefore urgent to rectify the situation and improve the consumer's situation.

private and public. to put it simply, without a full banking union, without a social, fiscal and democratic union, the euro is doomed to disappear. this is clear from the reflection paper tabled by the commission two weeks ago, but only in a watermark. it is in addition to those proposed in 2012 by barroso on the one hand and by van rompuy on the other, and to the famous report of the five presidents tabled in 2015. so we have been sitting on our hands for five years, because we lack the vision and the political will to act. yes, we need to complete the banking union by adding to common supervision and common crisis management a common deposit guarantee with a common public budgetary safety net. yes, we need a euro area budget to invest in particular in the energy transition, because, deployed in a counter-cyclical way, it will help cushion macroeconomic shocks, but it will also improve the strength of the european economy. yes, we must deepen the solidarity between our social protection systems, for example with a common unemployment reinsurance system. finally, if we are to tackle excessive deficits, whether they are trade deficits or budget deficits, we must tackle excessive surpluses with the same vigour, because they are just as deleterious as the former. we must also put a stop to the excessive growth of both public and private debts, because their increase is a sign of danger. none of this can be done without solid democratic control. the european parliament and the national parliaments must be closely involved in this process. my dear colleagues, sharing a currency is sharing a common good. this requires both strong solidarity and an equally strong sense of shared responsibility. all this is possible only with common rules, for example in the financial sector. in this respect, let me say that what we have seen over the past year is more of a discreet dismantling of the little progress we have made than the simplification and tightening of the rules we need. we have budgetary rules, but experience shows that they are inapplicable simply because they are procyclical, that is to say, they make the situation worse when it is already serious and, combined with the neoliberal doxa that continues to dominate this house, the commission and the council, they make it the weakest shoulders that end up bearing the heaviest part of the burden. rather than getting rid of the rules, we must have the courage to transform them, both financially and budgetarily, so that they regain their meaning, that is, the defence of the on behalf of the alde group. mr president, the glyphosate saga is continuing. the ball is in our court again today, in the european parliament, exactly 14 months after the vote on our resolution which already called for its prohibition in specific uses. from study to study, from agency to agency, from expert to counter-expert, opinions follow one another and do not resemble each other and clarity is not achieved, i am using an understatement. last march, the "monsanto papers" set fire to public opinion in the united states. the declassified documents have spoken. monsanto was seriously concerned about the mutagenic nature of glyphosate as early as 1999. a concealment of evidence if there is one, but the investigation will have to say so. the alde group, which i represent, continues to trust and wants to trust the work of the european food safety authority (efsa) and the european chemicals agency (echa), which is based in helsinki. we also know - and the commissioner has reminded us - that on this issue, as on gmos, for example, the member states hide their divisions and do not take their responsibilities. however, there is no time to lose: the commission's roadmap should be based on a few principles. the commission must ensure that its final decision is based on independent scientific studies and not on biased work and documents provided by monsanto or other firms. in this transitional phase, before december, it is imperative for the commission to change its model and finally move to a logic of gradual prohibition of the substance, rather than re-approval and derogation. the european agencies must also be given the means to achieve their ambitions. i find it quite shocking - i say this in passing - that efsa's budget is almost four times smaller than that of the european medicines agency, while food is the third largest item of expenditure for european households. that is what you should do, commissioner, but what you most likely will not do, given what you have just answered us this afternoon. basically, all the elements are known, i quote you, there is nothing to declare, and worse, the "monsanto papers" will not change anything, circulate. it is a startling end to the lack of response, which i find regrettable, and i am using a euphemism again, because the main thing - and sometimes the obvious must be hammered - is that your principle of public action, mr commissioner, must be the precautionary principle.
last year, i voted against renewing the glyphosate license. there was no scientific consensus on the question of whether glyphosate is carcinogenic. i was advocating greater transparency, clarity, independent inquiry, and that position remains fully in place today. news that monsanto has allegedly directed and manipulated research makes the call for transparency all the more compelling. the commission must now act. i strongly believe in evidence-based policy based on objective science. the question to the commission is therefore how it intends to continue to guarantee such a policy and how it is responding to the call for transparency. at the same time, we want to be open-minded about any structural changes to the system for the approval of herbicides and pesticides and we call for a thorough analysis of the professional use of glyphosate. i hope that the commission, like us, will put the protection of human health and the environment first and come up with clear answers.
mr president, commissioner, like you, we must have confidence in science. politics must rely on science to decide, but several decades ago, scientists said that asbestos was completely harmless, and today, asbestos still kills hundreds of thousands of people a year on the planet. scientists said aspartame was harmless and they still say that today, but aspartame is probably the next food bomb that's going to go off. scientists have said that glyphosate is harmless, but the monsanto papers, since last march, have shown us that some scientists have altered their findings and rigged their investigation to allow glyphosate to be put on the market. therefore, it is permissible to doubt the scientific opinions that are given. it is therefore necessary to make all the transparency about the independence and reliability of the investigations on which our agencies rely to give their opinions. commissioner, that is why we need a committee of inquiry, to establish the whole truth and reliability in order to have confidence in our system. it is in the interest of the 500 million european citizens and their health, and even of the 7 billion citizens who inhabit the planet.
mr president, the humanitarian and security situation in the democratic republic of the congo is deteriorating sharply. violence and repression are rampant in the once peaceful kasai. since august 2016, summary executions, child murders, the recruitment of child soldiers, sexual violence have been hitting civilian populations. the violence involving militias, soldiers and police has left more than 400 dead. more than 40 mass graves have been discovered, 400 000 children are at risk of starvation, and more than 1.5 million congolese have fled the region. this crisis in kasai is playing out against the backdrop of what should be a political transition provided for by the agreement of 31 december 2016 and which is barely coming to fruition. even though voter registration appears to be accelerating, the adoption of electoral laws and the establishment of the national monitoring council for the agreement are challenges that the congolese authorities must address by holding free, transparent and democratic elections. in a serious context, parliament called on the congolese authorities and the international community to make every effort to respect all the commitments made in the new year's eve agreement, including the setting of a clear and precise timetable for the holding of elections, without amending the constitution. we also call for an international inquiry into the violence in kasai so that those responsible can be held to account. finally, the european parliament also supports the targeted sanctions put in place by the union against those responsible for serious human rights violations. i would also remind you that it is the government's responsibility to ensure the safety of its people and that it has a responsibility for this. therefore, we call on the kabila government to protect its people.
in march, the bodies of zaïda catalan and michael sharp were found in kasai province. the two un experts were killed by militiamen or congolese soldiers. they were just sent out to find clues to the newly-charged violence. in the meantime, 20 mass graves have been discovered and sources speak of 1,500 dead. it could have been very different for the civilians in kasai. the potential to become a prosperous piece of africa is enormous. kasai is rich in minerals and fertile agricultural land. the kasai deaths are a symptom of the real problem. congo is a rudderless ship with a groveling elite, a divided army and an invisible president who clings to his position. the mass escape from bukavu prison in south kivu last sunday is further proof of this. the political system in the congo has failed. it's bankrupt, just like the international community. he's watching. it reacts occasionally but is otherwise completely divided and therefore powerless. the people of kasai have no use for international condemnation without consequences.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, for once i have to contradict manfred weber. he said that there is at least one positive outcome of the british election: the disappearance of ukip and of those who want not only britain out of europe, but who want to destroy the european union from inside. i think that it is true that there will be a lot of items on the agenda in the european council next week, but the main point on the agenda is whether we can now start the negotiations on brexit. it is three years since the start of the debate, a year since the outcome of the british referendum and three months since the triggering of the notification, and we are not still not in a position to start negotiations, because there is – for the moment anyway – no clear position from the new government in the united kingdom – if there is already a new government, because every day i read that there is a deal, but then six hours later there is no deal and they are still discussing it. but there are five pressing questions that need to be answered on a short—term basis. mr kamall, maybe you can help the government to respond to them. first: is the british position still that of the letter of 29 march, or will the new government take into account the outcome of the election, which was a little different from the hard talk of the letter of the 29th? the second question is: will it be the opinion of the tories or the position of the whole nation? party interest or national interest? because this is not about the tories leaving the european union but about the uk leaving the european union. the third question: what about the good friday agreement? what is the proposal so that the good friday agreement can be kept in place and that no hard border will be created between the republic on the one hand and northern ireland on the other hand? the fourth question: will the british government – as we do on the european side – one hundred per cent guarantee the current rights of eu citizens living in britain, like we are ready to guarantee the current rights of british citizens living on the continent? finally, the fifth question: is it still the opinion of the uk government to leave the single market and the customs union? is the populist illusion of limiting free movement in europe really more important than the prosperity and fortune of british workers, british industry, british companies and the british economy? because let us face reality. this brexit has already had a negative impact on everybody in europe, and especially on britain. zero growth in the first quarter in britain; for the moment it is three times less than in the last quarter of last year. and because of what? because of the uncertainty that has been created. so i think the time is now right for us to receive from the british side clear answers to these five questions so that these negotiations can start. i know that yesterday emmanuel macron, the new french president, spoke about an open door. he said that, if britain is changing its mind, it will find an open door. i do not disagree with him but, like in alice in wonderland, all doors are now the same. it will be a brand new door with a new europe: a europe without rebates, without complexity, with real power and with unity. that is the door towards europe. for me, jean-claude, the most important thing now is the reform of our union, and for this reform not to be bogged down in these brexit talks. brexit talks for the moment look more like the echternach procession – you are from luxembourg and maybe you can explain to everybody what the procession of echternach is – namely two steps forward and one step back. that is typical echternach. i am very grateful to the commission for the different reflection papers that they have put on the table, but the commission is more than a think tank. i think the moment is now there, when we start these brexit talks, to have also legislative proposals on the reform of the european union, on defence – as you have done – and on economic and monetary union. but not only reflection papers: we also need legislative proposals. do not leave the space open for the council. it is not for the council to fill in the space; it is the commission that will have to do it with legislative proposals. we have now a new president in france who is pro-european, and we will have german elections. that is the moment to go forward. we always say do not waste a good crisis. well, do not waste a good election cycle either.
the european council will soon be dealing again with the problem of the irregular migration of economic migrants from libya to italy. in addition to the 200 million euros we are investing in humane reception in libya and its environs, it is time to work on an eu return policy, based on our experience with the eu-turkey deal. this deal resulted in fast-tracked asylum procedures in greece, so that all refugees who were already safe in turkey could be sent back to that country. this is how we got the flow of refugees under control. i wonder what we are waiting for to set up the same kind of cooperation with all the countries in north africa. why should we stick to the one eu-turkey deal? after all, it's legal to send migrants back to a safe haven. the commission itself considers that this is a place where human life is not threatened, where basic needs are met and where transport can also be arranged. other north african countries besides turkey are certainly eligible. i therefore call on all european leaders to examine the legal possibilities for reaching an eu-libya deal, for example. it is the only way to avoid deadly maritime tragedies.
madam president, president trump made his announcement to leave the paris agreement, ironically, in the rose garden, and the question is: how should we react? we can be very angry, we can be frustrated – but let us not be afraid, and let us stand united. we have heard many of our political leaders in europe say that we will keep up the engagements in the paris agreement. it is nice to give good speeches, but the proof of the pudding is in the eating, and the first thing we need to do in europe is implement the paris agreement in full. when we have a vote on the effort sharing, when we discuss renewable energy, when we discuss energy efficiency, let us move up and make it more ambitious. that is our goal. second, we always say that europe is divided – with two speeds, or three speeds. well, we can also say that in the united states we have more than one speed: we have the trump administration, but we also have states, cities, mayors, companies that have said they will implement the paris agreement and that they will keep on track with it. let us not turn our back on the united states and their people, but work together with these cities and states. i think that is also what the european commission should do: to make sure that at international level we keep up with china, india, but also with the citizens of the united states. rest assured, trump is not the rightful representative of the people in the united states, because they do stand with the paris agreement and with combating climate change.
whatever the position of the united states, we have embarked on the path to a low-carbon, energy-efficient economy and there is no going back. this is of course the right choice, not least because it reduces our dependence on unreliable oil and gas suppliers. so ambition, especially in the reform of the division of labour which we shall be voting on shortly. but also: do not be naive. look at the reform of the ets, where we want measures to stop the carbon leakage. and at the same time, we're going to continue to work on that reform of the ets, making that system more robust and, above all, linking it to systems like california. because we have strong allies in the united states. let us also continue to work on a system, on a level playing field for industrial producers who want to produce for europe. we can also use the ets successfully for this.
the old continent, that is the name of the european union, while we have everything to be at the top of the international ladder. but for that, we're going to have to reform more, we're going to have to save more, so that we can use that money that we save to make sensible, smart investments. i sincerely believe that the european efsi fund can help in this. the ambition is very high: in three years, €315 billion in additional investment in innovation, in infrastructure, in smes. today we are evaluating the first phase of that efsi fund. and what's the conclusion? we're getting our numbers. that's a good thing. on the other hand, we find that the quality of investment must be improved, raised. because the efsi should not do what any ordinary bank or investment fund can do. we can expect more from the efsi, so that we can invest in more innovation, in quality projects, in more risky projects. that should be our priority: we must invest in excellence, put europe back on the map, not choose to level down, but choose to make real progress.
madam president, commissioner, as you said, the efsi is a project that has been quite successful, particularly in terms of the number of projects that europe has been able to support. but this is a recovery plan that is far from meeting all our expectations. the quality of the projects selected has significant gaps and shortcomings. i believe that we must move towards a better geographical distribution of projects, their sectoral diversification, a more transparent selection procedure and own funding, without drawing on the budgets of other initiatives. i would like to stress, commissioner, the importance of productive public investment directed towards the real economy. i think it is really necessary for the authorities, especially local authorities, to be able to invest in productive projects, such as insulation of public buildings, because the consequences are positive for the economy, generating local jobs which, in turn, are not relocatable and in line with environmental criteria. commissioner, i would like to emphasize that the golden rule is not a social project, it is a working method. as the treaties provide, i think it is high time to re-work the flexibility of accounting for our public investments if we are to re-create growth and jobs.
member of the commission. – madam president, thank you for the numerous comments, questions and remarks. efsi might not be perfect. it certainly is not a magical weapon that can, on its own, close the investment gap we suffer from: create growth and jobs and put our economy on a sustainable and inclusive track. but our debate and the reports prove that it is a valuable instrument in pursuit of this aim. i hear all the expectations in terms of job creation, ambition as regards macroeconomic impact, vigilance in the face of potential crowding out of other investors and fears of cannibalisations of other eu instruments. i understand them all and i share this impatience. but let us discuss on the basis of actual results, and focus on positive messages. efsi delivers, and we are in the midst of negotiations to extend it and also to make it better. je voudrais, à cet égard, répondre à quelques questions ou remarques. on m’a demandé si l’efsi était censé remplacer les fonds de cohésion. la réponse est non. nous attachons une importance majeure à la politique de cohésion. l’efsi et la politique de cohésion peuvent coexister et peuvent se renforcer mutuellement. tous deux partagent un objectif, qui est d’augmenter le niveau d’investissement au profit de la croissance et de l’emploi. il s’agit de développer les synergies entre l’efsi et les fonds de cohésion, en aucun cas de les opposer ou de les exclure. plusieurs remarques ont été faites sur tel ou tel pays et sur la façon dont ils sont desservis par les fonds de cohésion. à cet égard, bien sûr, nous avons des statistiques. je mentionnerai simplement deux pays. d’abord, chypre. c’est vrai que, pendant longtemps, il n’y avait aucun projet, mais un accord a été signé en décembre 2016 sur le volet pme entre le fonds européen d’investissement et la banque rcb. en ce qui concerne la grèce, il y a eu plusieurs interventions. pendant longtemps, c’est vrai qu’il n’y a pas eu de projet en grèce. une équipe du hub a été envoyée pour prêter assistance à la structuration de projets et, aujourd’hui, la grèce, écoutez bien, est le huitième pays à bénéficier de fonds efsi. par conséquent, il y a eu plus qu’un rattrapage en la matière. je saisis cette occasion pour dire à quel point je souhaite que, demain, lors de l’eurogroupe qui se tiendra à luxembourg, on parvienne à trouver un accord pour la grèce qui permette de débourser les fonds prévus dans le cadre du troisième programme et de dessiner les paramètres de mesure des dettes à moyen terme, tout cela sur fond d’une politique qui doit privilégier la croissance et l’emploi dans ce pays. parce qu’après la crise, après les efforts, il est temps que le peuple grec voie le résultat de tout ce à quoi il a consenti. mon collègue de la commission, valdis dombrovskis, faisait hier cette présentation à mes côtés, lors du collège, et la commission sait qu’elle a déjà fait beaucoup pour la croissance et l’emploi en grèce. à travers l’efsi aussi, nous voulons faire plus. il faut faire plus. c’est fondamental. mon collègue katainen et moi-même sommes prêts à revenir dans ce parlement d’ici un mois peut-être, avant la coupure de l’été, pour discuter du résultat des trilogues. le président juncker a présenté l’efsi 2 dans son discours sur l’état de l’union, en septembre 2016. soyez sûrs qu’en un an, nous avons déjà fourni des résultats dans cette direction au bénéfice de nos citoyens européens. par conséquent, mon message est simple: l’efsi est un instrument perfectible, critiquable, comme tout instrument, mais qui est aussi remarquable. alors perfectionnons-le ensemble. c’est ce que nous pouvons faire et c’est toute l’utilité de nos échanges d’aujourd’hui.
in writing. i would ask you to vote in favour of this report, which calls for an initiative by the commission to monitor foreign investment in strategic european sectors at eu level, especially when the countries from which these investments come restrict or prevent european investors from accessing their markets. i believe it is essential that the european union identifies the key sectors and industries which it must protect and, where appropriate, prevent targeted acquisitions by foreign investors. at the moment, as i have often said, there is a clear problem of reciprocity (especially with china) and major risks for europe, because strategic sectors such as robotics, defence, high technology and the airport sector are being targeted. the risk is that technology transfers to a third country will ultimately threaten european interests. more than ever, we need a strategic industrial policy: we need to protect our key industries.
member of the commission. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i believe that this is first and foremost a very constructive exchange on this absolutely crucial issue. the commission will have to provide precise answers to each other's questions, particularly in the framework of the international trade commission (inta). the european union needs policies that enable us to measure the positive and the less positive aspects of globalisation and thus respond to the concerns of our citizens. the commission analyses the challenges associated with the acquisition by foreign firms of european companies, which are themselves active in strategic sectors linked to industrial policy considerations. we must also be particularly vigilant when it comes from countries that apply restrictions to direct investment - that is the principle of reciprocity - and when there are no fair conditions of competition for our own investors. i would like to assure you that all the concerns related to such investments will be scrupulously and carefully analysed and that we will discuss with you the definition of all possible instruments to remedy these situations. the commission is, of course, ready to work closely with parliament and the other institutions to develop and implement policies that will address all the risks that can affect sustainability and prosperity in the union. we must be an open space, of course, but without naivety and with a concern for reciprocity.
president-in-office of the council. – madam president, it has been a pleasure to participate in this plenary debate regarding the formulation of an eu approach on foreign investments in strategic sectors—a topic which will be of growing relevance in the coming months. it is vital to regularly reassess whether we have all the tools to respond to the changing landscape of globalisation. in this context, the epp proposal and a letter by the three member states are an important contribution to the discussion. the council is of course prepared to act once a proposal has been tabled by the commission.
president-in-office of the council. – madam president, i would like to thank members for the opportunity to speak on the topic of child poverty. it is a very wide-ranging issue that must remain on our european agenda. the challenges are common to all member states. however, their extent, dimension and context differ from one member state to another. this is why the actions to be taken at national and local level vary accordingly. the most recent eurostat data from 2015 speaks for itself. children are most disproportionately affected by poverty. they are the age group at the highest risk of poverty or social exclusion. an estimated 27% of children in the eu 28 are concerned, as compared to about 25% of adults and around 17% of the elderly. the causes of child poverty are diverse and often multiple. the five main factors are: type of household, monetary poverty, the level of education, a migrant background, and living conditions. but, despite member states’ commitment to the europe 2020 poverty reduction target and despite steps being taken to fulfil this target, two further drivers have triggered a general increase in poverty and social exclusion. i am referring to the economic crisis and the phenomenon of inherited poverty. therefore the fight against child poverty needs to be tackled in its largest possible scope. this means combating exclusion and fighting inequalities in the spirit of the eu charter of fundamental rights. what measures have been, and should be, taken? let me first state what is self—evident: no child deserves to be left behind. in our actions we should be led by the overarching concept of investing in children. our responses need to be multidimensional. in december 2015, this house adopted the written declaration on investing in children and, in february 2016, you followed up on this initiative with a stocktaking exercise. in this context, the council adopted a set of conclusions on an integrated approach for combating poverty and social exclusion including child poverty. furthermore, the new commission initiative on the european pillar of social rights encourages us to further reflect on the provision which sets out a right for children to be protected from poverty. these issues are very much on the agenda in the council and will be discussed tomorrow in the council’s youth working party. the council is also looking forward to the launch and implementation of a preparatory action on a child guarantee as announced in the social pillar framework. in this context, i would like to thank parliament for its continuous commitment to maintaining the issue of child poverty on the agenda.
member of the commission. – madam president, i share your concerns about child poverty, even though since 2010 the proportion of children at risk of poverty or social exclusion in the eu has decreased slightly from 27.5% in 2010 to 26.9% in 2015. however these figures remain worrying, i would say, unacceptable. that means that more than a quarter of all children in the eu, around 25 million, are growing up in a household which either earns less than 60% of the median income, suffers from material deprivation or is a quasi-jobless household. child poverty is a multi-faceted issue that demands an integrated and, to the extent possible, preventive approach looking at both the employment situation of the household and the rights of the child. as you know, fighting child poverty is primarily the responsibility of the member states. however, the commission and my colleague marianne thyssen support their efforts through all the instruments that are at our disposal. first, policy guidance, including through the european semester’s country-specific recommendations, and second, our eu financial instruments; the european social fund (esf), the european regional development fund (erdf), and the fund for european aid to the most deprived (fead). of the latter, the funding possibilities for children in the existing eu financial instruments have been significantly strengthened. for the current programming period, approximately eur 21.2 billion of the total esf allocation was earmarked for social inclusion measures, of which eur 3.9 billion has been set aside for access to services, including childcare and eur 8 billion was earmarked for measures to prevent early school leaving. moreover, around eur 11 billion of erdf funding is planned for measures promoting social inclusion and combating poverty, while around eur 6 billion are planned for investment in educational infrastructure. the eu funds are also supporting healthy nutrition for children with the eu school fruit scheme: eur 150 million to ensure the distribution of fruits and vegetables in schools in 2015 and 2016. all these financial interventions are extremely important for preventing and breaking inter-generational transmission of poverty and social inclusion, which is of course the major factor. moreover, the newly adopted european pillar of social rights puts children at the forefront of social europe. it acknowledges the need to improve children’s life chances and to enhance children’s rights in the european union. principle 11 of the pillar recalls that children have the right to protection from poverty and that children from disadvantaged backgrounds have the right to specific measures to enhance equal opportunities. it also recognises that children have the right to affordable early childhood education and care of good quality. the commission trusts that parliament will join together with the council in the proclamation of the pillar by the end of this year. this builds upon many of the policy instruments’ elements contained in the 2013 ‘recommendation on investing in children: breaking the cycle of disadvantage’, in particular those on parents’ access to adequate resources, activation and quality services on 26 april the commission published a staff working document to take stock of the progress achieved by member states in implementing this recommendation. the experiences collected throughout the last four years show the areas in which good progress has been made and those where there is still room for improvement. therefore, it should provide a useful guidance for further action at local, national and eu level in the field. moreover, underpinning the relevant principles of the pillar, the commission has adopted an initiative on work-life balance, which should contribute to a larger and fairer participation of women in the labour market, which is also a key factor since, ultimately, the employment status of parents is one of the key determinants of child poverty. this initiative will continue to reduce the number of children who live in households at risk of poverty. one last word about the preparatory action for a child guarantee proposed by this parliament. we will launch a study that should initially analyse the possibilities of such an action to improve the situation of particularly vulnerable children in the european union, i.e. children in institutions, children left behind by parents working in another eu country, disabled children and children of migrants and refugees. let me once again assure you in conclusion that this commission is committed to addressing child poverty across the eu by restoring economic growth and upward social convergence, fostering job creation and ensuring social fairness. for the 40% of children in some of our member states that live in households at risk of poverty, we need to come with effective solutions to foster employment of their parents and to ensure their full participation in society. child poverty and social exclusion not only have a negative imprint on a child’s future but are also a huge waste of potential that our ageing societies cannot afford. we must on the contrary build on our children.
on behalf of the ppe group. madam president, all reports show that since 2008 the level of child poverty has been increasing in a large number of member states. in my country, belgium, which is by far the most affected, 25% of young people are actually on the verge of poverty. this is unacceptable. it is not possible, moreover, to isolate child poverty situations from the more general level of poverty. the fight against poverty must be and must remain a priority for the european union. this poverty of children is all the more unacceptable because it will affect them in the long term. the consequences are dramatic in terms of health, education and integration, and will mark the child and set him on a course that will handicap him throughout his adult life. combating child poverty, intervening quickly to break this negative spiral, means avoiding having to devote significant resources to integration policies later on, but above all it means enabling every human being to live with dignity, the dignity that must be that of our entire society.

on behalf of the group of the european right thank you, mr moscovici, i listened to you very carefully, but i have to agree with many of the previous speakers. the commission, led by mrs vestager, has always been a fervent defender of a free market with fair competition. as a true 'iron lady of denmark', mrs verstager fought against multinationals, cartels and dishonest companies. but today, in this case, i find that her zeal has the opposite effect. in a case against three member states, it argues that member states should tax port institutions as if they were purely commercial companies. that is a bad decision which will undermine employment, growth and investment in our flemish and european ports. i wonder whether this is a secret strategy to interfere in the taxation of the member states or whether the real intention is to bring our ports to the ground. our port companies do perform public tasks and therefore the state aid rules cannot be applied without further ado. with this attitude you are undermining the international competitiveness of our ports to the benefit of shanghai, singapore, hong kong and so on. and for you to punish three countries for a practice which is being applied throughout the eu is selective and anti-european.

budgets everywhere, that we have to deal with fewer defence personnel everywhere. and, moreover, the tasks we are asked to perform to respect and guarantee our security require ever more specialised citizens. that is why i hope that you will take this report to come up with that directive and those standards. then it's also clear what private security companies are allowed to do. more defence for less money. because it may be clear: we're not going to use private security companies to take over the military's duties. of course, that cannot be the intention. but we can use private security companies to perform certain non-military tasks in field operations. then we can control them very well. mrs mogherini, i would very much like us to take this report to heart. i would like to thank all my colleagues very much. we have worked very hard on this report and i hope that it will be adopted by a large majority tomorrow.
in writing. precarious employment is increasing. the number of workers with indefinite and involuntary parttime contracts has increased considerably after the financial and economic crisis. at the same time, we can see more fake self-employment, more poverty in work and an erosion of labour rights. it should be remembered that precarious employment not only harms the individual but also has considerable costs for society in terms of tax losses and higher public expenditure. that is why we have decided to reiterate our call for a directive on decent working conditions.
for the ecr group despite malta being the smallest eu member state, the maltese presidency managed to make some progress in areas that our citizens are aware of. in the area of asylum, migration and security, malta has made a strong effort to curb illegal migration flows from africa to europe. malta, for example, advocated training the libyan coastguard so that they could stop the migrants. all this to prevent african fortune seekers from illegally crossing the mediterranean sea via libya to europe. in addition, malta has managed to significantly increase the number of persons returning voluntarily. the figures prove that a strong and sustained approach to the migration crisis remains necessary. last week, as many as 10,000 economic migrants made the crossing from libya to italy in just two days! i therefore hope that estonia will continue to give priority to tackling the migration crisis, building on the work of malta. it is actually quite shocking to find that almost two years after the outbreak of the migration crisis, our external borders are still wide open and we continue to rely on non-eu countries to guard our borders. malta also managed to align all 27 member states on etias. this is a us-style system to pre-screen all non-eu travellers who are allowed to travel to the eu without a visa. the idea is to keep criminals out. in short, a small country that managed to make its mark on the european scene.
for the greens/efa group mr president, i would like to begin by raising a point of order, if i may. mr president of the commission, i myself come from a small state, and i fully understand your indignation: you are right to point out that the head of government of a small state is entitled to the same respect from this house as a head of state or government of a large state. nevertheless, i believe it was your responsibility to deliver your speech as you intended to do and therefore not to respond to one lack of respect with another lack of respect. that said, i'm coming to my point. mr muscat, i will begin by defining socialism as: 'a theory aimed at transforming social organization to a goal of justice between people in terms of work, pay, education, housing, etc.' i will not teach you anything, mr muscat, by saying that fair taxation is an absolutely crucial lever to achieve this objective of social justice. what have we seen from the maltese presidency in this respect? first, there was a great silence. in the priorities announced, the words 'taxation' or 'fiscality' were conspicuous by their absence. but was this surprising, given that malta, with an effective tax rate for multinationals of 5%, is, like belgium, the netherlands and luxembourg, part of the european tax haven club, at least for multinationals? then the panama papers and even a maltese government agency exposed the involvement of important political figures in the country in fraud, tax evasion and money laundering. so it is not surprising that the maltese presidency is not pushing the fight for tax justice, is not taking advantage of the opacity of trusts, which are one of the most essential vehicles in this phenomenon, or is not willing to shield senior european politicians from the scrutiny of the banks. in fact, your government prefers tax certainty to tax justice. this is the subject of the only initiative taken by your finance minister on taxation. let's get this straight. this is to give multinationals and, therefore, their managers and shareholders, visibility into the taxes they will have to pay. we are told that this visibility is being called into question by the flood of new rules being drawn up in the wake of the many scandals we have seen in recent years. in other words, rather than responding to the anger of those who are victims of tax injustice, you think it is more important to reassure the holders of capital. mr muscat, who would have guessed, given this record, that you are a socialist?
the rapporteur. madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the matter which will be put to the vote this afternoon is a crucial one, because we are really at the crossroads of european fiscal construction. the question is: what kind of europe do we want? a europe of fiscal justice or a europe of deregulation? a europe that treats its citizens and businesses alike or a europe that encourages tax aggressiveness between its multinationals, smes and start-ups to the detriment of its citizens? the country-by-country reporting is directly linked to tax transparency. i think it is really an additional tool to fight all the scandals we have known. first of all, i would like to thank the commission for taking the fight against tax evasion seriously. since the beginning of the parliamentary term, and particularly at the instigation of commissioner moscovici, a set of proposals has been put on the table and concrete measures have been adopted in this area. i am thinking in particular of the directive on the mandatory automatic exchange of information on tax returns, the directive on the rules for combating tax evasion and the directive on money laundering. at the same time, parliament has not been left behind, since we have also worked on this dossier, whether through the taxe 1 and 2 reports or, soon, the future recommendations of the committee of inquiry into the panama papers, which is further evidence of the need to legislate in this area. commissioner, you proposed a text to us, which we have amended. i would like to highlight five key elements in this report. the first is the threshold for enterprises, which is set at 750 million per year. we would have liked a threshold of 40 million, or no threshold at all, so that all multinationals could be put on an equal footing. but we have reached agreement on this threshold, and on the need for the commission to carry out an impact assessment after four years to assess whether this threshold should be lowered and whether the list of criteria is sufficient in relation to the objective pursued. the second element, which was strongly requested by civil society, concerns data: it will be published in a common register accessible to all free of charge. the third element is the list of criteria, such as the name of the company, the number of full-time employees or the value of assets, which will be published. this will enable the competent authorities to detect any errors or fraud. the fourth element concerns sanctions. each member state may decide to impose sanctions on companies that do not comply with the directive and therefore do not publish their data. finally, the last element is the full disaggregation of data, as already provided for in action 13 of the oecd beps project. this means that data will have to be broken down by country, sector and year. this is a key element. a safeguard clause has been added to protect sensitive business data, because we all here in parliament understand that we must also protect our multinationals. but unfortunately we have a difference of opinion on this point as regards the definition of the safeguard clause, because we socialists believe that the companies concerned can ask a member state for a derogation not to publish data which would be commercially sensitive, but under four conditions. the first is that this derogation must be applied for again each year. the second is that its relevance must be assessed and examined by the european commission to avoid problems like swissleaks. if the latter considers that the request for a derogation is unfounded, the undertaking must publish the data. as regards the third criterion, we believe that, when this derogation is terminated, the undertaking concerned must publish the data which were the subject of the derogation retroactively. finally, we think it is important that there should be a time limit for derogations. for us, this limit should be the tax prescription in each member state so as not to give a bonus of amnesty to potential cheats. i think it is really essential, for better tax justice, that we can know where the biggest companies make their profits and how much tax they pay in each of the states where they are active. without this information, tax fairness will never become a reality. every euro of tax not paid by multinationals is a euro too much paid by households. we must restore trust and tax justice in europe. i have to thank my colleague, evelyn regner, who is my co-rapporteur, and all the fictitious rapporteurs, but, madam president, i will come back to this in my conclusions.
multinationals in each country have to disclose how much profit they make, what their turnover is, how much tax they pay, how many people they employ. we've agreed to this internationally. we've entered that, that information needs to be delivered to the irs. now the european union wants to make that information public too. i think we can all agree that we are against tax evasion. of course, multinationals should not have more privileges or advantages than our own smes. and of course, we need more transparency. more information needs to be made public. at the same time, i would like to call for realism and against naivety. because in all fairness: it's always the same song. we have to be very careful about the way we use our money. that puts our companies at a disadvantage here in europe. they must do something and foreign companies must not. if we want to create jobs in our own country, we must be very careful about that.
vice—president of the commission. – madam president, i would like to thank everyone for this debate and also to take the opportunity to thank the rapporteurs, ms evelyn regner and mr hugues bayet, for their dedicated work, and all the members of this house who continue to bring corporate tax transparency closer to reality. we must continue to work together as the eu to achieve public tax transparency and, at the same time, we must be mindful not to jeopardise our competitiveness. we believe that our proposal strikes the right balance and hope that we will get this proposal agreed as soon as possible.
the rapporteur. madam president, as rapporteur, i would like to thank all my colleagues for their speeches. as i said when presenting this report, we are really at a crossroads in the construction of european taxation. our debate demonstrates this, and i am glad that we can vote on this text this afternoon. i hope it will have the largest possible majority. i would just like to make a technical reminder, because it is clear that every politician can defend his ideals, but i think it becomes problematic when you say the opposite of what is actually written in the texts. perhaps it is a matter of understanding, but as far as competition is concerned, i would ask all those who have doubts to read article 48b (3) again before the vote, which provides that any non-european company which has a subsidiary in europe is subject to the same conditions of publicity. this means that an american or asian multinational company with a subsidiary in europe will have to publish exactly the same information as european companies. so there will be no distortion of competition and i know of no, absolutely no, multinational company with a turnover of €750 million, which has no subsidiary in europe or which could lose a market of more than 500 million consumers. i think that there is a misunderstanding of this article. finally, i hope that i have been able to convince you, with all humility, of the need to vote for the safeguard clause, which is the most transparent, because i really want to ensure that the political world can never again be blamed for future tax scandals. i would like to conclude by thanking all the fictional rapporteurs who worked with evelyn and myself for their work, their perseverance and the understanding and respect they have shown during all the work on this dossier, which is - as you all know - technically and politically difficult. finally, madam president, ladies and gentlemen, a special thank you to my colleague evelyn, with whom it has been a pleasure to work.
in writing. civil society is calling for public transparency extending to all subsidiaries of multinationals in all countries. at present, multinational companies still take advantage of the international tax system to arrange for their almost complete immunity from corporate income tax. the imf estimates the loss of tax revenue due to these tax-optimization maneuvers by multinational corporations at $600 billion a year, and $200 billion a year for developing countries alone. in the united states, president trump and the republicans have succeeded in abolishing all forms of tax transparency for multinational corporations. we must not follow in their footsteps.
on behalf of the alde group. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, president tajani has given me the honour of speaking at this ceremony of tribute, because simone veil was a great lady from the liberal and democratic family. she belonged to the generation that preceded me and guided my commitment, the generation that europe has seen the worst of its history, but which has been able to make the best of a destiny shattered by war and deportation. simone veil, born jacob, of an old french jewish family, was 16 when she was deported to auschwitz with all her family. we were only a year away from the defeat of nazism, but the death camps were still running at full speed. only simone and her sister survived that hell, where they saw their father, mother and brother disappear. she'll live her whole life with the number 78651 tattooed on her arm. the holocaust, its horrors and its memory will, i believe, forge the strong and whole personality of simone veil. as a young married woman, she studied law, at a time when women were in the home, and quickly became a renowned magistrate. when she was appointed minister of health in 1974 by valéry giscard d'estaing, no one would have bet a former french franc on this senior civil servant to get the president's flagship promise, i.e. the decriminalisation of abortion, to be voted on. the french government in the 1990s. it would be too long to list all the achievements and successes of simone veil, who was to chair the liberal group from 1984 to 1989. parliament had few powers at on behalf of the ecr group. – mr president, commissioners, colleagues. these are turbulent times. we face challenges which spread far beyond the borders of the european union. we face global challenges which connect us all and require a global response. we face a humanitarian crisis, conflict at our doorstep and a barely recovering economy. this is why we need the summit to be fruitful. talk is cheap, and our electorate wants to see results. unfortunately, leader summits have become better known for updating the family photo than for plotting a strategic course for the next twelve months. leaders need to start looking at what they can realistically achieve at a global level in the next few months, and whilst the eu is willing to take on more and more responsibility on the world stage, the eu cannot start believing that it can solve every crisis by itself. we do not need an eu which thinks that just one policy can solve the migration crisis, but an eu which works with the un, nato, and the g20 to end the funding of terrorism and to punish the barbaric practice of human trafficking. the eu needs to sit at the table and commit to making the eu as global-facing and pro-free trade as possible. the eu needs to champion less regulation and lower taxes, not more red tape and bureaucracy. we need an eu which lives up to its obligations to protect the environment without stifling business, because it is jobs and growth which draw people out of poverty and prevent unsustainable economic migration. it is jobs and growth which bring countries closer together and gives people more self-confidence and respect. that is what we need to better connect us as nations and to help us move forward with confidence, unity and renewed vigour in the coming months.

i'm the reporter. madam president, our globalised economic system is based on fragmented and mass production processes. most of the goods we consume today are the result of a combination of operations carried out in different parts of the world. sometimes for the better: complementary research, innovation partnerships, resources and raw materials to be sought in a respectful way. sometimes for the worse: cheap, unprotected labour, environmental destruction, tax evasion. these operations are often coordinated by international companies through contractual partnerships or multiple subcontracting. the european union, as the world's leading exporter and importer of goods and services, can - and i would even say must - use its trade policy as a real lever to turn these global value chains into tools for sustainable development for all, and thus contribute to a fairer and more equitable globalisation. at present, the lack of transparency and the dilution of responsibilities throughout this process increase the risks of exploitation of workers, non-respect of fundamental rights and promote unfair competition on a social, environmental and tax level. the report also comes at a time when european trade policy is facing strong criticism from citizens who are calling for fairer and more transparent globalisation. the commission is therefore called upon to take a series of measures to make global value chains more accountable and transparent. to name just a few, i will focus, for example, on the inclusion in trade agreements of a binding but also enforceable sustainable development chapter, as well as the clause against tax evasion and tax fraud. there are also initiatives inspired by conflict minerals legislation or the duty of vigilance of the enterprise within a binding duty of vigilance framework as set out by the oecd, or the establishment of a labelling system that ensures a production method that respects international labour and/or environmental standards. i would like to mention, for example, the initiative taken by italy today to label the origin of tomatoes produced in a respectful manner. many other important elements are also reflected in this report, such as initiatives and the role of the private sector, multilateralism and end-of-life management. this report is the result of long negotiations between the different visions of how to manage this globalisation. we have, i think, struck a good balance in pursuing a common objective, which is to make trade a tool for emancipation for all. a colleague of ours recently quoted the wise words of simone veil, who said: 'the search for compromises must lead us to a solution which is higher than the particular position of each individual'. this report, in my view, illustrates this research. i thank the fictitious rapporteurs, and particularly mr ruas, who is here, for having found compromises that were acceptable to all of us. i hope that many of us will vote for this report tomorrow.
