president-in-office of the council. – mr president, it appears that on 20 july the belgian authorities informed the other member states and the commission about their findings of residues of fipronil in eggs. since then, the commission and the member states have been carrying out investigations and taking all necessary measures to protect consumers. it is now clear that the residues result from an illegal use of the product. while the overall responsibility for the investigations and protective measures remains with the commission and the member states, the presidency of the council has been following developments closely. on 5 september, at the informal meeting of the agriculture ministers held in tallinn, commissioner andriukaitis provided the ministers with an overview of the state of play and of the further actions planned. after this presentation, ministers held a discussion including, among other things, the need to ensure an efficient circulation of information in such cases. indeed, since the first contamination cases were reported, concerns have been raised, including in parliament, regarding possible weaknesses in the eu’s rapid alert system and the speed of information sharing. the question of how we can strengthen cooperation and the exchange of information between countries in order to deal more efficiently with such crisis situations will be further discussed at the high level ministerial meeting organised by the commission in brussels on 26 september, and we are grateful to the commission for taking this initiative. the outcome of that meeting will be presented to agriculture ministers at the october council session on 9—10 october this year. the ongoing investigations and follow—up discussions should give us a clear overall picture of what precisely happened and why, what went wrong and what can be improved. we will need this full clarity for our future debates. the council has consistently acknowledged the importance of food safety, high—level protection of public health and the prevention of food fraud. in its conclusions on the role of law enforcement cooperation in combating food crime adopted in 2014, the council encouraged the commission to continue its work on the fight against fraudulent and deceptive practices along the food supply chain and to develop any further action necessary for high-level protection of public health. furthermore, during the negotiations leading to the adoption of the new official controls regulation that entered into force on 27 april this year, parliament and the council agreed to reinforce the legal provisions to fight such practices. those reinforced provisions do not just include general rules. they also provide for the designation of european union reference centres for the authenticity and integrity of the agri—food chain, reinforced administrative assistance and cooperation mechanisms between member states, and enhanced coordinated assistance and follow—up by the commission. as i already mentioned, it seems clear that the present fipronil contamination is the result of fraudulent activity. there should be zero tolerance for such fraud. however, the infestation of hens by red mites is a real problem for many farms. therefore, we must also make every effort to ensure that legal and efficient ways to prevent and treat that infestation remain available. let me conclude by calling on all of us to support and encourage the actions taken by the commission and member states to ensure the safety of our daily food, to avoid these types of fraud and to resolve them efficiently whenever they occur.
member of the commission. – mr president, let me start by thanking you for this possibility to provide the house with the latest update on the recent contamination of eggs and egg products with fipronil. let me begin by assuring you that public health and food safety are always considered a top priority for me and are treated as such by the commission. coherently with this approach, the commission took immediately very seriously the fipronil issue. the situation continues to evolve, but i think it is important to clarify right from the beginning the succession of events. in november 2016, the dutch food authority was informed of the illegal use of fipronil in poultry but since there were no analytical results indicating the presence of fipronil in eggs or chicken meat, and also no danger to public health, the source of the problem was not established. tests on samples kept from september 2016 that revealed the presence of fipronil were done only recently and are under investigation by prosecutors from three countries – germany, the netherlands and belgium – to check these historical samples to assess what was known, when and by whom. prosecutors will help to answer these questions. on 2 june 2017, following self-controls by a belgian food business operator, the belgian authorities were notified of the presence of fipronil in eggs and started initiating an investigation to understand the source of the problem. as a consequence of this investigation, on 6 july the belgian authorities initiated a request for information from the netherlands in the administrative assistance and cooperation system. this is a different system, related to rasff. this system allows member states to liaise bilaterally or multilaterally to confirm rapidly suspected cases of fraud and is only actively monitored by the commission when a ‘european commission coordinated case’ is created. belgium initiated a normal case, not a european commission created case, which means that the commission was not an addressee of the notification and no follow-up was expected by the commission. once the belgian authorities had identified the source of fipronil in eggs and the possible extent of the problem, they notified the european union rapid alert system for food and feed (rasff) on 20 july 2017. therefore the commission was only informed of the fipronil contamination on 20 july. but as soon as the issue was notified through the rasff system, the commission took control of the situation, focusing on managing and minimising the consequences of the contamination. having said that, i want to start by stressing two points. firstly, that the risk to human health remains very low and has been successfully contained thanks to the measures which have been taken at european union level. and secondly, this incident is clearly the result of a criminal act resulting in food fraud. this is not to minimise the incident, as it relates to illegal use of an unauthorised substance, which is totally unacceptable, but this is not about a health risk issue. all contaminated eggs have been withdrawn from the market and the eggs from the few farms with acute risk levels have been recalled from consumers. so all in all, consumer safety has not been compromised. nevertheless, it appals me that the criminal actions of a few could jeopardise the integrity and reputation of our entire food chain. those criminals must be investigated and punished. so where are we now, today? the situation evolves every day, as you know; 26 member states and 23 non-eu countries have been affected by the contamination. since the very beginning of this criminal situation i have been systematically in contact with my counterparts in the member states most concerned. i have also asked for certain gaps in information to be verified and for detailed analysis and common views to be presented to me. i spoke with ministers for germany, the netherlands, belgium and austria. in response to the finding of fipronil the commission requested that specific measures be taken at national level; in particular we have instructed via rasff that the following measures be taken. first, on 31 july we shared information on applicable maximum residual levels and the measures to be taken as regards illegally treated farms. second, on 7 august member states were requested to be vigilant with respect to the possible use of other unauthorised substances used to control red mites in poultry establishments. third, on 16 august we reminded all member states that any past distribution of contaminated eggs, egg products or chicken meat had to be timely notified to rasff in order to ensure the appropriate follow-up by the authorities of the country of destination. apart from the immediate risk management actions, the commission has also taken additional important steps. on 21 august, a note on fipronil contamination was shared with our global partners to ensure transparency. the commission has also been in direct contact with a number of non-eu countries in order to limit the impact on the eu’s export capacity. on 30 august, we organised a meeting of the standing committee on plants, animals, food and feed, which agreed on an eu-wide monitoring exercise to investigate the possible use of illegal substances, including amitraz, in egg producing farms. i personally informed the european union ministers of the situation, collectively and bilaterally, in the context of the informal meeting of the agricultural and fisheries ministers last week in tallinn. finally, to avoid any potential side effects on the food chain, we are asking the competent authorities to ensure that contaminated food or animal bypass products do not find their way into animal feed and thus back into the food chain. let me assure you that my services are working hand in hand with those of commissioners malmström and hogan to ensure that the commission’s response remains coordinated, comprehensive and forward-looking. we also met with both the committee on agriculture and rural development and the committee on the environment, public health and food safety, where i was yesterday, and their suggestions, alongside yours today, will inform the ongoing response. now however we need to collectively examine what lessons can be learned and how we communicate and exchange information. this issue will also be discussed by the chief veterinary officers who will meet later this week, on 13 and 14 september. in addition the jrc will organise proficiency tests for official control laboratories in september. with this in mind, the commission is organising fact-finding missions in early october to the four member states where the original contamination occurred to gather information on the actions taken. this incident highlights the fact that we need to strengthen the way the european union networks dealing with food safety and food fraud are used. it is precisely for the reason of providing follow-up at political level that i have taken the initiative to call a high-level ministerial meeting on 26 september where we can reflect and agree on, first, how best to strengthen the way the eu networks dealing with food safety and food fraud are used, and second, how to improve coordination between the administrative assistance and cooperation system and the european union rapid alert system for food and feed. bear with me now a moment while i touch on the eu’s rapid alert system for food and feed which has been subjected to some misplaced criticism of late. the system was created in 1979 in response to the import of oranges contaminated with mercury. since then, rasff has ensured that the commission and member states were able to respond effectively to several crises, including crop contamination after chernobyl in 1980s, mad cow disease in mid-1990s and the more recent detection of e-coli bacteria in sprouts in the summer of 2011. rasff has identified hazards involving mycotoxins, heavy metals, allergens and pesticide residues. when it is used correctly and when it is fed with clear, rapid and reliable information by the control authorities of the member states, this system works. in practice, the rapid alert system for food and feed is mobilised when a member state notifies the system of a danger to public health. as the system’s manager, the commission verifies the notification and immediately communicates it to the other member states, and this is precisely what happened in the current situation. indeed, the use of rasff, in combination with food traceability provisions, has allowed us to quickly find and destroy the relevant food products in the eu. affected farms have been blocked and will remain so until member states are certain that their production is safe. let me be clear therefore, thanks to rasff multiple food-safety risks have been averted before they could harm european citizens. in 2016 alone almost 3 000 original notifications were transmitted through the system. many of these were alerts requiring and resulting in rapid actions. the original fipronil notification has led to nearly 600 follow—up alerts. while it is true that every system can be improved, it is worth stressing here that our analysis should focus more on the efficient use of, and interaction between, rasff and the administrative assistance and cooperation system rather than on their reliability. with this goal in mind, the commission is developing a common platform for these systems and exploring the possibility of a single network of member state authorities. now that we have a new official control law, i think this will help us to be more effective. let me finish by emphasising once more that close coordination and the steady flow of information between all parties is essential, especially in this case. we have constructed the european union’s food system and food fraud systems and networks together; now we must work collectively to maximise their potential and safeguard the highest standards of food safety for european citizens. parliament will be important in this regard and i am keen to hear your views on the matter today.
on behalf of the ppe group thank you, mr president, commissioner, thank you for the explanation. i think that the fipronil crisis has shown us, as you yourself have said, one thing very clearly, namely that there is a lack of information exchange between the various national agencies. we agree on that. that food safety information just needs to be shared automatically and shared quickly. i think we have now lost weeks, perhaps months, before that information could go smoothly from one member state to another, from one agency to another. that early warning system, to put it mildly, didn't work very quickly. that is at least the perception, and it is urgent that it be changed. it is good that you promised last night in our committee and here to come up with concrete measures to help eliminate this. those national alert systems should be directly linked to the european rapid alert system. that's got to be one oiled machine. that is what you say and we count on you to do so and to put that into practice at the european council on 26 september. one more thing, one more point: member states also use different criteria and different timing to inform consumers. this leads to a great deal of confusion among consumers and producers. i think we can do that much more efficiently and we are counting on you, commissioner, to propose and implement concrete measures in that area too, very soon.
the fipronil scandal is a case of manifest fraud. i think it is important to emphasize that in the first place. as in the horse meat fraud scandal of 2013, we are dealing with criminals who, for the sake of profit, are defrauding consumers at the expense of the sector itself. we need to get these fraudsters in their wallets. if you cheat on purpose, you're gonna have to pay for it. secondly, we must learn the lessons of this scandal. i am in favour of a sober debate, not a headless chicken. there's no benefit to anyone from pursuing a lawsuit against a food agency. at european level, the main challenge remains the rapid and effective communication between member states and despite the alert systems, it appears that important information was shared too slowly, too insufficiently. there is a different way to do it, and there is a different way to do it. to restore consumer confidence, we need to punish fraudsters, prevent fraud and deception and redirect the use of the eu alert system where necessary.

there are some 80 million disabled people in europe and some 150 million elderly people. any legislation that improves the accessibility of products and services is a step forward that benefits millions of people in europe. i therefore welcome the løkkegaard report, although i think it should have been a little more. i like to break a lance for more ambition. for example, inspiration for this can be found in the united states. there, the americans with disabilities act created a real cultural shift. not only the government but also private companies of all kinds see accessibility as an opportunity rather than an obligation. i think, for example, of apple, which launched its latest iphone yesterday. apple understands that responding to the needs of people with disabilities can be a competitive advantage in a hyper-competitive market. and there are many more examples of this. i hope that tomorrow's vote will be a meaningful step forward, a step towards a world in which people with disabilities and the elderly can participate in everyday life without facing barriers that impede participation.

on behalf of the verts/ale group. – mr president, dear british citizens, my fellow european citizens, the clock is ticking. you know where we greens, and the european free alliance, wanted your country to be: to remain at the heart of the european union. but the majority of those british citizens who voted decided otherwise. while we accept the result of the referendum, this decision will produce no winners. not only the business we do with one another but also the interests of both uk citizens and citizens of the eu 27 will be harmed by the separation. british citizens come to see me every week, to alert me to the damage that will be inflicted on your healthcare system or on the quality of research in your top universities, where so many eu nationals are working day in, day out. above all, both british citizens in the eu 27 and uk citizens on the continent have been thrown into total disarray, and europeans in the uk have started experiencing administrative vexations before brexit has even started. if we want to limit the inevitable damage that will be caused by this lose-lose situation we should waste no more time, and negotiate in good faith. but make no mistake about it, no deal by march 2019 would be the worst deal for everyone and, firstly, for our respective citizens. allow me at this stage to point to two contradictions in the position of your own prime minister. the first concerns ireland. how can she say, on the one hand, that there will be no border and, on the other hand, that she will take the united kingdom out of the customs union, the single market and the jurisdiction of the european court of justice? this will create a border, and i do not know where to place it while at the same time respecting the good friday peace agreement. the second contradiction is this idea that in order for britain to become global it needs to cut its ties with its closest neighbours, with whom it shares history and geography. i believe that, rather than being an impediment to britain being global, the european union is the only way for it as a european country, and one of a number of former imperial powers, to have any influence in a world where it represents barely one percent of the population. in the 21st century, more than ever, size matters. let me conclude with a few words on democracy. as your prime minister explained, the decision to leave the european union is a statement as to how you want your democracy to work. i take exception to the idea that only if the uk leaves the eu would decisions that affect your daily lives be taken by people directly accountable to you. who adopts eu law? on the one hand, this european parliament was elected by, and is accountable to, the very same people who elect your national parliaments. on the other hand, the council is 28 governments who are accountable to their citizens via their national parliaments. in other words, every eu law is decided by people who are directly accountable to you sitting at the table and taking part. but let’s be frank with one another: what seems unacceptable to some is that, in certain cases, britain might be outvoted. that, i am afraid, is a rule of democracy, and one that your own government has no qualms about invoking by remarkably overruling the pro-remain majorities in scotland and northern ireland. if democracy can be had only at the level of the nation state, i know many regions which would immediately aspire to become one. mr president, commissioner, it is clear that the development of low-cost airlines has opened up air transport to millions of europeans and, as a result, has generated thousands of jobs through the development of certain airports and their region. in my region, charleroi, the airport is one of the greatest economic successes of belgium in recent years, and this has been demonstrated to a great extent. but the present situation requires us to reaffirm the protective principles defined by europe, which must necessarily accompany this development. firstly, the rights of passengers must be fully protected. the rules are clear: any airline that cancels a flight must inform each passenger of his or her rights, including the right to compensation under certain conditions. therefore, commissioner, i think you should take the initiative to facilitate this compensation procedure and control the process. the clear example of ryanair shows that this does not work. secondly, the social rights of the crew must be fully respected. this is particularly the case with the recent judgment of the european court of justice on the national jurisdiction in matters of labour law. again, the device must be secured. we are of course counting on you, commissioner, to change both of these elements.
mr president, this question of the definition of endocrine disruptors is essential. who reported in 2013: 800 chemicals alter the human hormonal system. these are 800 substances that are responsible for or suspected of increasing a whole range of diseases: certain cancers, diabetes, obesity, infertility, as well as various disorders related to brain development. this means that the public health issue is a major one, but member states are divided on the issue of identifying these criteria. the compromise reached at the beginning of the summer is the best proof of this, with its plethora of exceptions. in going beyond its powers, and bas has explained it perfectly, the commission has excluded from its definition of pesticides those which are specifically designed to target the hormonal systems of insects and plants with considerable damage to those around them which are not targeted. the endocrine society, which brings together 18,000 researchers and clinicians worldwide, is expressing its deep concern. citizens in europe are mobilized. we are therefore calling for a new definition of endocrine disruptors tomorrow, a definition which will actually protect public health and the environment.
in writing. endocrine disruptors, substances which disrupt the body and cause adverse effects, cannot be authorised under any circumstances. unfortunately, under pressure from some member states, the european commission has, in the text which is to be submitted to us, gone far beyond its mandate by adding a derogation authorising pesticides. the reason: the purpose of these products is precisely to prevent pests from reproducing. but what about human beings? so after the glyphosate saga, now comes the endocrine disruptor saga? no, i'm not going. in both cases, the precautionary principle must continue to prevail! that will be the meaning of my vote, as it has always been. let's just drop the design-disruption clause. let us ask for the withdrawal of the implementing regulation and for a new proposal to be tabled without delay. once the text is adopted, i hope, the european commission will have to withdraw its draft and revise its copy in negotiations with the member states. and we will prove in passing that, no, "brussels" does not decide everything.
the 1998 directive was the first european law on passenger ship safety in the eu. the aim is not only to ensure that the safety of passengers on board passenger ships is not endangered by exceeding the maximum number of passengers allowed, but the directive also requires that search and rescue operations after an accident should be efficient. this directive is now being updated. i followed up this matter in the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs and tabled a number of amendments. i am glad that many of my proposals have been incorporated in the final version adopted by the committee on transport. the obligation to communicate the nationality of a seafarer to the master of the vessel was introduced alongside data protection principles. this will help to provide reliable information to relatives more quickly in the event of an accident. therefore, the identification procedures were simplified. identification always takes time, and so the sooner the better for anxious relatives.

mr president, commissioner, i do not think it would be desirable to include the stability and growth pact in the treaties, at least not without a fundamental change to the pact. if the tscg is to be replaced by a treaty which encourages the economic and social convergence of european countries and, more particularly, of the eurozone countries, it can only be done by avoiding, of course, the adoption of measures such as those which have led to the intensification of austerity. at the moment, let us admit it, the commission is only analysing budgetary data, but no overall vision is envisaged. however, it would be much more relevant to take account of all indicators, whether social, environmental or economic, and not just budgetary ones. by taking only part of the data into account, the tscg prevents member states from investing and, as we all know, without investment, no economic recovery is possible. this simply means fewer new jobs and more precariousness for european citizens. the amendment of the tscg is therefore an essential condition for providing quality jobs to the greatest number and combating growing poverty in the european union. the fact that the budget is seen as an entity disconnected from the lives of europeans has, as you know, created unacceptable inequalities in europe instead of providing solutions to reduce them. so have the courage to end austerity. we can see the damage every day. the return to public confidence requires a major change in social and economic direction. i think it is about time, commissioner.

, on behalf of the alde group. mr president, the maldives: sunshine, coconut trees, white sand beaches, turquoise waters and hotels like paradise. the postcard is idyllic on the front, but on the back, it's sharia law that dictates its law. the death penalty was reinstated three years ago, including for children. president yameen's regime has the "right" to execute only those sentenced by a majority. there are many cases of intimidation and murder of journalists and bloggers who defend freedom of expression: ismail hilath, yameen rasheed and all the others. lawyers were suspended for daring to call for the rule of law and mohamed nasheed, the first democratically elected president, was sentenced to 13 years in prison. the maldives is also one of the largest suppliers, proportionately, of jihadist reinforcements to the islamic state, and i haven't even mentioned women, with the public stoning of adulterous women, early marriage and rape. the list goes on and on. under the beach, it's hell! so you might as well know that when you click and book your vacation. a million tourists a year is a massive deterrent when diplomacy fails.
the proposal for a regulation under discussion today aims to ensure the functioning of the internal market for fertilising products, while ensuring that ec-marked fertilisers meet the requirements of protection of human, animal and plant health and environmental safety. while fertilisers are obviously crucial for agricultural production, their use can also have a negative impact on the environment and food safety, especially when they contain contaminants. at present, fertilisers are regulated at european level by the 2003 regulation. however, the latter only covers a part of the fertiliser market, since about 50% of all fertilisers on the european market are excluded from its scope. the existing regulation mainly concerns conventional fertilisers, so-called 'inorganic' fertilisers, which follow a linear economy model, since they are extracted from raw materials. the proposal for a regulation, on the other hand, initiates a shift towards a circular economy, as it aims to increase the reuse of raw materials that are currently disposed of as waste. putting organic fertilisers on an equal footing with conventional fertilisers can reduce waste and the environmental impact of fertilisation and agriculture. it is estimated that the recovery of nutrients already present in the european union, for example in the form of waste, could replace about 30% of the inorganic fertilisers currently on the market. this has been mentioned previously. this is a very technical issue, with a complex division of powers between the various committees involved. my colleagues and i have worked together to produce a text which i believe is well balanced. i would also like to thank the rapporteur, mr ţurcanu, who succeeded mrs gall-pelcz, who had done an excellent job, and my other fictitious colleagues for their cooperation. i therefore hope that tomorrow's vote will confirm the achievements we have made through the committee on the internal market and consumer protection. however, at the moment i am concerned about the possible reopening of the nitrates directive, which was a red line for my group from the outset and which, by common agreement, was not to be considered in this report. the 'nitrates' directive comprises two main elements: the principle of balanced fertilisation and the obligation to limit the use of manure, which avoids intensive livestock farming and promotes a rational agriculture. this is neither the place nor the time to rush to amend essential legislation to combat soil pollution. i would add that if the nitrate amendment were to pass what has been proposed at various stages of the dossier, i would instruct my group to vote against the whole of this report. i don't think we should take that chance. as regards the difficult question of cadmium thresholds, the compromise supported by my group in particular seems to me to be the most balanced solution, since it allows the precautionary principle to be applied in this area, while giving industry time to comply with these new requirements.
mr president, my party, the nva, is pulling hard to make our economy more circular. a level playing field between organic and inorganic fertilisers can strengthen that cycle model. flanders has been working on the biological cycle for 25 years. in 2015, over 800,000 tons of biowaste was converted into high-quality compost, which i like to call black gold. in addition, 2.2 million tonnes of other biowaste streams were converted into organic fertilisers. today we can incorporate these flemish best practices into european legislation by opting for ambitious european end-of-waste criteria for bio-waste. biowaste is a valuable resource with a lot of economic potential, but today still a third of it is lost in the garbage bag. we need to turn the tide by separating bio-waste and recycling it. this will enable us to replace a large proportion of the non-organic fertilisers. my delegation also supports the ambition to reduce cadmium gradually.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat the use of these substances, and i would like to thank the rapporteur for his report. for example, the substance acrylylfentanyl is a mouthful of a substance 50 times more potent than morphine. since the summer of 2016, the substance has killed 40 people. and yet, chinese manufacturers continue to sell it online. it is also legal in some member states. over the past decade, the number of variants of psychoactive substances at european level has increased dramatically. for example, in 2015, on average every three days a new substance was reported in the eu ews. the problem is that the current european system is outdated, because it is only aimed at classic drugs. it is also not a question of taking action quickly, hence the proposal for a directive which will be put to the vote tomorrow. under this new directive, counterfeit drugs are no longer excluded from the common european approach to the fight against drugs, with the ultimate aim of avoiding problems in police and judicial cooperation in the union. the new directive will significantly reduce the timeframe for rapid and effective action, making the eu's early warning system much more efficient. everyone agrees that dangerous designer drugs should be banned and removed from the market. but today any dangerous substance that europe wants to ban must go through a cumbersome legislative procedure. precious time is wasted. for example, tomorrow parliament is due to give its opinion on the substance furanylfentanyl. dealers get a free pass in the meantime. people get seriously ill or even die. and when the substance in question is finally banned, a new variant is already on the market. this is how we fight the beer dock. with these rules, we want to put an end to this. it is high time we tackled the harmful drugs that make big money.
in writing. whistleblowers are essential in a democratic society; however, they are often threatened with legal proceedings and reprisals in the workplace. national laws differ from one country to another and are often inadequate. we are calling on the european commission to come forward with a legislative proposal to ensure the protection of whistleblowers at european level by the end of the year. the paradox today is that whistleblowers are threatened, persecuted and prosecuted, while at the same time those who do are relatively protected. warning whistleblowers are the sentinels of our time, and when they serve the public interest, they must be properly protected.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, first of all, i want to thank president tusk for his intervention, and also for the publicity he has made for my hashtag campaign. thank you. i am a proud european, so you are also an adept of it, mr tusk. and i have to tell you, after the state of the union of our president of the commission a few weeks ago, we got a speech at the sorbonne by emmanuel macron, and we now have your agenda for the coming european councils. i have to confess that each time i have heard these proposals – this state of the union, your agenda – i have felt an enormous excitement. the same eagerness that i felt when i was a young boy and was browsing through the colourful catalogues my mother received at the end of every year – the 3suisses, was that in france? i think also in luxembourg it exists. i think it is the otto in germany. the shiny things in these catalogues seemed always so far away, dreams on colourful paper, and to be very honest, that is rather what i am feeling today again – that we have great plans, that we have huge ambitions, but are we really ready now in this european union to make the radical changes needed to make it happen? because, mr tusk, there are a number of structural flaws in the european union that we need to solve. the first, as we know, is the unanimity rule that is still applied in the european union, and especially in the council, which has been blocking progress for decades now. the european arrest warrant has taken more than a decade to implement because of the unanimity rule; with the european patents we had to wait more than 25 years because of the unanimity rule; the same is happening for the energy union – we do not have an energy union because of the unanimity rule; we do not have a fully-fledged banking union because of the unanimity rule. the americans were capable in nine months to react to the financial crisis by cleaning up the banks, with an investment programme of usd 900 billion and with quantitative easing of usd 1.2 trillion, and we, after nine years, are still debating all this because of unanimity. maybe it is a good moment to recall that, because with the posting of workers the council has taken a position for once. why? because they did not apply unanimity. for once, they have voted in the council and have reached a position. so that is the first thing to do. are we ready? there is a proposal by jean—claude juncker to massively use ‘passerelles’ to solve the problem. are we ready to finish this unanimity rule inside the council and inside our institutions? the second flaw is structural weakness: we know that there is a long, endless list of things, of doubts, of earmarks, of exceptions in our institutions. are we ready to finish this europe à la carte instead of continuing it as we still do? finally, the third disease we need to cure is what i call the ‘potemkin syndrome’ of the european union: that we always make beautiful plans, but without creating the necessary instruments, the necessary tools, or the necessary budget to make it happen. schengen without the border and coastguard; the euro without the treasury; foreign policy without a european defence community. so the moment of truth, mr tusk, is coming on the day – which is coming very soon – when we will decide on the new multiannual financial framework. are we really ready to implement these ambitious plans of jean-claude juncker, of emmanuel macron, and of your agenda? then it is necessary that we also be ready to open this financial framework and to give more than the former 1%, which is ridiculous if we want to fulfil all the ambitions that we have today. it is not really even 1% if you deduct from that the contributions that we received from the member states and are going immediately back to the member states; it is only 0.14% to protect our citizens against external threats, to protect our citizens and our borders internally, and at the same time to also organise the currency union. with 1%, sorry, that is not possible. to those populists and nationalists who are always saying ‘oh, this european superstate’, to have a budget of 1% and then talk about the european superstate is ridiculous and makes no sense at all. but that is the moment of truth. the moment of truth will not be the whole list of summits; the moment of truth will be when we discuss the money – when we discuss the budget, and when we discuss the new multiannual financial framework. that brings me, finally, mr tusk, to the remark you made on the composition of parliament and the announcement that there will be an informal summit on it on 23 february. fantastic! maybe there are other things that i could organise to have an informal summit about, but you have to know that the legislative initiative for the composition of the parliament is of this parliament and not the european council. i have to tell you that. result of a thorough exercise, an exercise in parliament's monitoring and control of how the money of the european budget, and therefore of the european citizen and taxpayer, has been spent over the past year. were the correct procedures followed? can you confirm how much money was spent? and moreover, did the resources used ensure that the citizen got what he paid for? the starting point of any good and proper discharge should be that everyone who spends money from the european budget should be monitored in a transparent way as to how it was done and also whether it was done in a responsible way. in accordance with the treaties of the european union, the european parliament is responsible for this exercise. the council shall make a recommendation to the european parliament. unfortunately, since 2011, the european parliament has been refusing to grant discharge to the council, on the grounds that there is a power struggle between parliament and the council, and also on the grounds that parliament has the habit of asking all the institutions to which it is required to grant discharge questions, both in writing and orally. the council takes a purely legalistic view and considers that the european parliament should only grant discharge to the commission. that's not in line with practice. parliament asks questions to the commission, asks questions to the secretary-general of the european parliament, asks questions to the economic and social committee, to the committee of the regions, to all the agencies. the council, on the other hand, refuses to answer our questions. the council is therefore refusing to engage in dialogue. it has to be said i admit it: there's been progress. the council is increasingly presenting information on its website, which is helping us to find out little by little what is happening with the budget of the council, the council of ministers and the european council. the council of ministers has not yet adopted a decision on the budget. let 's face it . only dialogue will lead to a solution in the end. i therefore met the secretary-general of the council at the beginning of my mandate as rapporteur. mrs gräßle, chairman of the committee on budgetary control, did the same. we wanted to know what the contours were of a modus vivendi, of improving relations between parliament and the council when it comes to granting discharge. after long and extensive discussions with my shadow rapporteurs, in close consultation with the chairman of the committee on budgetary control and in close consultation with the coordinators of all the groups in the committee on budgetary control, we have reached an agreement. that agreement was presented to the conference of presidents last thursday and since then we as the committee on budgetary control have had a mandate to negotiate with the council of ministers, myself as rapporteur, mrs gräßle as chairman of the committee on budgetary control and in the presence of klaus welle, secretary-general of the european parliament. what are these contours? what's the warrant? the council and the european parliament have different roles in discharge. the european parliament gives discharge. the council shall deliver an opinion, shall make a recommendation to the european parliament on the discharge. the second. the recognition that the council and the european parliament must do so on the basis of correct facts and correct information. the third. we ask the council to say that they will reply to the written and oral questions put to them by the european parliament. in return, we in the european parliament say: if you, the council, have questions for the european parliament, we are prepared to answer them in a form of good reciprocity. the result of this mandate and of the negotiations should be that the tension between parliament and the council is resolved and that we can deal with each other in an open and transparent way, so that the citizen also learns during the discharge exercise how the 540 million euro which the council spends annually is being handled. i hope, mr president-in-office, that our offer will be one which will enable us to enter into dialogue, to finally resolve our conflict and to resolve a dispute between the council and parliament in a proper way.

president, ladies and gentlemen, the new digital entry and exit system, known as the ees, is intended to make it easier to track who is entering or leaving the eu. because everything is digital, it should be smooth. this will certainly benefit tourism to the eu. the ees shall record the date, time and place of entry and exit of travellers. for example, visa holders who stay longer than they are allowed in the eu are more likely to run into trouble. the ees is also important in the fight against terrorism. the system will allow identifying foreigners who have destroyed their documents or whose passports have been stolen. it's also a detection tool, because suspects can be traced through the point of entry or through their fingerprints. the key now is to keep all databases up to date and to share information effectively. that is why the n-va fully supports this proposal. the security of our citizens and the prevention of illegal residence are absolute priorities for the n-va.
madam president, commissioner, my group - and i thank my colleague, angelika mlinar, for having said so right away, despite her personal reservations - is in favour of the agreement between the european parliament and the council. in our view, the reinforcement of the identification of persons entering and leaving the schengen area is a necessary corollary of freedom of movement within this area. with the help of the entry/exit system with automatic gates, we hope - and this will undoubtedly be the case - to reduce waiting times at border controls, and to improve the quality of these controls by making it easier to detect document fraud and identity fraud. this will allow us to automatically calculate the length of stay allowed for each traveller and to check whether it is being respected. of course, i am aware that following the opinion of the court of justice on the pnr agreement with canada, it was legitimate to raise questions about the compatibility of certain provisions of the entry/exit system with respect for the charter of fundamental rights. these questions, i repeat, were relevant and legitimate. however, i believe - and the overwhelming majority of my group agrees - that after the analysis of the conclusions made by our legal service, we can proceed calmly to the approval of the agreement reached.
madam president, commissioner, a european union without internal borders is of course only possible with strong, smart external borders. that is why i can only applaud this new entry-exit system. the system of stamping passports at the borders, the long queues, will be over, and we will be able to give the bona fide travellers quicker access to the territory. but what i also want to stress is that we can use this system to stop and track the travellers, the illegal migrants, the terrorists, much better. we will encourage the exchange of information and, above all, we will ensure that strong external borders will enable us to maintain our present system of free movement within the european union. we shall be supporting this wholeheartedly.
in writing. the new border control system for travellers to the schengen area allows us to strengthen the protection of the external borders and close the gaps in the network. until we know who's entering our borders, we can't guarantee the safety of our citizens. this automated system replaces the manual stamping of passports. the enhanced border control system records biometric data (fingerprints and facial scans) and entry and exit data of the more than 50 million travellers who travel to the schengen area each year and compares this information with data from europol, european and national databases. it allows for the immediate arrest of suspects with false identities at the border. we are thus making it impossible for terrorists to enter the schengen area unnoticed and then move from one member state to another without being detected.

president. - the next item is the report (a4-0173/97) by mr pimenta, on behalf of the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection, on the proposal from the commission to the council (com (97) 0173 - c4-0173/97). we do not want boat refugees to be automatically brought to the eu. we do not want to see illegal entry into europe rewarded with residence rights and benefits. by the way, pushback was possible in the mediterranean before 2012, but in 2012 the european court of human rights put an end to it. this is how the dogma of the right to asylum in europe was born. it is therefore virtually impossible for frontex to monitor the european borders and stop the influx. in addition, the european parliament recently decided to oblige the member states to transfer 200 000 refugees from the middle east each year. it was also decided that in return no pressure should be exerted to conclude a take-back agreement. this means that illegal immigrants and/or criminals cannot be sent back either. the vote on this in the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection was not entirely in line with the rules, with thanks to the left, which is always so adamant about respecting those rules. and that while germany and austria are voting to introduce an absolute maximum limit on the reception of refugees - something the nva has been advocating for years and also an idea that is constantly dismissed as impossible by left-wing politicians, but now it is entering, albeit only in national politics for the time being. fortunately, we can still count on the member states to provide us with a more realistic view of the situation.
i would like to thank mr hökmark for his efforts in drawing up this report. i also thank the other shadow rapporteurs for their positive and productive cooperation. the result is a clear compromise that can be supported by a large majority. every year, we spend a lot of time analyzing our economy and trying to figure out how we can do better. the main outcome is the country-specific recommendations. i would therefore ask the member states to implement it. of course, there are many different views on many measures, but a high debt burden is a risk and a mortgage on the future. we must ensure that our children and grandchildren do not have to pay the bill for this. we don't have to spend less, we have to spend differently. we need to invest more in research and innovation to remain competitive. modernise the economy, reduce administrative burdens and reduce the burden on labour, in order to attract more investment, which will stimulate growth. investing in innovation will secure jobs and prosperity for the future. countries that are doing less well, either in terms of budgetary performance or in terms of competitiveness and productivity, need to make more efforts. emphasis should be placed on education and skills. and financing for companies and smes needs to be improved. on the other hand, member states that do well should certainly not be punished. finally, i would like to emphasize the following: the basis of an economy is a healthy balance between income and expenditure, and therefore a healthy tax system. this is the responsibility of the member states. the semester is a document that gives a broad picture of our economy. employment is part of this, as are many other aspects. in future, economic and monetary affairs should remain the basis of the report.
mr president, the loss of confidence in the european market following the 2008 crisis, but also the reduction in public spending, have had a direct effect on european growth, which today remains slow and, as you said, mr commissioner, is not inclusive growth, given the great disparities in europe today. in the case of employment in particular, although there has been an increase in the employment rate, it is clear that this increase has not affected all countries or all regions of europe. it is particularly noteworthy that it is accompanied by more precarious jobs and has not reversed the unemployment curve for young people. today, some regions of europe have a youth unemployment rate of over 30%. then it's true that we must offer better. we have put forward a few proposals: first, to increase public and private investment, which creates jobs in regions that are not benefiting from the effects of the recovery; secondly, to give importance to education and training in the context of the european semester; third, strengthen the 'youth guarantee'; fourth, to stress the importance of collective bargaining in improving the purchasing power of workers; fifthly, to combat income polarization and to strengthen social protection systems. and finally, work on work-life balance. all this must be possible through discussions in the employment, social policy, health and consumers council (epsco), which i believe should be associated with the european semester.
when banks repackage a lot of loans to then sell them to other financial players, we call that securitization. very complex, but very important. for example, if you watch the movie "the big short", it explains very well how that effectiveness was part of the basis of the financial crisis in the united states. a good movie by the way, a highly recommended one, oscar winner too, a movie with ryan gosling and with christian bale. but we must be careful with these financial tricks, these techniques, and that is why i think it is really important that the european parliament should propose measures to make this easier and, above all, more transparent. that is the only way to ensure that our financial system becomes stronger, becomes more stable, and at the same time we can ensure that sufficient resources are still being pumped into our economy and in particular into our smes.
mr president, on 17 december 2003 the european parliament took a major step forward in protecting the environment by adopting this directive on environmental liability. protecting nature and biodiversity while focusing on innovation, on the technologies of the future, is clearly in the dna of our parliament, it is clearly in the dna of european liberals. it was my friend and former dutch liberal colleague, toine manders, who was the rapporteur at the time and who was in the process of putting this community system for the prevention and repair of environmental damage on the water table. whether it's gmos and the risk of unintentional contamination of conventional or organic crops, or the carbon market, created in 2005 to reduce greenhouse gases, the polluter pays principle applies every time. in its 2016 implementation report, the european commission acknowledges that while the 2004 directive is effective in repairing environmental damage and encouraging prevention, it has not yet - to put it diplomatically - realised its full potential. as mrs girling has pointed out, this is a question of observing the application of the directive, and as our rapporteur has said, there is sometimes a gap between theory and practice. mrs ferrara, who has done an excellent job - i want to thank her - spoke earlier of a non-homogeneous and superficial application. i wrote down his words. this is true for damage to protected species and natural habitats. it is known how the 'habitats' and 'birds' directives can be circumvented by some member states. the same is true for damage to water resources. another category covered by the directive is soil pollution, which can have serious consequences for human health. here again, there is room for improvement. progress which - it is important to say - can also be achieved through prevention. a very recent example: we voted here two days ago against the renewal of the glyphosate programme. i do not want to reopen this debate and i do not want to go back to the carcinogenic nature of the substance, but to its impact on the soil. simply avoiding it would therefore be the most environmentally responsible approach, namely prevention. in conclusion, i would say - as others have said here - that we can of course strengthen environmental law, but for me the urgent thing is to apply the polluter-pays principle concretely and fully in european policies and also - this is our paragraph 29 - it is important, and this is what mrs ferrara proposes - to set up this european fund for the reparation of environmental damage, because the victims are counting on us.

for the greens/efa group mr president, one does not prevent the other. when the european council meets, we discuss it before and we discuss it after. i think that for a summit of this importance, this is the least we can do.
it is good that there are common criteria so that people who want to work in inland navigation or who want to develop in such a profession can have their skills recognised more easily. the eu qualification certificate can then open doors where in the past there were different separate schemes. in flanders we have grown thanks to our waterways and it is time we rediscovered the possibilities of transport by water. 80% of flemish farms are located within 10 km of a navigable waterway. the european inland waters are also still under-exploited. as a sustainable alternative to road transport, inland waterway transport can also contribute to reducing the environmental impact of transport. i have seen firsthand how entrepreneurs in flanders are working in the sector, responding to the strengths of inland navigation with innovative solutions. but future growth depends on the availability of qualified personnel. now the average age is 55. in order to make inland waterway professions more attractive, the new directive is therefore welcome in my view.
the figures have already been mentioned by the commissioner: every year, 25 500 people die on our european roads and 135 000 are injured. this is a tragedy for the people involved, for the families and for our society. the safety of road traffic and the reduction of road fatalities are also our top priorities. as this report rightly points out, the integration of intelligent driver assistance systems in vehicles can certainly contribute to this. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect, and i am sure that the commission will be able to support them. i have questions, however, about expensive european studies on binding targets for road checks or, for example, for european intervention in cycling infrastructure, which are asked for in the report. such initiatives, such decisions can be taken better by the competent authorities in the member states, at national or regional level, where they better meet the needs of the specific context and road situation and the needs of the road users there. flanders, the nation i represent, will provide the proof. the flemish minister for transport is succeeding in reducing the number of road deaths year by year. so tailored policy works. let us therefore work with the member states and the regions to build a safer europe that really saves lives.
in writing. i am in favour of new road safety devices becoming standard equipment. we would like to see driver assistance systems such as speed control, automatic braking systems and lane keeping assistance systems made compulsory on all new vehicles. the figures speak for themselves: 25 500 people die on european roads and 135 000 are seriously injured on our roads every year. the european parliament therefore votes in favour of technologies that make driving safer. among the proposed measures, we would like to see the mandatory detection of so-called vulnerable users such as pedestrians, cyclists and motorcyclists; helping drivers avoid them would save many lives. more specifically for trucks and buses, we would also like to see front, side and rear cameras installed as well as curve assist systems which are very useful in reducing blind spots.

for the greens/efa group madam president, 2008 the ubs scandal, 2013 offshore leaks, 2014 lux leaks, 2015 swiss leaks, football leaks, that is the hsbc scandal, 2016 the panama papers and the bahamas leaks, 2017 the malta files and now the paradise papers. you have counted correctly, ladies and gentlemen, with these ten scandals, the vast majority of our citizens are victims, their loyalty card is full. what will they gain from the eleventh, can they hope for a fair taxation? assuming nothing here is illegal, which is doubtful, is it still legitimate? one thing is certain: tax injustice is not the product of a natural phenomenon, but rather the result of deliberate political choices. they are not unaware that for the last 30 years, the elected political majorities in europe have made our taxes less and less progressive and have been competing with each other for creativity in creating the gaps in which the profiteers of the system can burrow. but why, why, why, even today, those who govern us, as here in france, emmanuel macron, continue to pass tax laws that benefit the richest? of course, there are some who would like to lick the hand that feeds them, or perhaps will one day feed them. but above all, there's this delusion that making the rich richer and protecting mega-corporations benefits everyone. in reality, all this only strengthens their ability to extract a rent from our societies and the planet. this is why tax administrations are reduced to a dry regime, this is why accounting transparency for multinationals and for the beneficiaries of trusts, foundations and shell companies is still not the rule. this is why the harmonisation of corporate taxation is not progressing, and why tax havens continue to flourish, including within the union. and who is to blame for all this? tax evasion is a criminal offence, tax avoidance is not. in principle, everyone has the right to pay as little tax as possible, as long as he stays within the legal limits. and perhaps that is why we should amend the legislation as you propose, commissioner. but the crux of the problem is that while every individual and every company has the same rights, they do not have the same opportunities. a small and medium-sized enterprise in my region of west flanders cannot afford appleby's services. that's what i call class fiscality. some feel that the super-rich and the multinationals should retain the right to continue to slip through the net's maze because those profits trickle down to the average man, because that money does end up there. but that's outdated, the facts prove it. we have to make the political choice for fiscal justice, and if we make that choice, we have to do it together. but for that, we must all be involved, all the member states of europe. i was really astonished this weekend to hear dutch prime minister rutte announce that the dividend tax would be abolished. in one breath he added that the netherlands did not want to end up like belgium, and i quote: "an economic desert that has only one multinational left". the mistake is that this is a sovereign decision of one member state. of course, that's not the case. it's short-term thinking. it is blindly following the logic of multinationals, and the latter are the only real winners. how much outrage must there be before everyone realizes that this class taxation is eating away at the foundations of our society?

madam president, just a year ago the commission came forward with a first proposal to amend the european anti-dumping law. thanks to the hard work of our colleagues, but also thanks to a common front between the companies and the trade unions, we have succeeded in amending this legislation with a major trilogue. so in the future, when countries or industries resort to unfair practices, the european union will continue to have the possibility of taking calculated anti-dumping measures against third countries. but now - and this is a step forward - social and environmental standards will be taken into account in the assessment of significant distortions. the burden of proof will not be on the industries. the commission will therefore be evaluating and producing a number of reports, and this is also to be welcomed as a year. it is therefore a victory for the future of european industry and we must be able to celebrate its victories. now the fight is not over, because there is a second legislation, the trade defence instruments, which it is important to be able to renew. so we call the council because today there is a certain reluctance on the part of the council to change these trade defence instruments. we know that without this review we will not have the necessary efficiency to be able to protect some sectors of our european industries properly. it is therefore essential that the council act on these matters.

author. investing in nature and biodiversity must be at the heart of a smart, sustainable and inclusive europe. protecting biodiversity is not only necessary for nature itself, but is essential for our well-being and prosperity. for example, one in six jobs in the european union depends on nature and the added value of natura 2000 is many times greater than the costs of management. the erasure of the biodiversity strategy will cost us 50 billion euros a year. stopping biodiversity loss must be a political priority. the birds and habitats directives are a crucial link in this story and must be fully implemented. this is the key message of the biodiversity report that the european parliament adopted in february 2016 and for which i was rapporteur. at the end of 2016, the commission finally removed the uncertainty about the nature directives. the commission also decided that the directives were fit for purpose and that the focus should be on their better and faster implementation. the action plan for nature, people and the economy, which the commission proposed earlier this year, is undoubtedly a step forward. but at the same time, the plan is inadequate and insufficient to meet the biodiversity targets. without additional effort, we'll miss our 2020 appointment again. the environment committee has a number of clear and specific questions and it also wants to send a strong signal about what can and should be improved. the action plan contains only one reference to the biodiversity strategy. how will the commission respond to parliament's more general calls for the 2020 targets to be effectively met? on behalf of the group of the european right thank you, mr president. i fully agree, ladies and gentlemen, that greater equality and upward convergence must be a priority. great inequality hurts us all. it is a drag on economic growth and employment. but the way in which this report proposes to tackle inequality is wrong. first, the answer to inequality is not ever-increasing europeanization. the rapporteur wants, for example, a european minimum income scheme, a european unemployment scheme, a european directive on work-life balance, and so on. the report does not respect the principles of subsidiarity and proportionality. when member states are faced with the problem of social cohesion, they cannot - especially given the scarcity of resources - take sufficient account of the needs of their own citizens. the inevitable consequence of this is that we are alienating the citizen from europe even further. do we want that? secondly, i regret the repeated condemnation of atypical work. no, we absolutely must not create working arms. but having a job is still the best guarantee against poverty. atypical employment contracts allow people who otherwise would not be able to work to get a job. creating flexibility in the labour market creates opportunities and i am a strong believer in giving people opportunities, of course in combination with a good social safety net where necessary. finally, i regret that the youth employment initiative fails to help young people to find work in a structural way and leads to the opposite. it is a very important step towards the creation of a european employment strategy, which is a key element in the european union's strategy for employment. we need just focused and fundamental solutions to boost job creation and growth. i regret that the report has taken a different path. thank you, sir.
in writing. on friday 17 november, the presidents of the european commission, the european parliament and the council will sign a proclamation to establish the european pillar of social rights. equal opportunities, better access to the labour market, fair working conditions, increased social protection and inclusion are the different objectives that the european commission and the member states will have to pursue together. if the signing of this proclamation is a first step in the right direction, it will have to be accompanied by legislative initiatives at european and member state levels, as well as new financial means to achieve these objectives. otherwise, this foundation will find its place in the cabinet of beautiful promises. i am therefore pleased that parliament has voted largely in favour of this report on combating inequalities, which in my view contains many initiatives to be implemented within the framework of this european pillar of social rights. now let us turn these promises into concrete actions to improve the well-being of all european citizens.
the european parliament has been a very active participant in the process of negotiating the agreement on the european union. three and a half long years. our farmers have always been told: this crisis is temporary. it's the fourth damn crop we can't export to russia. it's not temporary anymore. it's structural. it's time to change the shoulder gun. the fruit growers have a structural problem. but you, commissioner, also have a structural problem if you want to keep your promise to support farmers. i think we should open our wallets to compensate our farmers for the damage they have suffered. they're the victims. we must also focus on structural solutions. because it is not the will and the labour of our farmers. the commissioner: many farmers are very happy. there are dramas. i do a lot of field trips. a lot of companies are on the verge of bankruptcy. farmers live in poverty. and what 's worse: in my area, many commit suicide. in our case, 60% of the conference peers went directly to russia. we cannot make up for that with small exports to new markets. i therefore ask you, commissioner, to move from temporary measures to solid structural measures. i'll help you with that. i hope that after this debate parliament will adopt a resolution in which we insist on real compensation for the damage suffered. i also urge an action plan for new markets. i would like you to speak to commissioner malmström about a real action plan to promote our fruit. we europeans can be proud of the best fruit and vegetables. let's promote that, too. i know that the european council is imposing sanctions on russia, but i would like you to speak to mrs mogherini to improve our agricultural relations with russia. thank you.
the russian boycott has indeed dealt a severe blow to the fruit and vegetable sector. for our flemish growers - and i am thinking in particular of the perennial growers - it is a disaster. in order to absorb this initial shock, to guarantee income for farmers and to ensure continuity, crisis support is absolutely necessary. but a structural solution is not. especially when we know that a resumption of exports to russia is not for tomorrow. you mentioned the figures, commissioner. so far, 1.7 million tonnes of fruit and vegetables have been bought up for many tens of millions of euros. i must say, and many people agree with me, that i find it difficult to accept that for that money quality products end up as compost or animal feed or are not harvested. that is a matter of concern to everyone, and first and foremost to the growers themselves. i therefore prefer that we devote our resources to exploring new markets, to introducing and promoting strong quality labels, to selling our fruit rather than destroying it, and to thinking about alternative models that do not encourage overproduction and waste. there 's only one condition for that: the commission has also proposed that the european union should be able to provide a single market for the whole of the eu, with the cooperation of all parties - politicians, growers, exporters, processors and consumers.
president. - ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, farmers are the victims of this embargo. the european intervention was welcome, but often insufficient to cover the costs of high-quality production, with the conférence peer in mind. it costs more to produce a porsche than, with all due respect, a lada. secondly, a market of 130 million consumers doesn't just replace you. pigs keep growing, dairy cows keep producing milk, and the growth of lettuce, potatoes or apples is not just going to stop. the result: the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat the problem. for pears, for example, this rises to 25 percent. whether it is ukraine, russia or brexit, the farmer is always being presented with the bill. fortunately, our farmers are creative. some have invested in other varieties. it has been tailored to a growing middle class in asia and south america and the increasing demand for fruits and vegetables there. it worked. this year, for the first time, more than 10 million kilograms of belgian apples reached the indian market. but it is also of the utmost importance that we learn from our mistakes. if 40 percent of your exports go to one country, like russia, you know you're taking a risk. however, not all sectors learn at the same rate. the pork quota that went to russia is going to china today. we are giving one country the power to plunge our pig sector back into crisis with the snap of a finger. my suggestions to you, commissioner hogan, are therefore clear. please continue your structural policy, because yes, mrs vautmans, there is indeed a structural policy. continue to support exports, but take into account the needs of each sector. evaluate trade by sector and then open new outlets by sector. learning to diversify and invest, that's my credo.
member of the commission. – madam president, i thank all of the speakers who have responded to the questions initiated by mr siekierski. in relation to the russian embargo, you can hear from the members of the committee on foreign affairs here this evening – for which we are grateful that they attended, including the chair, mr mcallister, and the rapporteur, mr preda – that there is a difficulty in relation to lifting the embargo and the russian sanctions are going to remain in place. this is going to have a knock-on effect of retaliatory measures on the russian part in respect of farming and agricultural products. i do not see any political sign that this policy on russia that we have in the european union at the moment is going to change politically. if it changes, it changes everything, but we have to be realistic that politically it is not likely to change in the short term. mr valcárcel siso was not reflecting on what i said in my contribution when i outlined all of the measures that are being taken within the budgetary framework available. eur 490 million has been allocated in the last three years. i have extended the measures until the end of june 2018 and we will review the situation again then. we have increased the rate of aid for the withdrawal of products from the marketplace since last july under the delegated acts. these are the issues that we have been improving. on ms petir’s notion that we need to have a crisis fund outside of the budgetary position, we have had a crisis fund of eur 1.5 billion mobilised for all sectors outside of the existing cap budget. i am sure you noticed this in relation to all of your sectors in terms of your farmers. this was a significant amount of additional money that ms georgieva – the vice-president of the commission at the time – and i were able to mobilise for the farming community, and member states were able to do more on top of that. i take note of the fact that we have not sufficiently replaced the markets that were available in russia from the fruit and vegetable sector. we have not been able to achieve the type of market options around the world to replace that particular market for fruit and vegetables. therefore, there are no initiatives on promotion to target the food and vegetable sector which i am prepared to look at. i do not want to give anybody the impression that i have not been doing anything in relation to the marketplace. i have been to china twice on trade missions, japan twice, canada, mexico, colombia, saudi arabia and iran last week, vietnam and indonesia, and i have been promoting all european products, including fruit and vegetable products, on trade missions and to companies throughout that time. i am probably the first commissioner to get outside the office and into the marketplace to try and replace some of the markets that were lost at the time of the russian embargo, and i am very happy to be able to do so. in relation specifically to structural solutions, i certainly hear what ms vautmans has said in relation to the belgian situation – that structural solutions are required. but in addition to the measures that i have already outlined and that we are implementing, i will certainly be prepared to consider what the belgian government is prepared to do in terms of state aids, in terms of modifying the rural development programme, for example, if it wants to look at structural solutions. in response to other members here that have mentioned that, certainly if there are structural proposals that we can look at, we will look at them in the context of modification of our rural development programmes. that is an option that is open to them. it is an option to look at state aid provisions within the ceilings of eur 15 000 per farmer as well. we are allocating and promoting fruit and vegetables in various ways, not just through our promotion programmes, but through our school schemes as well. an extra eur 50 million in addition to the original cap budget was mobilised last year, arising from the decision that was made here to promote our school schemes, which include fruit and vegetables. i had the pleasure of visiting pc fruit with ivo belet mep and tom vandenkendelaere mep. i was looking at the excellent work that pc fruit is doing there, looking at options and varieties and new consumer tastes and patterns in order to find new markets. some of the markets that we have had in the past, the products perhaps may not be suitable anymore in relation to these particular consumer markets, and we have to look at options and consider helping in the research area with companies like pc fruit in order to develop alternatives in terms of consumer tastes and to promote those types of new products. i agree that we have not solved the problem in relation to fruit and vegetables arising from the russian embargo. i respect the sentiments that have been expressed in relation to that here tonight, but i am also being open about the fact that solutions are not going to be easy, particularly the ones that involve removing the sanctions, which had the consequence of stopping the export of our products to the russian market. but i do not accept, mr marias, that i did nothing. you know i did a lot for your country as well over a period of time. maybe i will give you the full details in relation to what i did for greek agriculture in the last three years, including in the fruit and vegetable area, and you might be surprised.
