on behalf of the s&d group. – madam president, i would like to thank the commissioner for his statement on the state of play on the energy union. i think it is always interesting to take some time for that, and in my time today i just want to convey one message. i want you to pay a lot of attention to the strong message this parliament’s committee on industry, research and energy gave yesterday. we had a very important vote on two very important files. there was a clear majority, supported by nearly all groups in the house, to raise the level of ambition on the deployment of renewable energy in our union to a target of 35% by 2030 –that is 80% above the target in the commission’s proposal. our high level of ambition on renewables takes fully into account the recent spectacular cost reduction in solar panels and offshore wind. the international renewable energy agency (irena) has calculated that the eu can double its renewables share from 17% to 35% in 2030 in a cost-neutral way. if we accelerate our energy efficiency policies renewables can even deliver a higher share of remaining energy demands. this was also voted for yesterday with a less broad majority, a smaller majority. we voted for a 40% target on energy efficiency. i would like to ask the commission to pick the side of parliament because up until now the commission has come up with too weak proposals because of the situation in the council. now we have the opportunity, dear commissioner, to take up full responsibility and ambition and take the side of parliament.

the rapporteur. as 3 december is the international day of persons with disabilities, i would like to be able to discuss in this house my report on the implementation of the european disability strategy in this plenary session. i want to thank all the shadow reporters and the secretariat very much for wanting to meet this deadline with me. it's so important that we keep thinking about constraints and keep inclusion on the political agenda. after all, people with disabilities are not the sexiest agenda item, and very few politicians like to talk about it. and this is despite the fact that one in six people has some degree of disability. that is 80 million europeans! can we really afford to ignore this large group of european citizens? i am speaking of citizens, because that is what they are: european citizens. they should therefore be able to enjoy the same rights as everyone else. the right and freedom to go to school, work, go out to dinner, go to the theatre or the cinema. the right to travel abroad and enjoy their leisure time with family and friends. people with disabilities should not be treated as second-class citizens because of their disability. mr. president, i would like to ask everyone to please keep in mind that not every person with a disability is as fortunate as i am. they are not always able to tap into their full potential, take the job that they want, live where and how they want or even marry who they want. the eu is effectively one of the most advanced and richest regions on the planet, yet it is not inclusive enough. we unfortunately still manage to exclude so many of our citizens on a regular basis. the result is that we are losing out on valuable skills and potential labour force in the process, while they are more than ready and willing to contribute to our society when given the opportunity. let's not forget that money is lost due to inaccessible infrastructures, goods and services. therefore, the european disability strategy 2010-2020 is an essential tool in ensuring all our citizens are able to form an integral part of our society and contribute to our society. we need a post-2020 strategy to follow up and it needs to be both bold and effective, because if we don't get it right who will? it's time for action. let us all work together to make the european union more inclusive.
i would like to thank the shadow rapporteurs and the other members for their constructive input in today's debate. we all agree that europe must become more accessible in all areas that are really important to all of us, and therefore not just to people with disabilities, but to all of us. that means accessible education, employment, training, public transport, you name it, zoning, shops, media, the internet, goods and services. i would also like to mention erasmus+. we must not forget that the european union and almost all the member states of the european union have ratified the convention on the rights of persons with disabilities. but that's not enough. there must be action. here in this house, many fine words have been spoken about equal opportunities, equal rights for disabled people and also about the importance of inclusion for every disabled person. many member states recognize this, but let's face it: i am not saying that we should not be concerned about the future of the community, but that we should be concerned about the future of the community. in the united states, people with disabilities can go to school, they can get a job, they can get help to walk into a store, they can go to the bank. they can go to the cinema, to a museum, to a sports stadium; the list is endless. they can do so without barriers. but if we look at europe, the situation is sometimes crying foul. a lot of people can only dream of this place. the european union is a great example of this. not accessible! why this backwardness here compared with the united states? i think we should be more ambitious. people with disabilities still face many obstacles in europe. in the united states, they have long since been eliminated. why can't we do this here? why are those barriers still there? - aren't these people part of it? i think we should be brave enough to face the problems. you know that december 3rd is international day of persons with disabilities. so here's my question to you: put yourself in those people's shoes for a day. blindfold yourself, put earplugs in your ears, borrow a wheelchair and then come back and tell me why people with disabilities still face so many barriers. we can make a difference for them in europe. not just words, but actions! the adoption of my report will enable us to insist on its implementation in the member states and in the council and the commission. i think we must send a clear signal to the commission, the council and the member states that everyone counts. people with disabilities are also full citizens of europe!
the measures we are voting on tomorrow will really make a difference to farmers today. firstly - and this is very important - the member states will have the opportunity to give young farmers a boost. and that's what's needed. the number of active farmers is declining. there is no future for the farmer without young farmers. so more money for beginning farmers can really make a difference. second important point: the position of the farmer in the food chain. no fair price without a stronger position for the farmer. the agreement now allows the creation of producer organisations in all sectors. in the fruit and vegetable sector, we see that this approach really works. thirdly, and to conclude, there is this concern: the commission has already made a number of proposals. if the definition of an 'active farmer' is made optional, this would open the door to the misuse of the european agricultural cent, and undermine the agricultural policy. agricultural land used as a recreational area does not deserve a penny of aid. young, active farmers deserve it.
the european parliament, the council and the commission have adopted a number of amendments to the proposal for a directive on the protection of the environment by the placing on the market of certain dangerous substances. moreover, emissions are also increasing. the commission will continue to monitor the implementation of the paris agreement and its implementation in the medium term. flights within europe have been covered by the european emissions trading system since 2012. for international flights, that exception to the rule still applies. the text before us is to extend that exception for the very last time. we are giving the global icao agreement every chance of success, but we are not writing a blank cheque. the clear end date for the international flight exception keeps the pressure on the icao boiler and a stick behind the door. i would like to congratulate mrs girling on her thoughtful approach. the nva delegation will support the agreement in its entirety.

tax evasion is a criminal offence, tax avoidance is not. in principle, everyone has the right to pay as little tax as possible as long as he stays within the bounds of the law. the crux of the problem is that, although every individual and every company has the same rights, they do not have the same opportunities. an sme in west flanders simply cannot afford appleby's services. this is what i call class taxation. the panama papers and then the paradise papers revealed the extent of this. now we have to make the political choice for tax justice, and we have to do it together. the recommendations put forward by the pana committee are necessary, but as long as the member states do not follow the logic of fair taxation, the fight will continue with one arm. the commissioner also mentioned it in his introduction. the commission and especially the council must be involved. as a government, it is important to establish a legal framework without legal loopholes and with transparency as the main guiding principle. but a government must also know its limitations. we're not gonna win anything with a manhunt. nor is it a political game between member states. ladies and gentlemen, our reputation as a european parliament is at stake here. i therefore urge everyone to be aware of the impact that our recommendations will have. we must be ambitious, but we must not lose ourselves in unrealistic, populist proposals. the member states must be convinced and not pitted against each other. if we fail to do so, the member states will blindly follow the logic of the multinationals and they will be the winners in the end.

vice-president of the commission. – madam president, honourable members, it is very nice to see a full house here. it is a very important moment because, once you vote in favour of the extension of efsi, it means that you will raise its firepower up to half a trillion euros by the end of 2020. it is a significant tool for improving europe’s competitiveness and for increasing the number of jobs. i must say that the biggest challenge we still have with efsi is the lack of awareness. (the president called for silence)many companies, many regions, many municipalities and authorities have never heard of efsi, even though we have been communicating massively. if you look at the countries and regions which have benefited the most from efsi you can see that local authorities, and also private banks and meps, have been actively promoting the opportunities which efsi can offer. i can give you some examples of very active meps. i am sorry not to mention them all but, for instance, ms spyraki from greece, mr kelly from ireland, mr gualtieri from italy, ms kaili from greece and ms maydell from bulgaria have organised a significant number of events promoting efsi opportunities for local entrepreneurs and local authorities. i encourage you strongly to promote this good example. the second point i want to make is that efsi has not created a division between east and west. (the president called for silence)if you look at the list of the countries which use efsi most, estonia, bulgaria, greece, portugal, and spain are the first five, then you have finland, latvia, lithuania, italy, poland, france and croatia. so, efsi can be used everywhere but it depends on the activism of local authorities and entrepreneurs, and also meps, as to how well informed people are of the opportunities. i would like to stress the importance of combining efsi opportunities with structural funds because this would further expand the opportunities for cohesion countries to use efsi. my final point is that, since efsi is a demand driven fund, it is not the medicine for all diseases. we have to make sure that companies working in the circular economy field, in artificial intelligence, in agriculture and forestry, and in energy efficiency, to mention just a few areas, use more efsi financing, because it can give better opportunities to invest in new innovations. i would like, once again, to thank this house for the fruitful cooperation. commission statement as a result of the political agreement between the european parliament and the council on the financing of efsi 2.0 an amount of eur 275 million will be redeployed from cef financial instruments, which represents a reduction of eur 225 million in comparison with the commission proposal. the commission confirms that the financial programming will be revised to reflect the corresponding eur 225 million increase of the cef programme. in the framework of the annual budgetary procedures for the years 2019-2020 the commission will make the appropriate proposals to ensure an optimal allocation of this amount within the cef programme.
in writing. we are voting today on the interinstitutional agreement which aims to double the duration and the capacity of the european investment plan to provide assistance. this flagship initiative of the juncker commission was launched in late 2014 to evolve the eu's policy priorities after the financial crisis by focusing on a boost to investment in europe. today's vote aims to pursue it in such a way as to increase its impact to €500 billion by 2020 (and €630 billion by 2022). i would remind you that since autumn 2015, support of eur 250 billion has been provided to more than 500 000 enterprises, thus creating 690 000 new jobs. i welcome the fact that in this new version the criteria for additionality have been strengthened, so that projects which would not receive any, or only a small amount of, funding can be selected. it remains to focus it even more on those member states which need it most. i also welcome the opportunity to support innovative projects in the cultural industries and in the economy and social infrastructure. i can only stress the need to invest again and again in the social sector! it's an emergency.

- a writer. i fully agree with what christel schaldemose said. this debate is not about banning kebabs. this debate is about maintaining the current legislation which clearly prohibits the addition of phosphates to meat preparations. we therefore oppose the commission's proposal to allow this. yes, there are problems in the member states. yes, there are debates in some member states as to whether kebab is a meat preparation or a meat product. but then it is up to the commission to help the member states, to step up the checks, to guide them there. i have tabled an amendment to that effect which clarifies the situation. mrs sommer, you have led the resistance in germany and i very much blame you for doing so in a populist, unfair way. this is not right. the addition of phosphates to kebabs is not permitted. that's what the regulations say. there are only three reasons why the commission can depart from that and still include those phosphates in a positive list. mrs. schaldemose has listed them. it must be clear whether or not they are harmful to public health. there are enough studies to cast doubt on that. even efsa has not provided a conclusive answer to date and efsa has decided to carry out an overview study, which will be completed only at the end of december 2018. the precautionary principle then says that we should wait a while until there is clarity. secondly, we must be sure that there is an alternative. the debate in the press in recent days and weeks shows that there are alternatives, alternatives which do not add e numbers to kebab preparations, which are safe, which are healthy. if that is possible - and i have also tabled an amendment to that effect - then it becomes clear that the opposition of mrs schaldemose and myself is not in fact opposition to the kebab industry or to the workers in the german meat industry. finally, it is true that phosphates are also added to other products such as hamburgers, sausages and even soft drinks. if the efsa study says that they are harmful to public health, then you should remove them from the positive list.

for the ecr group when romania and bulgaria joined the union in 2007, their legal systems were not yet ready. they had to catch up and make the necessary reforms. the fight against corruption and organised crime also needed to be stepped up. the commission has since been monitoring the situation through the cooperation and verification mechanism, known as cvm for short. i have recently spoken with my ecr colleague macovei about the recent developments in romania. the government had issued ordinances to condone tax crimes and thwart police investigations and court cases, all to keep corrupt rulers in power. i asked the european commission to follow up the case. in this year's cvm report, romania was therefore counted out. the commission has also received comments from the united kingdom, the united kingdom and ireland. so too are the provisions on organised crime. the schengen area has no internal borders. it is therefore of fundamental importance that we avoid importing crime into our countries in this way. however, little progress has been made. yes, there are positive developments, but not enough. that is why my delegation, the n-va, is opposed to the accession of bulgaria, romania and croatia to the schengen area. there is a real need for more efforts before the political green light can be given.
president-in-office of the council. – mr president, honourable members, commissioner, to start with let me thank you for the opportunity to intervene here today on the european pillar of social rights. the pillar has rightly attracted a very high level of attention. on 17 november at the social summit for fair jobs and growth in gothenburg, our three institutions – the european parliament, the council and the commission – proclaimed the 20 rights and principles of the social pillar. the purpose of the pillar is to serve as a guide towards more efficient social and employment outcomes while taking into due account different socio-economic environments and the diversity of national systems. the tools for delivering on the pillar are in the hands of the member states, the social partners, civil society and the eu institutions. we must respect national competences, as well as the diversity that exists in europe. we must all do our utmost to make the pillar a reality for our citizens. this is our shared political commitment and responsibility. the pillar sets the framework for various legislative and non-legislative initiatives, including the first legislative proposal on work-life balance, which is currently under discussion in the parliament and in the council. the council is also looking forward to working with you on the proposal to revise the written statement directive. further initiatives are included in the 2018 commission work programme, more specifically the social fairness package with proposals on a european labour authority, access to social protection for atypical workers and a european social security number. as far as the implementation of the pillar is concerned, another very important instrument is the european semester for economic policy coordination. the european pillar of social rights also ties in with the discussion on the social dimension of europe taking place in the framework of the broader debate on the future of europe. during the estonian presidency, the council adopted political commitments to reduce gender segregation in education and employment to facilitate independent living and to respond to the ongoing changes in the world of work. we also reached a general approach on the european accessibility act. i would like to thank the european parliament for its strong commitment to a more social europe. your strong impetus helped to make the social pillar happen. we all agree on these principles, it is now time to put our values into practice. we look forward to working together as co-legislators in an endeavour to promote the social dimension, hand-in-hand with further deepening of economic convergence, implementing and enforcing the single market in all its dimensions by june 2018, just as our heads of state or government have repeatedly called for.
member of the commission. – mr president, on 17 november in gothenburg, the three european political institutions proclaimed our european pillar of social rights at the first social summit in 20 years. this proclamation is a unifying message to our citizens and to the world. in europe, we share important social values and we stand ready to defend a shared vision for a fair society now and in the future. the european parliament had indeed a major role in shaping the pillar, and i would like to thank the president of the parliament, the committee on employment and social affairs (empl), the president of the empl committee and the rapporteur ms rodrigues and the shadow rapporteur mr tom vandenkendelaere and the shadow rapporteurs of the other groups for their steering role and political commitment to the pillar ahead of the gothenburg summit. the pillar is our chance to reconnect europe with its citizens. as the commission president rightly said, if we want to be credible, we have to deliver. the time has come to make sure that the pillar’s rights and principles are effectively guiding our actions at european, national, regional and local level. the pillar is essentially a joint endeavour. its implementation should entail full respect of subsidiarity and the division of competences within the union, as well as of the role of the social partners in delivering employment policies in member states. this is the message that i have just sent to all ministers, social partners and all the stakeholders. i strongly encourage member states to continue their efforts to make the pillar a reality on the ground, in close cooperation with the social partners and the civil society at large. we, from our side, will make full use of the european semester. the european semester will be the key instrument to steer the process of social convergence every year, and we are starting now. the 2018 annual growth survey already makes clear that the semester will be key for delivering the pillar. the joint employment report includes the new social scoreboard to monitor areas covered by the pillar, such as the share of school leavers, the youth unemployment rate or the impact of social transfers on poverty reduction. the employment guidelines – as you know, the legal basis for the country specific recommendations – have been aligned with the principles of the pillar. we will now bring this forward in our bilateral contacts with member states within the framework of the semester, where we will identify together the main challenges. the commission is already doing its part to translate the pillar into concrete initiatives. we proposed an initiative on work-life balance, and i welcome the estonian presidency’s efforts to make progress on our proposal, and i count on you to deliver on this important file within the mandate of this parliament. as the social partners could not agree to start negotiations for an autonomous social partner agreement, next week we will present a proposal on the revision of the written statement directive. the aim of this proposal is to increase the rights for the most vulnerable workers. we want to ensure clarity for workers and employers on their contractual relationship, irrespective of the type of the contract. we are also working on a social fairness package, to be launched on 7 march 2018. this social fairness package will include a proposal for a european labour authority and one on a european social security number, to ensure that european union rules on labour mobility are enforced in a fair, simple and effective way. it will also comprise an initiative on improving the access to social protection of people employed on non-standard contracts and in various forms of self-employment. to achieve our aims, we also need a budget. the negotiations on the financial programming after 2020 are crucial. the european pillar of social rights should be a compass to guide us also in these negotiations. we count on the support of the parliament for working in this direction and to make the social dimension of europe visible also in the next multiannual financial framework (mff). we can only build a fair and a more social europe by working together. our common goal is to improve the life chances of every european. i count on you to help deliver on all our legislative initiatives that are implementing the pillar, and i count on all members of this house to be ambassadors of the pillar, and to tell your constituents that the european union stands up for the rights of its citizens.
for the ppe group the european pillar of social rights was signed at the social summit in gothenburg on 17 november. these 20 principles provide a basis for establishing a set of basic rules at both european and member state level. at the same time, a strong signal has been given that the european story is not just about the economy but also about the social, that we must move forward in both areas. and that's what's needed. the emergence of digital platforms and the economic revolution that accompanies them is upending all the established principles of our labour market. new forms of work are emerging. old jobs, like the man or woman at the checkout counter in the store, will eventually disappear and new jobs, like the deliveroo bike courier, will take their place. our social security and labour market policies really need to adapt to this. the question at the heart of this is actually very, very simple: who will protect these people? my answer is clear: then we have to. europe must do that and the european parliament must be able to do that. we must not only concentrate on fair competition, as we do in the case of the abuse of posted workers. we must also ask ourselves what the new labour market will mean for the european citizen. what about pensions, social rights, digital literacy? that is where the social pillar can point the way. that is precisely the function of the social pillar as a compass. i stand by the principles of that pillar: equal access to the labour market, the right to age in dignity, being prepared for digital challenges. but we need to be able to do more. we need to put it into practice.
for the greens/efa group mr president, in defining and implementing these policies and actions, the european union takes into account the requirements linked to the promotion of a high level of employment, the guarantee of adequate social protection, the fight against social exclusion and a high level of education, training and protection of human health. that, ladies and gentlemen, is the wording of article 9 of the treaty on the functioning of the european union. in this context, we welcome the fact that the european pillar of social rights has finally been formally endorsed by the european heads of state or government. if all the rights set out in this document - the right to equality, the right to education and health, the right to decent work and income, the right to social protection - were realised in practice, the european union would have made immense progress towards shared prosperity. but at this stage, this catalogue of rights is only a declaration of intent, and there is still no clear majority within the european political space to turn it into binding legislation, a necessary condition for these objectives to be achieved. we saw this with the eu 2020 strategy, which gave us the objective of reducing the number of poor people in europe by 20 million and, three years from now, i.e. 2020, we can only hope that this figure, after having increased by 6 million, will return to its 2010 level. the situation is clearly catastrophic within the euro area. then the right-thinking people will tell me that the best way to guarantee social rights is employment. the commission recently welcomed the return of the unemployment rate to its pre-crisis level, but at what cost? the question is: what is the solution to the problem of forced parttime work, or even zero-hour contracts, or of wages below the poverty line, or of mass exclusion? even the european central bank admits that the only real measure of the phenomenon is the underemployment rate, which is twice as high as unemployment. if work would lift people out of poverty, we would not have 21 million working poor in europe. the crux of the problem is that in their choice of economic policy, the majorities in power in the member states and at european level are going completely against the requirements of the social rights pillar. what are we talking about? on the one hand, completely counterproductive budgetary rules and, on the other, fiscal and social competition between member states, all of which is accompanied by a weakening of collective bargaining in the member states and crowned by free trade agreements favourable to multinationals. all this can only result in two things: on the one hand, a reduction in public expenditure and investment, which are absolutely essential to guaranteeing social rights, and on the other hand, a reduction in wages, working conditions and social protection. without questioning these political choices, we shall have no chance, ladies and gentlemen, of achieving and making the european pillar of social rights a reality, and that is what we expect.
mr president, twenty years, it will have taken us twenty years to have a new social summit. the last was luxembourg. that is, if gothenburg was expected. some people wonder: is this platform anything other than a communication operation? well, i think that question should not be asked, because we have no choice, we have no right. this social pillar must be a window of opportunity to revive the idea of a social europe, but this will only be possible if the member states do what they have to do. they need to take responsibility. the european construction cannot proceed without a genuine social footprint. that is why we must put economic and social issues on the same footing. at the beginning of this parliamentary term, jean-claude juncker advocated the triple a social package. well, let's go, it's about time! we need a clear and precise road map, with legislative proposals, with the means to do so. a first step can certainly be the introduction and reform of the written declaration directive. it is also necessary to continue to organise the mobility of employees in the most balanced way possible, and in particular to work on the coordination of social security systems. but today, too many women, too many young people, too many disabled workers, too many migrants, too many people are suffering from precariousness. the cooperation of the states is essential if the union is to put in place the appropriate policy coordination mechanisms and financial instruments. it must be an added value. social europe is a necessity, it is indispensable for the future of the european union.
yes, we must all work together in europe to make progress, there can be no doubt about that. and yes, the eu must do something about it. we need a socially just europe and a social pillar could be a big step in the right direction. the member states must be supported and encouraged by europe to make social progress. i fully support such european social initiatives. but - and there is an important but - the pillar must not be an excuse for an ever-widening european competence contrary to what the member states agreed in the treaties. subsidiarity and proportionality are very important principles. these ensure that social policy is adapted to the needs of citizens. a one-size-fits-all approach to social policy simply leads to a one-size-fits-none result. this should be a matter of real concern to us and i regret that it is not, or is not sufficiently, reflected in the present concrete proposals on the social pillar.
in writing. the social pillar and its 20 principles are finally putting social policy back on the political agenda. i welcome that, of course. however, there is a life-size "but" that threatens to dampen the festive mood. the social pillar may be full of good intentions, but it remains a non-binding 'compass' that member states 'can' but must not use to improve their social systems. as long as the social pillar remains a symbolic gesture of goodwill, it risks being pushed aside by the member states when it really matters and workers want to assert their rights. i believe that the social pillar must lead to concrete measures and enforceable legislation to protect, among other things, all workers, regardless of their status. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the draft directive. the commission is also proposing to introduce a new system of social security contributions for the elderly, which will be based on the principle of equal treatment for men and women. i therefore call on the european commission to take legislative initiatives to address the expectations of the social pillar and on the council to honour its promises and support concrete measures, funding and legislative initiatives.
on behalf of the s&d group. – mr president, sustainability is not just a buzzword. it is a sine qua non for the future of our planet and for the future of our people. therefore, reaching the sustainable development goals within the eu is vital. to do that, today we now have the eurostat monitoring report, which should show us the way in the progress that we are making within the european union. let me be frank. there is something fundamentally wrong with this report. it says that we are making progress and we are moving in the right direction. it gives us nice arrows and these arrows are painted in hopeful green, but when we peel off the green glitz, we are left with an unsatisfactory report lacking the essential data, focusing on the past and missing the point of the universal character of the sdgs by not taking into account their interconnection nor their impact on eu policies. you could say that, in times of fake news, it is good to have data. of course that is true, but when you look at the data and you see that, for instance, the 1% is being qualified as progress of the highest category, then we are fooling ourselves. some say that 15% is a good way forward in the 12 years to come, but if we know that we need 40% to get, for instance, the paris climate goals in place, then we know that we are fooling ourselves. heading in the right direction is not the same as being on the right track fast enough to go in that same direction. as you mentioned, madam commissioner, some of these sdgs will not be met. they are in your portfolio – no poverty, reducing inequality, decent work. that is really worrying, because it outlines that we do not sufficiently take into account european policy and governance. let me conclude. we need to revise our measuring tools, using better indicators and data and providing a clear plan for the future. you need to do that not just with parliament, but also with ngos.
i would like to thank the rapporteur for this strong and clear report. there must be absolute zero tolerance when it comes to the sexual abuse and exploitation of children. unfortunately, we still have too many problems today in the detection and prosecution of such criminal acts. this is where europe can and must prove its added value. good crossborder cooperation between member states is of the utmost importance. there are still many challenges, especially in the area of online crime. we are faced with ever-new forms of online abuse that require continuous investment in new digital detection techniques, an adapted legislative framework and further prevention and awareness-raising. i would like to conclude by saying that children with disabilities are particularly vulnerable. they are much more likely to be subjected to physical and sexual violence. there were also other forms of abuse than in children without disability, such as during the daily hygiene routine or during their treatment. this really requires special attention and vigilance.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the father i have been and remain and the grandfather i have become cannot hide his unease and concern that we have not done everything - or at least everything we could - to prevent sexual predators from taking action. of course - and i welcome this - the european union adopted a directive in 2011, which mrs corazza bildt, in her excellent report, objectively and critically appraises, distinguishing between what has been done, what can be improved and what remains to be done. i would like to emphasize a few measures which i consider both necessary and urgent. we must ensure that member states exchange information on paedophiles, so that when paedophiles move to another member state they cannot pursue an activity which puts them in contact with children. we must, in cooperation with the internet service providers, give ourselves the technological means to detect and block child pornography and payments for such services. we need to ensure that the people behind each internet connection can be identified and to this end, encourage internet service providers and network operators to take the necessary measures to limit the number of users per ip address. finally, we must ensure that member states which have not yet done so criminalise, as is already the case in belgium, cyber-predation, i.e. an adult's disguising himself and communicating online with a minor or a person he believes to be a minor, with the aim of subsequently committing a criminal offence against him. in conclusion, i know that all this will not prevent any passage to the act of sexual predators, but at least we will have done everything we can to prevent it.
member of the commission. – madam president, i would like to thank members for their comments, and i would like to underline that the low-emission strategy will bring significant benefits in addressing the environmental footprint of transport in a comprehensive and graduated manner. the transport sector has to contribute its share of reducing greenhouse gas emissions. there is no doubt about that. the process to the low-emission strategy will also contribute to making our industry stronger and more competitive, and it will help clean up the environment for citizens and improve their quality of life through better air quality in cities and reducing congestion. i agree with the point raised about urgency. yes, we need to act, and we need to act now. we have already initiated a series of initiatives to support the move to lower emissions. member states and local authorities, educational and research institutes, as well as producers and investors can all provide much-needed incentives. one of the comments referred to the commission’s approach to alternative fuels as only a communication and not as a legislative proposal. as specified in directive 2014/94 on the deployment of alternative fuels, by the end of 2018 the commission will present an action plan on alternative fuels infrastructures. we presented this action plan in november of this year. regarding a legislative proposal amending the directive, this is foreseen for before the end of 2020 if the commission considers the need for it. a number of speakers also mentioned the importance of addressing aviation and the maritime transport sector. in order to effectively address emissions from international sectors, such as aviation and maritime, global solutions are needed. the eu is fully committed and strongly contributing to the ongoing processes for reaching effective and ambitious measures within the international civil aviation organisation (icao) and the international maritime organisation (imo). for the commission, this is a priority. there was also a mention ofthe need for horizontal initiatives or horizontal enablers, and i can refer to a number of initiatives that are in place which should strengthen the impact of the steps outlined above. for instance, advancing the electromobility update by encouraging consumers to charge batteries at off-peak times through new rules for electricity market design. in addition, there are preparations for an eu strategy on research, innovation and competitiveness that should bring together energy technologies, transport and industry and facilitate the development of energy storage solutions, such as the next generation batteries, and focus more on research into advanced bio and synthetic fuels. rules for a digital single market should set standards for using digital technologies in transport and ensure free flow of data. the shift, as already mentioned by mr eickhout, to low-emission mobility will also have an impact on jobs in the transport sector, which currently account for 15 million or 7% of eu jobs, by requiring new skills. this challenge has been taken up in the new skills agenda for europe. this strategy therefore aims to develop a horizontal enablers’ programme as well, in order to create the right framework conditions for innovation and investment in the low-emissions sector. i would like once again to thank the european parliament for this constructive cooperation on this file. i would also once again like to thank mr eickhout and the shadow rapporteurs. we all look forward to a positive vote.
in writing. all economic sectors need to step up their efforts to meet the paris climate agreement. industry, energy, buildings, agriculture, they all need to reduce their greenhouse gas emissions significantly. the transport sector needs to take action a fortiori, as it is one of the few sectors that has increased its co2 emissions in recent years. seven eu countries, including belgium and the netherlands, have already taken a shot at the bow. they want co2 emissions from cars to be reduced by at least 40% over the next decade. the only way to do that is to accelerate the switch to electric cars. shipping and aviation also need to make much greater efforts, as co2 emissions are increasing exponentially. it is good that we as the eu continue to exert international pressure to further tackle greenhouse gas emissions from ships and aircraft.
over the years, vietnam has emerged as a full partner of the european union, and this is also confirmed in a free trade agreement. now, the concept of "free" is a word that vietnam is foreign to. the communist rulers do not tolerate freedom of the press, of assembly, of opinion. human rights activists, journalists and bloggers face intimidation, violence and imprisonment on a daily basis. that is why today we express our support for the growing number of brave citizens who have dared to speak out against the politburo. among them are blogger nguyen van hoa and human rights activist nguyen ngoc nhu quynh, both sentenced to long prison terms for spreading their message. dear commissioner, the imprisonment of bloggers and activists is something that we as the european union cannot tolerate. i therefore call on you to point out the shortcomings of the vietnamese government and to put pressure on it to fulfil all its human rights obligations. free trade and a free society go hand in hand.
author. madam president, this is the fifth resolution on cambodia, the second in two months, but it was necessary. why would you do that? since the last resolution in october, we have been to cambodia, several colleagues from the asean delegation, including president werner langen and myself, and we have made the cambodian authorities aware of the importance of not dissolving the main opposition party, because this is an act which obviously kills the democratization of this country, and they have not heard us. since then, the dissolution of the main opposition party, the cnpr, has been announced. seven of the 55 members of the national assembly live in the country. the majority of them, the other 48, have had to leave the country because they are under threat. this is clearly intolerable and this resolution must go further. given the limited impact of many previous resolutions, i particularly welcome paragraph 12, which i consider important, as it recommends the freezing of the assets of regime officials responsible for this party dissolution abroad and in europe, and imposes visa restrictions. i also welcome the decision of the commission and the high representative to cut subsidies for democratisation, because there is no democratisation and so these european subsidies now look like a waste of money.
yes, there is evidence that phosphates in food can be harmful to health. the health effects of phosphates are currently being evaluated by the european food safety authority and results are expected by the end of 2018. it is therefore logical to await this efsa report before deciding on additional exemptions for the use of phosphates in food. the consumer is also at risk of being left out in the cold. phosphates can increase the weight of meat due to their water-binding properties and no information is given on a product label. this is not about banning kebabs at all. innovation is making alternatives to phosphates possible. in flanders, flanders food plays a strong role in this area. this is important not only for health reasons but also for the efficient use of the scarce raw material phosphate. for all these reasons, the n-va delegation supported the objection.
